## bobzien-1998-inadvertent
Sources utilisées : /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md, /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md (consulted only to locate the misplaced quote of node eph_hemin_3), /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien_1998.summary.md (derived summary, NOT citable; source of two errors propagated into the nodes), /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/Extractions_articles/Bobzien_1998_Inadvertent.md (reading notes, no page map, AUDIT-SKIPPED; NOT citable) · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Source: Phronesis 43/2 (1998) 133-175, native extraction (43 pp., 2024 lines), page_map header-detected with citation_confidence 0.95, audit status 'passed' (3/3 tests, 43/43 citation pages agree); agentic_search --strict-cite accepts hits from this file (map_conf 0.95, pdf_overlap 1.00) and fails the strict gate only because of the unmapped Extractions_articles/Bobzien_1998_Inadvertent.md notes file (AUDIT-SKIPPED, p.?), which must not be cited. Line-to-page table confirmed from page_map start_lines (p.133=12, 134=57, 135=106, 136=153, 137=202, 139=297, 140=345, 141=393, 142=439, 143=488, 144=533, 148=721, 156=1103, 164=1489, 166=1583, 167=1631, 170=1775, 172=1868, 175=2004). Greek in the extraction is in a legacy font encoding (e.g. '¤fƒ ²mÝn' = eph' hemin, '¤jousÛa' = exousia, 'aétejoæsion' = autexousion, '¤leuyerÛa' = eleutheria); evidence quotes reproduce it exactly as in the file and no Unicode Greek was generated; 'fi' ligatures are dropped by the extraction (' rst', 'signi cance'), so quotes were chosen to avoid those words, and line-break hyphens were silently joined. Systemic issues: (1) Two nodes (eph_hemin_3, moral_responsibility_2) carry page_range values ('375-412', '118-143') that are LINE ranges from Bobzien_1998.summary.md misread as printed pages; the summary's bracketed references are [file:line], not pages. (2) The autexousion node inherits a factual error from the summary: Bobzien's n. 55 concerns exousia, not autexousion, in Justin and Tatian — this matters directly for H2 and should be corrected wherever the summary's sentence has been reused. (3) The eph_hemin node's quote_verbatim is from Bobzien 2001, p. 412, mixed into a 1998 node. (4) Several nodes flatten Bobzien's hedges ('our evidence suggests', 'I can only offer conjecture', 'may have been', 'at least at times', 'rough and schematic'); given her stated method (deny awareness absent evidence, p. 137), those hedges are load-bearing. Vocabulary: 'compatibilist', 'libertarian', 'indeterminist', 'MR1/MR2', 'one-sided causative / two-sided potestative' are all Bobzien's own terms on the cited pages and may be used in reporting her.

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_alexander_of_aphrodisias_as_fi_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander of Aphrodisias as first evidence for free-will problem — The earliest unambiguous evidence for awareness of any 'free-will problem' occurs in Alexander of Aphrodisias; his formulation resembles modern problems in involving universal causal determinism with 'same causes, same effects' and indeterminist freedom without a concept of will
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at file lines 187-189, printed p. 136 (agentic_search: p.136, map_conf 0.95, pdf_overlap 1.00). The three descriptive elements (earliest unambiguous evidence in Alexander; resemblance to modern problems via universal causal determinism with a 'same causes, same effects' principle; indeterminist freedom without a concept of the will) are all in the next sentence, same page. The verified_reference's 'sources Alexander, De Fato and the Mantissa' matches the abstract (p. 133). quote_page 'pp. 136-137' is slightly loose: the passage is entirely on p. 136.
  - « The earliest unambiguous evidence for the awareness of any kind of Òfree-will problemÓ occurs in Alexander of Aphrodisias. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:187-189] (Bobzien 1998, p. 136)
  - « It resembles the problems modern philosophers discuss in that it is concerned with a theory of universal causal determinism which contains a principle of the type Òsame causes, same effects,Ó and in that it involves a concept of indeterminist freedom without invoking a concept of the will. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:189-193] (Bobzien 1998, p. 136)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_aristotle_s_conception_of_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's conception of ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Aristotle's concept of ἐφ' ἡμῖν does not entail indeterminism; he meant only that things depending on us are those which generically it is possible for us to do and not to do, given we are not externally prevented—not that the particular action is causally undetermined
- Constat : Quote located verbatim (including Bobzien's own 'what depends us') at file lines 560-564, printed p. 144 (page_map: lines 533-577 = p. 144). The gloss 'not that the particular action is causally undetermined' is a fair rendering of Bobzien's closing sentence of section IV ('certainly there is no mention of freedom to act or choose otherwise, circumstances and agent being the same'). Two caveats on metadata only: (a) the Greek quotation of EN 1113b7-8 given in verified_reference is NOT in Bobzien's text — she cites 'EN 1113b7-8, cf. EE 1226a27-8' by line number only; (b) source_file points to the Extractions_articles reading notes, not the extraction. Section IV runs pp. 143-144, so page_range is acceptable.
  - « But AristotleÕs concept of what depends us does not entail indeterminism. We have no reason to assume that he has anything more in mind than that the things that depends on us are those which on a generic level it is possible for us to do and not to do, given that we are not externally prevented from doing them. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:560-564] (Bobzien 1998, p. 144)
  - « nor is he concerned with fate or causal determinism; and certainly there is no mention of freedom to act or choose otherwise, circumstances and agent being the same. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:573-575] (Bobzien 1998, p. 144)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_autexousion_as_philosophical_t_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : autexousion as philosophical term — Autexousion is not found in Justin and Tatian in the technical context of exousia-accounts of 'what depends on us'; from the late 2nd century onwards autexousion became more common as a philosophical term used instead of eph' hemin, and by Nemesius' time seems to have superseded it
- Constat : The first clause misreads footnote 55 (file lines 1530-1532, p. 164). The sentence there is about the term EXOUSIA (encoded '¤jousÛa' in the extraction; it is the subject of section XI, 'The term ¤jousÛa in the accounts of what depends on us'): Bobzien says exousia is not found in this kind of account in Aspasius, the Anonymous, Alcinous, [Plutarch] On Fate, '(nor in Justin and Tatian)', Mantissa ch. 22 or Alexander's logical commentaries. She says NOTHING about autexousion in Justin or Tatian. The error originates in Bobzien_1998.summary.md ('autexousion is not found in Justin and Tatian' [1531]) and was propagated. For a thesis whose H2 is that Justin introduces autexousios, attributing to Bobzien the claim that the word is absent from Justin would be a serious misattribution. The second clause (late-2nd-century spread of autexousion, superseding eph' hemin by Nemesius' time) is verbatim at lines 1634-1637, p. 167; the node's quote_verbatim reproduces the file's legacy-font encoding exactly.
  - « XI. The term ¤jousÛa in the accounts of what depends on us » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1501] (Bobzien 1998, p. 164)
  - « in this context in Aspasius, the Anonymous on the Nicomachean Ethics, Alcinous, [Plutarch] On Fate, (nor in Justin and Tatian) nor in Mantissa ch. 22 and AlexanderÕs commentaries on the Topics and Prior Analytics. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1530-1532] (Bobzien 1998, p. 164 n. 55; the sentence's subject, at line 1530, is the encoded term ¤jousÛa = exousia)
  - « for from the late 2nd century onwards aétejoæsion became more and more common as a philosophical term used instead of ¤fƒ ²mÝn, and at NemesiusÕ time seems to have superseded it. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1634-1637] (Bobzien 1998, p. 167)
- Description proposée : Bobzien's n. 55 (p. 164) states that exousia — not autexousion — does not occur in the relevant kind of account of 'what depends on us' in Aspasius, the Anonymous, Alcinous, [Plutarch] On Fate, 'nor in Justin and Tatian'; she makes no claim about autexousion in Justin or Tatian. On autexousion she notes a link with exousia already in Epictetus, 'in a deterministic setting', and suggests that from the late 2nd century onwards it became more common as a philosophical term used instead of eph' hemin, superseding it by Nemesius' time; Alexander uses it very rarely (Fat. 182.22-4), and it 'may then have been understood by some as implying indeterminist freedom' (pp. 166-167).
- Pages : 164 n. 55; 166-167

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_chrysippus_and_early_stoics_on_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chrysippus and early Stoics on ἐφ' ἡμῖν — The early Stoics, particularly Chrysippus, had only a one-sided, causative conception of ἐφ' ἡμῖν (meaning 'attributable to us'), not an indeterminist two-sided conception; they were causal determinists who did not face a free-will problem
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at file lines 464-465 (line-break hyphen in 'inde-terminist' joined), printed p. 142 (agentic_search: p.142, conf 0.95). 'One-sided, causative' and the translation 'attributable to us' are Bobzien's (pp. 141, 149: 'the Stoics had a one-sided concept of what depends on us'). 'Causal determinists who did not face a free-will problem' matches pp. 143 and 172 ('The Stoics did not require a concept of free-will ... they had no reasons to concern themselves with any free-will problem'), with Bobzien's own reservation that they faced instead the problem of the compatibility of autonomy and determinism (p. 136). One hedge is worth noting: n. 8 says we do not know with certainty which Greek expressions Chrysippus used, so 'Chrysippus' conception of eph' hemin' is slightly firmer than her wording. page_range 137-142 covers sections II-III; the decisive statements are on pp. 142-143.
  - « The early Stoics, in particular Chrysippus, clearly did not have an indeterminist two-sided conception of what depends on us. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:464-465] (Bobzien 1998, p. 142)
  - « What makes an agent morally responsible is that the agent, and not something else causes the action. There is in this model no space for free will (i.e. for a decision making faculty that is causally independent of the mindÕs individual nature). » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:496-499] (Bobzien 1998, p. 143)
  - « We do not know with certainty which Greek expressions Chrysippus used, but it is likely that ¤pÛ with dativus personae was among them. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:483-485] (Bobzien 1998, p. 142 n. 8)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_eph_hemin_what_depends_on_us_3` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : eph' hemin (what depends on us) — Two conceptions of eph' hemin must be distinguished: the one-sided causative conception (Stoic, attributing causal responsibility) and the two-sided potestative conception (developed in the Peripatetic tradition, implying power for alternative courses of action)
- Constat : The description (one-sided causative vs two-sided potestative) is faithful to the article, where the distinction is made at pp. 139-141. But the node's page_range '375-412' is not a page range of the article (which runs pp. 133-175): it is the summary file's LINE range '[Bobzien - 1998:375-412]' mistaken for pages. The quote_verbatim ('In conclusion, the problem of the compatibility ...') is NOT in the 1998 article at all; it is located verbatim in Bobzien 2001, Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy, file line 19155, printed p. 412 (page_map conf 0.98) — the article's parallel passage (pp. 173-174) is worded differently ('appears to have been formulated only in the 2nd century A.D. ... based on AristotleÕs ethics'). Minor wording point: Bobzien says the two-sided potestative sense is 'well-attested' in Greek and that its philosophical form is 'based on Aristotle's ethics', worked up by an 'Aristotle scholar' who could be 'a Peripatetic or a Platonist' (p. 148) — 'developed in the Peripatetic tradition' compresses this. She also insists the two-sided potestative eph' hemin 'itself entails neither determinism nor indeterminism' (p. 140).
  - « The phrase apparently could denote both what I have named a Òone-sided, causativeÓ concept of what depends on someone, and what I call a Òtwo-sided, potestativeÓ concept of what depends on someone. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:333-336] (Bobzien 1998, p. 139)
  - « Note that the two-sided, potestative ¤fƒ ²mÝn itself entails neither determinism nor indeterminism. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:357-358] (Bobzien 1998, p. 140)
  - « In conclusion, the problem of the compatibility of causal determinism and freedom to do otherwise appears to have been formulated only in the second century AD. » [Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:19155-19157] (Bobzien 2001, p. 412)
- Description proposée : Bobzien (1998, pp. 139-141) distinguishes two senses of the Greek 'depending on someone': a 'one-sided, causative' concept ('attributable to us', naming who bears causal responsibility — the Stoic use) and a 'two-sided, potestative' concept ('up to us': if x depends on us, so does not-x), well attested in Greek and, philosophically, 'based on Aristotle's ethics'. The two-sided potestative eph' hemin 'itself entails neither determinism nor indeterminism'; only on an indeterminist reading does it imply freedom to do otherwise. The 'In conclusion…' quote belongs to Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy (2001), p. 412, not to the article.
- Pages : 139-141 (results: 173-174); quote_verbatim: Bobzien 2001, p. 412

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_exousia_and_conceptual_shift_t_5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : exousia and conceptual shift toward free will — Alexander's replacement of dynasthai by exousia in accounts of 'what depends on us' introduced the idea of the agent as a causally undetermined decision-maker, marking a further step toward the concept of free decision and free will, distinct from earlier Stoic conceptions
- Constat : The substance is in section XI, pp. 164-166 (decisive sentences at file lines 1600-1611, printed p. 166, agentic_search conf 0.95). But the node turns Bobzien's explicitly conjectural and qualified claim into a flat assertion: she writes 'On this question, I can only offer conjecture' (p. 164), the change 'may have been of great significance', and the indeterminist reading of the terms holds only '(at least at times)' — n. 58 adds that the exousia phrase 'can also be understood as not being hindered either way ... and also as having a general two-sided capacity to act', in which case 'freedom of decision would need to be involved' in neither. The contrast she draws is with Aristotle and Epictetus (where control meant being unhindered), not with 'earlier Stoic conceptions' generally. The summary file's 'Extractions ~ll. 1602-1611' in verified_reference is a line range, not pages.
  - « How can we explain the appearance of ¤jousÛa in the accounts? On this question, I can only offer conjecture. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1515-1516] (Bobzien 1998, p. 164)
  - « In AlexanderÕs account, the terms are (at least at times)58 understood differently: what makes us have control over things is the fact that we are causally undetermined in our decision and thus can freely decide between doing/choosing or not doing/choosing them. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1600-1603] (Bobzien 1998, p. 166)
  - « it provided a way to express that the agent is a causally undetermined decision maker. The introduction of the term into this context is then also one further step towards the concept of a free will, since such a concept requires an independent faculty of decision making. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1608-1611] (Bobzien 1998, p. 166)
- Description proposée : Bobzien conjectures ('I can only offer conjecture', p. 164) that Alexander's replacement of dynasthai by exousia — used by him as virtually synonymous with being kyrios over something — 'may have been of great significance', since it provided a way to express that the agent is 'a causally undetermined decision maker', and is 'one further step towards the concept of a free will'. This holds only 'at least at times' (n. 58: the exousia phrase can also mean being unhindered, or a general two-sided capacity). The contrast is with Aristotle and Epictetus, for whom control meant that nothing hindered us (pp. 164-166).
- Pages : 164-166

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_greek_terminology_for_freedom_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Greek terminology for freedom — The proper Greek term for freedom, ἐλευθερία (eleutheria), played no role in discussions of determinism and moral responsibility up to the 2nd century A.D.; the relevant conceptual development concerns the phrase ἐφ' ἡμῖν (eph' hēmin, 'up to us')
- Constat : Passage located at file lines 107-114, printed p. 135 (agentic_search: p.135, conf 0.95). The quote_verbatim is accurate in wording but (a) cuts the evidential hedge that governs the sentence — Bobzien writes 'our evidence suggests that' eleutheria played no role, in line with her declared strategy of denying awareness 'as long as there are neither textual evidence nor philosophical reasons' (p. 137); (b) the '*not*' emphasis cannot be verified in the extraction; (c) it omits the complementary point that eleutheria later attaches to autonomous agency and to freedoms of types 5-7. The description's gloss of eph' hemin as 'up to us' is not Bobzien's neutral rendering: she uses 'depending on us' and reserves 'up to us' for the two-sided potestative reading (p. 140), 'attributable to us' for the one-sided causative one (p. 141).
  - « Note that the only proper Greek term for freedom is ¤leuyerÛa, and that our evidence suggests that ¤leuyerÛa played no role in the discussion of determinism and moral responsibility up to the 2nd century A.D. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:108-110] (Bobzien 1998, p. 135)
  - « It is the notion of autonomous agency (see below) and of not being determined by something else (freedoms type 5-7) which in philosophical discussions in later antiquity becomes connected with ¤leuyerÛa. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:114-117] (Bobzien 1998, p. 135)
  - « Thus, in the two-sided, potestative understanding, Òup to usÓ would be a good translation of the Greek expression » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:349-350] (Bobzien 1998, p. 140)
- Description proposée : Bobzien notes that the only proper Greek term for freedom is eleutheria and that 'our evidence suggests' it played no role in the discussion of determinism and moral responsibility up to the 2nd century A.D.; in particular it is not involved in the development of the concept of freedom to do otherwise. The pertinent development is that of the phrase eph' hemin ('depending on us'), which has 'an altogether different history'; eleutheria later becomes connected with autonomous agency and with freedoms of types 5-7 (p. 135). 'Up to us' is, for her, the translation fitting only the two-sided potestative reading (p. 140).
- Pages : 135 (cf. 140-141)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_historical_marginality_of_libe_7` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : historical marginality of libertarian freedom — Concepts of freedom to do otherwise at the turn of the 2nd century were not widespread; the one-sided causative eph' hemin remained dominant even in non-Stoic Platonist and Peripatetic writings. Alexander's position was historically marginal
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at file lines 1655-1658, printed p. 167 (page_map lines 1631-1675 = p. 167); the 'not part of the philosophical repertoire for long' clause is at lines 2011-2012, p. 175, so page_range '167, 174-175' is right. The core claim (Alexander 'almost an isolated case', freedom to do otherwise 'a rather marginal phenomenon') is faithful. But 'the one-sided causative eph' hemin remained dominant' overstates: Bobzien says it 'was by no means peculiar to the Stoic system' and 'seems to have been regarded as a serious alternative or as a complement to the two-sided, potestative conception' in 2nd-3rd century Middle-Platonist and Peripatetic writings — she does not rank it as dominant. The topic label's 'libertarian' is admissible only because Bobzien herself uses 'indeterminist libertarians' (p. 134) and 'the libertarian condition' (p. 143).
  - « There are several points that suggest that at his time Alexander is almost an isolated case, and that concepts of freedom to do otherwise are a rather marginal phenomenon without a clear philosophical context. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1655-1658] (Bobzien 1998, p. 167)
  - « On the contrary, it seems to have been regarded as a serious alternative or as a complement to the two-sided, potestative conception in 2nd and 3rd century Middle-Platonist and Peripatetic writings. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1663-1665] (Bobzien 1998, p. 167)
  - « and it was not part of the philosophical repertoire for long. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:2011-2012] (Bobzien 1998, p. 175)
- Description proposée : Bobzien warns against assuming that at the turn of the 2nd century a general awareness of the problem of causal determinism and freedom to do otherwise had arisen: Alexander 'is almost an isolated case', and concepts of freedom to do otherwise are 'a rather marginal phenomenon without a clear philosophical context'. The one-sided causative eph' hemin was 'by no means peculiar to the Stoic system' and seems to have been regarded as 'a serious alternative or as a complement' to the two-sided potestative conception in 2nd-3rd century Middle-Platonist and Peripatetic writings (p. 167); the problem 'was not part of the philosophical repertoire for long' (p. 175).
- Pages : 167-168, 174-175

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_middle_platonists_on_contingen_7` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Middle-Platonists on contingency and ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Middle-Platonists contributed to the development by linking contingency (τὸ ἐνδεχόμενον) with ἐφ' ἡμῖν and introducing notions of power (ἐξουσία, δύναμις, αὐτεξούσιον), but did not fully articulate a free-will problem
- Constat : Two of three elements contradict Bobzien. (1) 'introducing notions of power (exousia, dynamis, autexousion)': she argues the reverse — exousia is NOT found in Alcinous or [Plutarch] On Fate (n. 55, p. 164) and 'Alexander seems to be the first – of whom we know – to use the term' in such accounts (p. 164, file lines 1508-1510); the Middle-Platonist account uses only 'being capable of' (dynasthai), which Alexander later replaces; autexousion is tied to Epictetus and to late-2nd-century usage (pp. 166-167), not to the Middle-Platonist fate doctrine. Her one Middle-Platonist exousia witness, Maximus of Tyre, concerns how vice entered the world, not accounts of what depends on us (p. 165). (2) 'Middle-Platonists contributed ... by linking contingency with eph' hemin': faithful to sections VI-VII, but Bobzien attributes the passage to an 'Aristotle scholar' who 'could be equally well a Peripatetic or a Platonist' (p. 148). (3) 'did not fully articulate a free-will problem': she leaves open whether they read eph' hemin as causally undetermined (p. 156), and says Platonists later faced a free-will problem within their system via providence (pp. 172-173). The Alcinous Greek in verified_reference is quoted by Bobzien (p. 154, legacy encoding) but plays no role in the node's claims.
  - « The original author of this bit of ÒMiddle-PlatonistÓ theory could be equally well a Peripatetic or a Platonist – if indeed such a distinction made sense at the time. We may say the author was an Aristotle scholar. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:730-733] (Bobzien 1998, p. 148)
  - « in this context in Aspasius, the Anonymous on the Nicomachean Ethics, Alcinous, [Plutarch] On Fate, (nor in Justin and Tatian) nor in Mantissa ch. 22 and AlexanderÕs commentaries on the Topics and Prior Analytics. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1530-1532] (Bobzien 1998, p. 164 n. 55, on the term ¤jousÛa = exousia)
  - « I leave it open whether the Middle-Platonists understood their two-sided concept of ¤fƒ ²mÝn as implying undeterminedness or non-predetermination by causal factors. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:1113-1115] (Bobzien 1998, p. 156)
- Description proposée : On Bobzien's reconstruction, an 'Aristotle scholar' behind the Middle-Platonist common source — 'equally well a Peripatetic or a Platonist' (p. 148) — subordinated what depends on us to the contingent ('in equal parts'), combining EN III.3, De Int. 13 and De Int. 9 (pp. 147-152); Alcinous and Ammonius may show a step towards undeterminedness (pp. 154-155), but she leaves open whether Middle-Platonists read it as causal indeterminism (p. 156). Exousia is absent from Alcinous and [Plutarch] On Fate: Alexander seems the first known to use it in such accounts (p. 164 n. 55); autexousion is linked to Epictetus and late-2nd-century usage (pp. 166-167).
- Pages : 146-156; 164 n. 55; 166-167

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_types_of_freedom_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : types of freedom — Seven distinct types of freedom must be carefully distinguished: freedom to do otherwise, freedom of decision, freedom of the will, un-predeterminist freedom, freedom from force/compulsion, freedom from external causal factors, and freedom from certain internal causal factors
- Constat : The seven labels are Bobzien's and are all at pp. 133-134 (file lines 35-105; agentic_search locates 'neither exhaustive nor' at line 103, p. 134). But the node flattens her architecture and her disclaimers: she presents (i) 'three kinds of indeterminist freedom' (2 a subtype of 1, 3 a subtype of 2), (ii) 'un-predeterminist' freedom, which she says is 'often confounded' with indeterminist freedom in reading ancient texts — the confusion her whole article turns on — and (iii) three freedoms 'compatible with both indeterminism and un-predeterminism'. She calls the distinctions 'rough and schematic', 'deliberately vague', and the list 'neither exhaustive nor exclusive' — the opposite of 'must be carefully distinguished'. page_range '133-175' (whole article) should be 133-134.
  - « These distinctions are kept rough and schematic. They are left deliberately vague in certain respects, because the ancient theories whose understanding they are intended to further are themselves stubbornly vague in those respects. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:36-39] (Bobzien 1998, p. 133)
  - « In the interpretation of ancient texts, indeterminist freedom is often confounded with un-predeterminist freedom. From both these types of freedom must be distinguished the following ones which are compatible with both indeterminism and Òun-predeterminismÓ: » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:82-85] (Bobzien 1998, p. 134)
  - « The list of types of freedoms 1) to 7) is evidently neither exhaustive nor exclusive. » [Bobzien - 1998 - The Inadvertent Conception and Late Birth of the F.md:103-105] (Bobzien 1998, p. 134)
- Description proposée : Bobzien's opening distinctions — 'kept rough and schematic' and 'deliberately vague' — separate three kinds of indeterminist freedom (freedom to do otherwise; freedom of decision, its subtype; freedom of the will, a subtype requiring a faculty of the will) from 'un-predeterminist' freedom (no prior determining causes, yet the same agent in the same circumstances would always choose the same), and from three freedoms compatible with both: from force and compulsion, from determination by external causal factors, and from (external and) certain internal causal factors. Indeterminist and un-predeterminist freedom are 'often confounded' in reading ancient texts; the list is 'neither exhaustive nor exclusive' (pp. 133-134).
- Pages : 133-134

Recurring systematic errors: (i) page_range fields frequently point to a different chapter than the quoted page; (ii) 'compatibilist framework' attributed where Bobzien denies awareness of the problem (Epictetus) or where the argument targets fate-determinism, not moral responsibility (Idle Argument, co-fated); (iii) the early-Stoic 'what depends on us' is reported as one-sided/causative although Bobzien reserves that for 'happens because of us' and assigns the 'through us' formula to PHILOPATOR. Greek in the .md is OCR-garbled throughout; no Greek token was reproduced in this report beyond what is verbatim in the file, and corrected descriptions use Bobzien's English renderings only. Bobzien uses 'compatibilism'/'compatibilist' herself (chs. 6, 8), so the term is admissible in reports of her views; she does not use 'libertarian' except when quoting Sharples (p. 378 n. 47).

Bobzien does call ch. 6 'Chrysippus' compatibilism' and reads the cylinder/cone as separating 'external antecedent causes' from 'the agent's own nature'. The node drops one point she stresses on the same page (responsibility grounded on 'the autonomy of the agent', not on freedom to do otherwise), but nothing it says contradicts her. page_range 235-330 is off by one at each end: ch. 6 = pp. 234-329; the analogy is §6.3.3, pp. 258-271.
  - « it will emerge that the conception of moral responsibility of Chrysippus and his contemporaries is not grounded on any concept of freedom to do otherwise, but on the autonomy of the agent. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:10983-10986] (Bobzien 1998, p. 235)
  - « The faculty of assent functions in such a way that the nature of the mind, and not the externally induced impres- sion, is the decisive factor for the effect. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:12548-12550] (Bobzien 1998, p. 269)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_chrysippus_compatibilism_fate__1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chrysippus' compatibilism: fate and moral responsibility — Chrysippus developed a sophisticated compatibilist position that distinguishes between 'simply fated' (haplos heimarmena) and 'co-fated' (sunheimarmena) events. Actions are co-fated: they require both external circumstances and our own assent (sunkatathesis). This preserves moral responsibility within causal determinism.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 9912-9922 (p. 211). But the description inverts and over-reads it. In Bobzien it is future occurrents (recovering) that are 'fated together with' an action (calling the doctor), the relation being that of a necessary condition; the things that happen because of us are 'fated-along-with' the administration of the universe. The word 'assent' does not occur anywhere in ch. 5 (rg over lines 8400-10900: 0 hits) — the co-fated analysis is about action and effort, not sunkatathesis. And Bobzien explicitly denies that the refutation is about moral responsibility: it 'is not a defence of free-will' (p. 181); moral responsibility is added only in ch. 6 (p. 235). page_range 'Chapter 5-6' too vague.
  - « First, the relation between the fated occurrent and the action together with which it is fated is that of a necessary condition; so the general formu- lation in (5): it cannot happen without us wanting it to happen and making an effort » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:9923-9926] (Bobzien 1998, p. 211)
  - « It is important to see that Chrysippus' refutation is in the first instance just that: a refutation of the argument, based on his own doctrine of fate. It is not a defence of free-will, or a piece on prophecy, etc. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:8520-8523] (Bobzien 1998, p. 181)
  - « adding to the 'preserved' concepts of contingency and intentional action finally that of moral responsibility » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:10976-10978] (Bobzien 1998, p. 235)
- Description proposée : In the Diogenianus/Eusebius report Chrysippus distinguishes occurrents 'fated simply or in isolation' from occurrents 'fated together with' some human activity; the things that happen because of us are 'fated-along-with' the administration of the universe. The co-fated occurrent stands to the action as to a necessary condition ('it cannot happen without us wanting it to happen and making an effort'), and those actions are themselves fated. Bobzien reads this as a refutation of the Idle Argument preserving intentional action within fate-determinism — 'not a defence of free-will'; moral responsibility is a separate problem (ch. 6).
- Pages : 211-212 (quote p. 211); §5.3 pp. 217-226; cf. p. 181

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_chrysippus_modal_logic_and_con_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chrysippus' modal logic and contingency — Chrysippus' modal system, based on his distinction between fate (what is determined) and necessity (ἀνάγκη), whereby not everything fated is necessary, allows for contingency within determinism. Something can be determined/fated without being necessary in the modal sense. This resolves apparent conflicts between Stoic determinism and their modal logic.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 5840-5846 (p. 122, §3.2). The node states that Chrysippus' modal system is 'based on his distinction between fate and necessity' and 'resolves' the conflict. Bobzien is more cautious: the modal system is built by modifying Diodorus' and Philo's notions (§3.1); 'We have no direct evidence that Chrysippus linked his modal theory to his doctrine of fate'; the fate/necessity non-identity is an inference she draws ('I believe we can infer'), and the terminological distinction between Necessity and 'that which is necessary' is treated separately in §3.4 as a reply to opponents who 'mixed up' the two. page_range 'Chapter 3' = pp. 97-143.
  - « We have no direct evidence that Chrysippus linked his modal theory to his doctrine of fate. But we know that he denied that everything that is fated is also necessary. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:5844-5846] (Bobzien 1998, p. 122)
  - « From this I believe we can infer that he thought that, at least according to his own modal notions, not everything fated comes out as necessary » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:5847-5849] (Bobzien 1998, p. 122)
- Description proposée : Chrysippus' modal notions (a minimal modification of Diodorus' possibility, with Philo's criterion added) preserve counterfactual possibilities and so secure 'an adequate modal basis' for purposeful action and the attribution of responsibility. Bobzien stresses that there is 'no direct evidence that Chrysippus linked his modal theory to his doctrine of fate'; from his denial that everything fated is necessary she infers that, on his own modal notions, not everything fated comes out as necessary. Opponents alleged an inconsistency; Chrysippus' reply distinguishes cosmological Necessity from the modal 'that which is necessary' (§3.4).
- Pages : 97-143 (ch. 3); quote p. 122 (§3.2, pp. 122-131); §3.4 pp. 136-143

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_divination_and_determinism_5` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Divination and determinism — Chrysippus used the existence of divination as proof of causal determinism, but distinguished between conditional predictions (if someone is born under a certain sign, they will have certain traits) and absolute predictions; divinatory theorems involve sign-relations and regularity of occurrents, not direct active causation
- Constat : No quote_verbatim. The sign-relation half is faithful (§4.2 'active causation versus regularity of occurrents', p. 166). But the node conflates Bobzien's two categories: its example 'if someone is born under a certain sign, they will have certain traits' is what she calls a divinatory THEOREM (universal, a sign-relation), whereas her 'conditional predictions' (§4.2.5 Appendix, pp. 175-179) are prophecies addressed to a particular individual whose antecedent is a future action of the addressee, and which the Stoics DID regard as causally relevant. 'Absolute predictions' is not her term; she says theorems 'do not predict the future categorically'. The Chrysippean proof from divination is ch. 2, §2.2 (pp. 87-96), not 59-96 as a whole.
  - « Conditional predictions crucially differ from divinatory theorems in that (i) they are signs,75 (ii) they are not universal, but address a particular individual (or a group of individuals), and (iii) their antecedent concerns a future action of the addressee of the prediction. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:8333-8336] (Bobzien 1998, p. 176)
  - « another kind of divinatory sign, which even the Stoics considered to be causally relevant to what happens in the future. These are divinatory predictions, mostly pro- phecies, which are at least covertly of conditional form. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:8322-8325] (Bobzien 1998, pp. 175-176)
  - « Taking all these points together, I hence assume that for the Stoics, divinatory theorems do not connect causes with effects, nor state any causal relations in a wider sense. They connect signs (i.e. actualizations of propositions) with future facts. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:7893-7895] (Bobzien 1998, p. 166)
- Description proposée : Chrysippus proved fate through the existence of divination (§2.2). Against the objection that divination and Stoic contingency are incompatible, Bobzien argues that divinatory theorems (universal, e.g. birth at the rise of Sirius / not dying at sea) 'do not connect causes with effects' but 'connect signs ... with future facts': they express regularity of occurrents, not active causation. She distinguishes these from 'conditional predictions' (prophecies addressed to an individual whose antecedent is the addressee's future action), which the Stoics did treat as causally relevant and fitted into the causal network of fate (§4.2.5).
- Pages : 87-96 (§2.2); 144-179 (ch. 4; sign-relations §4.2.2 pp. 159-170; conditional predictions §4.2.5 pp. 175-179)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_divination_and_determinism_the_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

Divinatory theorems take the form of negated conjunctions rather than simple conditionals, preserving contingency while allowing predictive knowledge.

The negated-conjunction point is §4.2.1 (pp. 156-159) and Bobzien states the motive exactly as the node does: avoid the necessity of what is predicted while preserving the validity of the theorems. page_range 'Chapter 4' is vague but not wrong; precise range below.

  - « They connect signs (i.e. actualizations of propositions) with future facts. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:7894-7895] (Bobzien 1998, p. 166)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_epictetus_on_freedom_5` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Epictetus on freedom — Only the wise person is truly free; freedom requires reason and absence of inappropriate attachments; Epictetus connects freedom with 'what depends on us' but within a compatibilist framework
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 15911-15929 (p. 342, §7.2.2). The first two clauses paraphrase Bobzien acceptably ('freedom is a moral quality which only wise people possess'; freedom = not desiring or deploring what does not depend on you). But 'within a compatibilist framework' contradicts her: she says Epictetus 'shows no signs of any critical awareness of the problem of compatibilism', 'avoided talking about fate' qua network of causes, and that his notion of what depends on us is 'not concerned with indeterminist freedom of decision or freedom to do otherwise' — i.e. neutral, not compatibilist. page_range 204-211 is wrong (ch. 5 material); ch. 7 = pp. 330-357.
  - « except that in his extant works Epictetus shows no signs of any critical awareness of the problem of compatibilism. Epictetus' concept of that which depends on us—like that of Chrysippus—is not concerned with indeterminist freedom of decision or freedom to do otherwise. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:15721-15724] (Bobzien 1998, p. 338)
  - « As in the early Stoa, so in Epictetus, freedom is a moral quality which only wise people possess, and it is frequently opposed to slavery » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:15911-15912] (Bobzien 1998, p. 342)
- Description proposée : For Epictetus, as for the early Stoa, freedom is 'a moral quality which only wise people possess', opposed to slavery and linked to persons and their states of mind rather than to actions. Unlike the early Stoics, Epictetus expressly connects freedom with that which depends on us: one is free if, knowing what depends on one, one never desires or deplores anything that does not. Bobzien adds that Epictetus avoided fate qua network of causes and shows 'no signs of any critical awareness of the problem of compatibilism'; his notion is not concerned with indeterminist freedom either.
- Pages : 330-357 (ch. 7); quote p. 342 (§7.2.2, pp. 341-343); cf. pp. 337-338

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_epictetus_on_freedom_and_what__3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Epictetus on freedom and what depends on us (τὸ ἐφ' ἡμῖν) — Epictetus developed a conception of freedom (ἐλευθερία) and 'what depends on us' focused on the correct use of impressions and assent, distinct from the early Stoics; this represents a shift toward internalization of freedom
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 15497-15502 (p. 333). The restriction of what depends on us to the 'use of impressions' (assents, impulses, beliefs) is faithful. But 'a shift toward internalization of freedom' is not Bobzien's characterization: internal/psychological freedom is for her already the early Stoic (indeed pre-Stoic philosophical) notion (pp. 339, 341). The shift she actually describes is one of function and perspective — from a backward-looking notion in physics/psychology preserving responsibility to a forward-looking practical notion within ethics — plus the restriction of range. page_range 331-357 is acceptable (ch. 7 starts p. 330).
  - « The perspective from which that which depends on us is discussed has thus become a per- spective towards the future, concerned with guidance of actions and behaviour; before, in Chrysippus and the old Stoa it primarily was a back- wards perspective or time independent, concerned with the attribution of responsibility » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:15491-15496] (Bobzien 1998, p. 333)
  - « Stoic freedom is thus internal or psychological freedom, i.e. it depends entirely on the state of the mind of the person » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:15865-15867] (Bobzien 1998, p. 341, on the early Stoics)
- Description proposée : Epictetus places in the class of things that depend on us 'primarily — or perhaps even exclusively — the "use of impressions"', i.e. our assents and the impulses and beliefs that follow from assenting; everything else (actions, results, indifferents, the impressions themselves) does not depend on us. Bobzien reads this as a change of function and perspective: in Chrysippus the notion belonged to physics/psychology and served, backward-looking, the attribution of responsibility; in Epictetus it belongs to ethics and serves, forward-looking, the guidance of life. Freedom as internal, psychological freedom is already early Stoic.
- Pages : 330-345 (§7.1 pp. 331-338; §7.2 pp. 338-344); quote p. 333

- Nœud actuel : Stoic determinism: nature and definition — Stoic determinism is best understood as a combination of teleological and causal determinism, not merely fate as necessitarianism. But 'in a law-governed universe' contradicts Bobzien, who repeatedly denies that Chrysippus or PHILOPATOR presupposed empirically detectable 'laws of nature' (pp. 225, 372); at most she allows 'in modern parlance' enormously complex laws governing whole world states with a single instantiation. page_range 'Chapters 1-2' (pp. 16-96) does not contain the quoted p. 12.

  - « As in the case of Chrysippus, there is nowhere a clue that PHILOPATOR had a plurality of empirically detectable laws of nature in mind. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:17322-17324] (Bobzien 1998, p. 372)

- Description proposée : Bobzien presents Chrysippus' determinism as combining teleological determinism (the world rationally ordered by the active principle) with causal determinism (every motion predetermined by antecedent causes within the causal network of fate), and not as naive fatalism, logical determinism or necessitarianism; it is meant to be compatible with 'reasonable concepts of contingency, action, and moral responsibility'. She denies that Chrysippus presupposed empirically detectable 'laws of nature'. The early Stoic problem is not the modern free-will problem: concepts of freedom to do otherwise arise only in later antiquity (§8.7).

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_the_idle_argument_argos_logos__4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : The Idle Argument (argos logos) and its refutation — The Idle Argument poses a serious challenge to Stoic compatibilism by suggesting that if events are fated, effort is pointless. This is not merely a sophism but a substantive philosophical position about the structure of causation.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 8520-8527 (p. 181). Two misreadings. (a) 'a serious challenge to Stoic compatibilism': for Bobzien the argument 'is levelled against fate-determinism' and concerns the futility of action; 'that which depends on us does not feature at all in any of the reports', and the refutation 'is not a defence of free-will'. (b) 'not merely a sophism but a substantive philosophical position': the Stoics and Middle Platonists 'considered the Idle Argument as a sophism', and Bobzien's own verdict (§5.1.4) is that it 'contains various sophistic elements or lends itself to be interpreted as a fallacy'; what is substantive is Chrysippus' refutation, which elaborates his theory's 'concepts of causal relevance and intentional action'. The simply/co-fated summary is fine. page_range 'Chapter 5' = pp. 180-233.
  - « Chrysippus' refutation was countered, in a standard way, by pointing out that it destroys that which depends on us; but note that that which depends on us does not feature at all in any of the reports of the Idle Argument. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:8531-8535] (Bobzien 1998, p. 181)
  - « The Stoics (and the Middle Platonists) considered the Idle Argument as a sophism. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:8712-8713] (Bobzien 1998, p. 185)
  - « one can certainly say that it contains various sophistic elements or lends itself to be interpreted as a fallacy. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:9338-9339] (Bobzien 1998, p. 198)
- Description proposée : The Idle Argument is 'levelled against fate-determinism': if everything is fated, making an effort is futile. The Stoics treated it as a sophism, and Bobzien finds that it 'contains various sophistic elements'. Chrysippus' refutation — distinguishing occurrents 'fated simply' from those 'fated together with' our actions, which are their necessary conditions — is 'in the first instance just that: a refutation', 'not a defence of free-will'; that which depends on us does not figure in any report of the argument. Yet it yields essential information on his determinism's concepts of causal relevance and intentional action.
- Pages : 180-233 (ch. 5; quote p. 181; sophism §5.1.4 pp. 193-198; refutation §5.3 pp. 217-233)

### `scholarly_argument_bobzien_to_eph_hemin_what_depends_on_u_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : to eph' hemin (what depends on us) — For early Stoics, this was one-sided and causative, meaning 'that which happens through us' (di' hemon), not a two-sided power for alternative action; the concept is about causal attribution, not indeterminist freedom
- Constat : No quote_verbatim; page_range 196-197 is wrong (that is §5.1.4). The node collapses three things Bobzien keeps apart in §6.3.5 (pp. 276-290): (1) 'happens because of us' (Greek para c. acc. / ek c. gen., trans. 'because of us') — this is the phrase she calls 'one-sided' and 'causative' for Chrysippus; (2) 'depends on us' / 'in our power' (eph' hemin / in nostra potestate), which she assumes 'was generally used to denote a two-sided, potestative concept', with a reading 'neutral with respect to determinism and indeterminism', and which Chrysippus treated as a necessary condition for moral responsibility; (3) 'that which happens through us (by fate)' — the dia-formula — which is PHILOPATOR's later Stoic account (§8.4, p. 378), not early Stoic. So 'one-sided and causative, meaning that which happens through us (di' hemon)' misassigns both the phrase and the property to eph' hemin and to the early Stoa; 'not indeterminist' is right.
  - « I assume that in our context the phrase was generally used to denote a two-sided, potestative concept of depend- ing on someone—a use that is well-attested » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:13115-13117] (Bobzien 1998, p. 281)
  - « this does not usually denote a two- sided, potestative, concept of dependency, but a one-sided causative one. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:13235-13236] (Bobzien 1998, p. 284, on 'happens because of us')
  - « There is a short version of it, 'that which happens through us', and a long version, 'that which happens through us by fate'. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:17586-17588] (Bobzien 1998, p. 378, PHILOPATOR's account)
- Description proposée : In §6.3.5 Bobzien separates two early Stoic concepts. 'What happens because of us' is 'one-sided' and 'causative': the 'we' names a necessary causal factor, and Chrysippus shows its compatibility with fate (it is a subclass of what is fated). 'What depends on us / is in our power' she assumes to be two-sided and potestative — a capacity for either walking or not walking, absent external force — readable in a way 'neutral with respect to determinism and indeterminism'; Chrysippus treats it as a necessary condition for moral responsibility, and the sources rule out an indeterminist reading. The 'through us (by fate)' formula is PHILOPATOR's later account.
- Pages : 276-290 (§6.3.5; esp. 281-286); later 'through us' account 378-386, 396

- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 834-844 (all on printed p. 13; n. 27 at line 856 reads exactly 'e.g. Gilbert 1963, Voelke 1973, Dihle 1982, Kahn 1988'; p. 14 begins at line 858). The first two sentences of the description are faithful and precise. The final sentence — 'The claim demarcates her project from studies, such as Voelke's, that read the Stoic theory of action as a doctrine of will' — is an inference not in the text: Bobzien lists Voelke 1973 only as one example ('e.g.') of the literature on the concept of the will and characterizes none of the four works. That over-claim confines the verdict to the last sentence; drop it or mark it as the node's own gloss.
  - « However, as I see it, the development of the concept of the will did not spring from the discussion of early Stoic determinism and freedom. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:836-838] (Bobzien 1998, p. 13)
  - « e.g. Gilbert 1963, Voelke 1973, Dihle 1982, Kahn 1988. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Bobzien - 2001 - Determinism and Freedom in Stoic Philosophy.md:856] (Bobzien 1998, p. 13 n. 27)
- Description proposée : In the Introduction Bobzien brackets the history of the concept of the will from her subject: although 'clearly related to the problem of determinism and freedom', 'the development of the concept of the will did not spring from the discussion of early Stoic determinism and freedom' (n. 27 cites, as examples, Gilbert 1963, Voelke 1973, Dihle 1982, Kahn 1988). She does not deny that the early Stoic concept of assent and, in its wake, the Epictetan concept of prohairesis are essential both for the development of the concept of the will and for the compatibility problem; she treats them where required (6.3.3-6, 8.7) without aiming at comprehensiveness.

### `scholarly_argument_boys_stones_comparison_between_middle_plat_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Comparison between Middle Platonist and Stoic fate theories — The Middle Platonist theory of fate is considerably closer to the Stoic account than generally acknowledged. Both accept that events in the cosmos follow one another with predictable regularity according to antecedent causes. The crucial difference is that Platonists (unlike Stoics) insist that the cosmos itself need not have been this way—no sublunary event is fated absolutely.
- Constat : The three claims (closer to the Stoic account than acknowledged; regular succession of events within the cosmos; the one crucial difference = the cosmos need not have been this way, so no sublunary event is fated absolutely) are stated almost word for word by the author. The decisive passage sits on printed p. 432 (introduction), not 431. Note that this is Boys-Stones's own proposed reading ('I shall argue'), presented against the received view.
  - « To this extent, the Platonist theory of fate is considerably closer to the Stoic account than is generally acknowledged. It differs in one crucial respect, however. For while events within the cosmos follow one another with predictable regularity, Platonists (unlike Stoics) insist that the cosmos itself need not have been this way. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Middle_Platonists_on_Fate_and_Human_Aut_OCR.md:83-89] (printed p. 432, running header)
  - « This is how Platonists can at the same time say that no event (at least, again, no event in the sublunary realm) is fated absolutely. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Middle_Platonists_on_Fate_and_Human_Aut_OCR.md:90-92] (printed p. 432)

The operative point is the distinction between god's providence and that of the heavens: since Stoics identify nature, fate and providence, Platonists concede 'let nature be providence' but as a 'secondary providence', restoring 'the supremely providential role of the Demiurge' (pp. 446-447). Ps.-Plutarch's 'tertiary' providence may be a Sorites aimed at the Stoics (p. 447).

### `scholarly_argument_boys_stones_two_source_metaphysics_vs_stoi_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Two-source metaphysics vs. Stoic monism — Platonists posited two causal streams (divine providence + pre-existing matter) versus Stoic coextension of God and matter; this makes the cosmos a product of chance encounter, not divine planning
- Constat : Two causal streams vs god coextensive with matter is verbatim the author's (p. 440). But 'this makes the cosmos a product of chance encounter, not divine planning' flattens a distinction he insists on: god remains 'the ultimate explanatory cause of the ordered cosmos as such' and 'responsible for the laws of nature'; what is 'literally, the product of chance' is 'the set of individuals that populates the world' (sublunary individuals), for which god is not directly responsible (p. 441). The phrase 'chance encounter' is not his either.
  - « For the Platonist world is the product not of one, but of two causal streams.   - « god (the demiurge or divine intellect) is the ultimate explanatory cause of the ordered cosmos as such (which means that he is responsible for the laws of nature, which is the law instantiated by fate), he is not directly responsible for any of the individuals » [01_Philosophie_antique/Middle_Platonists_on_Fate_and_Human_Aut_OCR.md:338-344] (printed p. 441, right column)

- Description proposée : Where the Stoics take god to be coextensive with matter, so that 'everything is due to god', the Platonist world is 'the product not of one, but of two causal streams': divine providence which transcends the world, and matter which (at least conceptually) predates it (p. 440). God remains 'the ultimate explanatory cause of the ordered cosmos as such' and of the laws of nature instantiated by fate; but he is 'not directly responsible' for the sublunary individuals that result from applying those laws to matter — 'the set of individuals that populates the world is, literally, the product of chance' (p. 441).
- Pages : 440-441

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_absence_of_will_as_arbitrary_i_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Absence of 'will' as arbitrary impulse in Greek cosmology — Greek philosophical cosmology did not require assuming a will with unpredictable impulse behind rational programming of reality; concepts like nature, cosmos, order, providence illustrated that everything happens from preconceived rational arrangement without separate spontaneous will
- Constat : Quote located verbatim (OCR variants 'rhe', 'accord-ingly' aside) at lines 229-237, printed p. 2 (page_map citation_page=2, conf 0.95; agentic_search --strict-cite confirms p.2). Dihle frames this as the 'basic presupposition' of Greek philosophical theology/cosmology: the human mind can grasp the rational order, hence 'no need for assuming ... a will of which the impulse or manifestation is unpredictable'. The description reproduces both halves (no separate will; nature/cosmos/order/providence as illustrations). Page range 2-3 acceptable (passage ends on p. 3 with the caveat about man's empirical condition).
  - « There is no need for assuming behind or apart from the entirely rational programming of reality a will of which the impulse or man-ifestation is unpredictable. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:235-237] (Dihle 1982, p. 2)
  - « all these concepts illustrate that everything happens only in consequence of a preconceived and ra-tional arrangement without a separate will spontaneously interfering with the process. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:241-243] (Dihle 1982, p. 2)

- Nœud actuel : Augustine's invention of the philosophical will — St. Augustine formally invented the philosophical concept of will as a tool of analysis, drawing on Biblical anthropology (man created in God's image) and Neoplatonic ontology through introspection, making Trinitarian theology and human psychology directly related
- Constat : The opening sentence of ch. VI is located verbatim at lines 5425-5428 (printed p. 123) and Dihle endorses it in his own voice at p. 144 ('St. Augustine was, in fact, the inventor of our modern notion of will'). The Biblical-anthropology (Gen 1:27), introspection and Trinity/psychology elements are on pp. 124-125. But the node says Augustine drew 'on Biblical anthropology ... and Neoplatonic ontology through introspection', which flattens Dihle's contrast: on pp. 123-124 the shared Neoplatonic/Christian ontological premises are what 'precluded the transference of the notion of will ... to the field of Biblical anthropology', and Augustine's introspective method was 'quite against the philosophical tradition'; unlike Victorinus he 'did not use the Porphyrian triad' to explain the Trinity. Dihle does say (p. 144) Augustine worked 'in continuation of' the Greek Trinitarian theologians, so the relation is continuation-plus-break, not a drawing-on. The word 'formally' is not Dihle's. page_range '5425-5428, 5494-5511' are .md line numbers, not printed pages: printed pp. 123-125 (and 144).
  - « the notion of will, as it is used as a tool of analysis and description in many philosophical doctrines from the early Scholas-tics to Schopenhauer and Nietzsche,' was invented by St. Augustine. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:5425-5428] (Dihle 1982, p. 123)
  - « These fundamen-tal assumptions precluded the transference of the notion of will, which had been invented ro attain an adequate description of the unfolding of reality, from theology and ontology to the field of Biblical anthropol-ogy, ethics, and soteriology. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:5445-5448] (Dihle 1982, p. 123)
  - « But St. Augustine's attempt to verify the similarity by dis-covering the points of direct reference on both sides was quite against the philosophical tradition. He attempted to apply what he had per-ceived by means of introspection and self-analysis to the image of the Trinity. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:5503-5508] (Dihle 1982, pp. 124-125)
- Description proposée : Dihle accepts the general view, and asserts it himself, that St. Augustine 'invented' the notion of will used as a tool of analysis from the Scholastics to Nietzsche. Augustine knew the Neoplatonic (Porphyrian) triad used by Marius Victorinus but did not apply it to the Trinity; he approached will from the Biblical belief that man is God's image and, 'quite against the philosophical tradition', applied introspection and self-analysis to the image of the Trinity, so that Trinitarian theology and human psychology became directly related. The shared ontological premises had previously precluded transferring 'will' to anthropology.
- Pages : 123-125, 144

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_greek_concept_of_will_0` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 3054-3056 (chapter IV opening, printed p. 68; page_map conf 0.95, agentic_search confirms p.68). The rest of the description is a faithful paraphrase of p. 69: the concept of choice (προαίρεσις) 'does not admit the existence of a will separate from the intellectual cognition ... It only admits intention as an intellectual phenomenon and striving as the result of instinct or emotion'. But page_range '3054-3057, 3110-3127' are .md line numbers, not printed pages; the printed pages are 68-69.
  - « we came to under-stand why the Greeks, in their attempts to analyze and to evaluate hu-man action, never developed a distinct concept of will. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:3054-3056] (Dihle 1982, p. 68)
  - « the concept of choice does not admit the exis-tence of a will separate from the intellectual cognition and evaluation of means and ends. It only admits intention as an intellectual phenome-non and striving as the result of instinct or emotion or a combination of both. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:3121-3124] (Dihle 1982, p. 69)

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_greek_philosophical_theology_a_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Greek philosophical theology and divine will — Greek philosophical theology from Plato and Aristotle onward did not conceive of divine will as arbitrary or unpredictable intention; rather, divine activity manifests perfect rationality, order, and beauty. The demiurge realizes only what reason shows to be possible and always chooses the best. No separate will spontaneously interferes with the rational programming of reality.
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 208-213 (printed p. 2; OCR 'tfe kg.' for de leg.). The demiurge clause matches p. 1 ('The Greek creator or demiurge brings to reality only what reason evinces as being possible, and from all possibilities he always chooses the best one'); 'from Plato and Aristotle onward' matches Dihle's 'as expounded most clearly by Plato and Aristotle' and 'from the very beginning' (p. 2). The 'no separate will' clause is p. 2. Page range 1-3 correct.
  - « Yet this will does not mean arbitrary intention or unpredictable exercise of divine power. Every act of divine rule over the universe is but a detail of a comprehen-sive and perfectly rational programme » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:210-212] (Dihle 1982, p. 2)
  - « The Greek creator or demiurge brings to reality only what reason evinces as being possible,'1 and from all possibilities he always chooses the best one.' » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:186-188] (Dihle 1982, p. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_historical_development_of_the__3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Historical development of the 'will' concept — The concept of will as a distinct, arbitrary faculty separate from reason was not developed in Greek philosophy but emerged from Biblical theology and later Christian thought (foreshadowing discussions of Paul, Philo, and Augustine in subsequent chapters).
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 979-982, printed p. 19 (end of ch. I), not pp. 3-4 as page_range states (quote_page 'p. 19' in the node is right, page_range is wrong). Content: Dihle says the 'clear-cut notion of will' was 'always implied in the Biblical image of God, man, and the universe, though utterly alien to Greek cosmology and ethics', and that it 'took a very long time to conceive, by the means and methods of Greek philosophy'. The description's 'distinct, arbitrary faculty separate from reason' imports 'arbitrary' (Galen's word for the Jewish creator's intention, p. 1) into Dihle's definition of will; Dihle's own gloss (p. 20) is 'will as a factor separate from both reason and emotion or instinct'. 'Emerged from Biblical theology and later Christian thought' also drops Dihle's point that it was conceived by Greek philosophical means (Augustine, ch. VI).
  - « Yet it still took a very long time to conceive, by the means and methods of Greek philosophy, the clear-cut notion of will which was always implied in the Biblical image of God, man, and the universe, though utterly alien to Greek cosmology and ethics. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:979-982] (Dihle 1982, p. 19)
  - « Moreover, the idea of will as a factor separate from both reason and emotion or instinct permeates all layers and periods of European thought. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:1000-1002] (Dihle 1982, p. 20)
- Description proposée : Dihle closes ch. I by stating that the 'clear-cut notion of will' — will as a factor separate from both reason and emotion or instinct — was always implied in the Biblical image of God, man and the universe, yet utterly alien to Greek cosmology and ethics; it took a very long time before it was conceived 'by the means and methods of Greek philosophy' (the story of Paul, Philo, the Greek theologians and finally Augustine in the later chapters).
- Pages : 19-20

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_jewish_and_christian_conceptio_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Jewish and Christian conception of divine will vs. Greek philosophy — Jewish and early Christian theology conceived of creation as dependent solely on the arbitrary will of the divine creator, who could transform a lump of earth into any creature. This conception was preferable to Epicurean chance but incompatible with Greek rational cosmology. This fundamental difference between Greek and biblical thought becomes central to the historical development of the 'will' concept.
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 185-195 (printed p. 1). But the passage is Dihle's report of Galen's digression in De usu partium: 'The Jews, he says, derive...'; 'This opinion, according to Galen, is certainly preferable to the aleatoric conception of Epicurus'. The node turns reported speech into Dihle's own assertions: (a) the 'preferable to Epicurean chance' judgement is Galen's, not Dihle's; (b) 'early Christian theology' is not in the passage — Galen speaks of the Jews, and Dihle of 'the Biblical concept of creation' (p. 3) and 'Biblical cosmology' (p. 4). Dihle does endorse, in his own voice on p. 4, the incompatibility (creation 'results from the power and the pleasure or will of Yahveh, and from nothing else'; the creator 'can create, change, and destroy as He pleases'), so the substantive contrast is his — but the node's framing misattributes Galen's evaluation.
  - « The Jews, he says, derive rhe origin and structure of the universe exclusively from the arbitrary intention of the divine creator.' He can transform a lump of earth into a horse or a bird or whatever he pleases. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:185-188] (Dihle 1982, p. 1)
  - « This opinion, according to Galen, is certainly preferable to the aleatoric conception of Epicurus who attributes everything to chance. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:189-191] (Dihle 1982, p. 1)
  - « There is no stan-dard, nor rule applicable to the creator and his creation alike. Creation results from the power and the pleasure or will of Yahveh, and from nothing else." He can create, change, and destroy as He pleases » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:303-306] (Dihle 1982, p. 4)
- Description proposée : Dihle opens with Galen's digression (De usu partium): according to Galen, the Jews derive the universe exclusively from the arbitrary intention of the creator, who can turn a lump of earth into a horse or a bird — an opinion Galen judges preferable to Epicurus' chance but incompatible with Greek cosmology as expounded by Plato and Aristotle. Dihle himself adds (p. 4) that Biblical cosmology admits no rule binding creator and creation alike: creation results from the power and pleasure or will of Yahveh and from nothing else. This contrast frames the whole inquiry into 'will'.
- Pages : 1-4

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_monotheistic_convergence_with__3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

- Constat : Located at lines 290-303, printed pp. 3-4 (page_map conf 0.95; agentic_search confirms 'does not create ex nihilo' at p. 4). Dihle: both conceptions 'were monotheistic, despite their disagreement in many other respects'; the Greek 'supreme creator and ruler always restricts his activity, according to philosophical doctrine, by laws or rules which the human mind can understand as reasonable, good, and salutary'; 'he does not create ex nihilo. He molds what was without shape'. Description reproduces these three points. Dihle also adds 'above all, he does not transcend the order which embraces himself as well as his creatures' — not in the node but no distortion.
  - « Both conceptions, the Greek and the Biblical, were monotheistic, despite their disagreement in many other respects. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:290-291] (Dihle 1982, p. 3)
  - « But this supreme creator and ruler always restricts his activity, according to philosophical doctrine, by laws or rules which the human mind can understand as reasonable, good, and salutary. Such a creator, to be sure, has also the desire to create and to govern the universe. Bur he does not create ex nihilo. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:295-299] (Dihle 1982, p. 4)

### `scholarly_argument_dihle_paul_s_concept_of_will_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Paul's concept of will — St. Paul implicitly but revolutionarily introduced a notion of will distinct from both intellect and emotion, grounding moral action in obedience to God's commandment rather than rational knowledge, though he lacked precise terminology for it
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 3719-3727, printed p. 83. Dihle: Paul's will is 'distinguished from all intellectual achievements ... as well as from all unconscious and spontaneous emotions', it 'responds to the commandment of God' and 'determines human action'; 'There is no such term in the language of St. Paul'; 'The underlying concept, however, is quite clear'. The obedience-not-knowledge point is at p. 80 ('every factual fulfilment of the Law is primarily an act of obedience'; the works of the Law 'testify to the will rather than the intellect'). Two problems: 'revolutionarily' is not Dihle's word or judgement (no 'revolution' in the text), and the node drops Dihle's own limitation on p. 83 that the impact of Paul's doctrine 'on Christian theology in the following two or three centuries was not too heavy'. page_range '3719-3734' are .md line numbers; printed pp. 80-83.
  - « for it is, in his view, a will, as distinguished from all intellectual achievements—even with regard to the knowledge of the divine Law— as well as from all unconscious and spontaneous emotions, which re-sponds to the commandment of God. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:3721-3724] (Dihle 1982, p. 83)
  - « To St. Paul, every factual fulfilment of the Law is primarily an act of obedience, appropriate in every creature towards the Creator. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:3589-3590] (Dihle 1982, p. 80)
  - « and the impact it made on Christian theology in the following two or three centuries was not too heavy. » [Albrecht Dihle - The Theory of Will in Classical Antiquity (Sather Classical Lectures) (1982, University of California Press).md:3703-3704] (Dihle 1982, p. 83)
- Description proposée : For Dihle, Paul's teaching implies a will of man distinguished both from all intellectual achievements (even knowledge of the divine Law) and from unconscious, spontaneous emotions: a will that responds to God's commandment, determines action and is therefore subject to divine and moral judgement. Fulfilling the Law is primarily an act of obedience, testifying to will rather than intellect. Paul had no term for this ('no such term in the language of St. Paul and his contemporaries'), though the underlying concept is 'quite clear'; Dihle adds that its impact on the next two or three centuries of Christian theology 'was not too heavy'.
- Pages : 80-83
