## eliasson-2008-2009
Sources utilisées : /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md, /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Both works are in the fonds as native extractions with page maps (page_map.json: 2008 book via PDF page labels, 265/266 pages mapped; 2009 article via running headers, offset -406, 24/24). Printed pages were resolved with page_map_io.citation_for_line and cross-checked against the Brill proof running headers ('... page N.') in the 2008 file and the '4NN Erik Eliasson' headers in the 2009 file. Greek in both extractions is font-degraded (2008: diacritics dropped, e.g. 'φ’ μν'; 2009: Symbol-font garble, e.g. '3retQ dA 3d@spoton'), so the polytonic Greek written in the node descriptions (τὸ ἐφ' ἡμῖν, ἀδέσποτον, νόμοι εἱμαρμένοι, τάξις καὶ νόμος τῆς εἱμαρμένης, συγκατάθεσις) could not be verified against these files; evidence quotes reproduce the file text as is. Summary: 2008 lot — 5 FAITHFUL (vi8_knowledge_conditions, self_directedness_unprecedented, critique_stoics_alexander_impressions, roman_stoa_eudaimonist_shift), 4 OVERSIMPLIFIED (alexander, aristotle, plotinus_reconsidered, that_which_depends_on_us), 3 DISTORTED (chrysippus: Eliasson largely agrees with Bobzien 1998a rather than calling for re-examination; middle_platonist: Philo and Plutarch are his stated exceptions, not exemplars of the standard notion; leroux: the eph' hemin/eleutheria conflation charge targets Armstrong 1982). 2009 lot — 5 FAITHFUL, 1 PAGE_WRONG (plato_s_synthesis is on p. 409 not 410), 2 OVERSIMPLIFIED (myth of Er: the 619b3/619e1/620d inconsistency is flattened; plotinus_s_theory_of_fate: 'rather than primarily as anti-Stoic polemic' contradicts his 'sans naturellement négliger'). Vocabulary note: Eliasson uses 'compatibility' (pp. 83, 118) and, once in a footnote, 'Chrysippus’ compatibilism' (p. 84 n. 14) and 'the compatibilism of fate and providence' (p. 30); he never uses 'libertarian' or 'contingency' in the Alexander section. All quotes verified verbatim; the two exact-search misses ('Plotinus, then, in this respect...' and 'Notre objectif est de préciser') were due to line-end hyphenation and were confirmed by reading the lines.

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_alexander_of_aphrodisias_on_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander of Aphrodisias on ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Alexander developed a distinctive notion of ἐφ' ἡμῖν in the context of defending Aristotelian contingency against Stoic determinism, representing a key stage in the development of the concept.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 74 (Greek dropped in the extraction exactly as in the node). Page range 72-79 = section 3.4 (conclusions 3.5 run to p. 80). But the description replaces Eliasson's actual point with a generic frame: he never speaks of 'contingency'; what he identifies as the development is that Alexander 'explicitly opts for an exclusive notion', spelled out as what comes about with an unforced assent in accordance with reason and judgment, and that this move lies behind his 'perhaps innovative association of the notion with choice' — while insisting Alexander only associates the notion with choice and never says choice is eph' hemin. The anti-Stoic context is only a footnote caveat (n. 110: De fato 'must counter mainly Stoic criticism of Aristotle'; p. 72: a problem 'unknown to Aristotle').
  - « On the other hand we do find something in Alexander that clearly constitutes a development in the Aristotelian notion of τ φ’ μν. Unlike Aristotle, who is vague as to whether the notion of φ’ μν is to be understood in what we have called the inclusive or exclusive way, Alexander in fact explicitly opts for an exclusive notion » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:4048-4052] (Eliasson 2008, p. 74)
  - « Thus, Alexander never to my knowledge goes as far as saying that ‘choosing’ is φ’ μν. This simply is not the way he uses the notion. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:4045-4047] (Eliasson 2008, p. 74)
  - « Alexander, on the contrary, clearly develops an exclusive notion of φ’ μν, defining it as what comes about with an unforced assent that is in accordance with reason and judgment. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:11361-11363] (Eliasson 2008, p. 218)
- Description proposée : Eliasson finds in Alexander's De fato one clear development of the Aristotelian notion of eph' hemin: where Aristotle is vague between an inclusive and an exclusive notion, Alexander explicitly opts for the exclusive one, defining what depends on us as what comes about with an unforced assent in accordance with reason and judgment (De fato 183.26-32), and this move underlies his 'perhaps innovative' association of the notion with choice — though Alexander only associates it with choice and never says choice itself is eph' hemin. Eliasson notes (n. 110) that De fato must counter Stoic criticism unknown to Aristotle.
- Pages : 72-80

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_aristotle_s_notion_of_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's notion of ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Aristotle developed both an inclusive and exclusive notion of ἐφ' ἡμῖν. The inclusive notion covers all voluntary actions, while the exclusive notion is narrower. Aristotle applied the notion to internal states as well as actions, and there is a tentative ontology underlying his usage.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 60 (section 3.1.5); pages 47-60 = section 3.1 (ends p. 61). Internal states (3.1.4, p. 57-60) and 'tentative ontology' (3.1.3, p. 52) are Eliasson's own headings. Two losses: (1) Eliasson does not say Aristotle 'developed' two notions — the inclusive/exclusive pair is a distinction he introduces to capture a 'basic unclarity' / 'vagueness' in Aristotle's condition that the origin is in the agent; (2) the pair is defined by rational agency (inclusive = 'in no way tied to rational agency alone'; exclusive = rational agents and their activities alone), not by 'all voluntary actions'.
  - « his notion is still unclear in a way which poses problems for its interpretation. The basic unclarity consists in that he employs both what we have called an exclusive notion of φ’ μν, related to a notion of action on which all actions, including actions that are φ’ μν, are rational actions » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:3306-3310] (Eliasson 2008, p. 60)
  - « The inclusive refers to the notion being in no way tied to rational agency alone. The exclusive refers to the notion being applied to rational agents and their activities alone. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:4335-4337] (Eliasson 2008, p. 80)
  - « Aristotle applies the notion of φ’ μν equally not only to actions (πρ*ξεις) but to internal states of the agent as well » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:3290-3291] (Eliasson 2008, p. 60)
- Description proposée : Eliasson holds that Aristotle's notion of eph' hemin, central to the preconditions of virtue in EN III, is 'unclear': the condition that the origin is in the agent is vague, so Aristotle employs both what Eliasson calls an exclusive notion (tied to rational agents and their activities alone) and an inclusive notion (not tied to rational agency alone). Aristotle applies the notion equally to actions and to internal states (character states, affections, thoughts, reasonings), and his account of deliberation adds a 'tentative' ontological basis not present in ordinary-language uses.
- Pages : 47-61

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_chrysippus_and_stoic_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chrysippus and Stoic ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Chrysippus's notion of ἐφ' ἡμῖν must be understood within the framework of Stoic compatibilism and fate theory (causal origination through assent). The standard interpretation requires careful re-examination based on the sources.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 116 (section 4.6). The first sentence's content (causes/fatedness, auxiliary causes, agent's nature expressed in assent) is faithful; 'compatibilism' is attested in Eliasson only in n. 14 (p. 84, 'Chrysippus’ compatibilism'), elsewhere he says 'compatibility'. The second sentence misreads his stance: the 'recent interpretation' (4.1.1) is Bobzien 1998a, with which Eliasson says 'we have reasons to largely agree' and 'I will agree with most of this', proposing only to 'slightly modify or specify' points (i) and (iv). He also adopts from Bobzien the view that the issue was the agent's autonomy as cause, not alternative possibilities. 'Requires careful re-examination' is not his position.
  - « One fairly recent and substantial view that we have reasons to largely agree with is the one defended in Bobzien (1998a). » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:4534-4535] (Eliasson 2008, p. 84)
  - « In what follows I will agree with most of this. Still, the textual basis, I take it, will demand that we slightly modify or specify (i) as well as (iv). » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:4576-4578] (Eliasson 2008, p. 85)
  - « Thus, Chrysippus’ notion of φ’ μν is one that seeks to identify the contribution made by agents to the causation of their actions. This feature vanishes in the Roman Stoa. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:6247-6249] (Eliasson 2008, p. 116)
- Description proposée : For Eliasson, Chrysippus' notion of eph' hemin (applied to internal acts of assent and impulse, and to external actions) rests on his account of causes and their fatedness: auxiliary external causes do not by themselves explain an action, which also requires reference to the agent's nature as expressed in his assent; the notion thus identifies the agent's contribution to the causation of his actions, within a defence of the compatibility of the theory of fate with a common notion of what depends on us. Eliasson largely agrees with Bobzien 1998a, only slightly modifying two points.
- Pages : 82-97, 116-118

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_middle_platonist_notions_of_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Middle Platonist notions of ἐφ' ἡμῖν — Middle Platonists developed a standard notion of ἐφ' ἡμῖν that drew on Platonic sources while responding to Stoic and Peripatetic developments. This standard notion is visible across diverse sources including Philo, Plutarch, Alcinous, Ps.-Plutarch, Nemesius, Calcidius, Apuleius, and Maximus of Tyre.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 166; the quote itself already says 'several authors that constitute interesting exceptions to this standard view'. The description lists Philo and Plutarch among the sources in which the standard notion 'is visible', whereas Eliasson names exactly these two as the exceptions: the early Philo does not connect the notion to an account of fate at all (exclusive notion tied to praise and blame), and Plutarch works with a tripartition of causes that excludes eph' hemin from fate. The standard account is found in Alcinous, Ps.-Plutarch, Nemesius, Calcidius, Apuleius (with a caveat, n. 201) and Maximus. 'Responding to Stoic and Peripatetic developments' is also not his framing: the account was 'inspired by some hints in various Platonic passages', with the modal taxonomy drawing on Aristotle's EN III.
  - « As examples of such exceptions, e.g. the early Philo of Alexandria does not connect the notion of φ’ μν to an account of fate, but the issue is often simply the proper analysis of the basis of moral praise and blame. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:8817-8820] (Eliasson 2008, p. 166)
  - « With these two exceptions in mind, the elements of the standard Middle-Platonist account become more clear. In the main sources for this account analyzed above, Alcinous’ Didascalicus, Ps.-Plutarch’s De fato, Nemesius of Emesa’s De natura hominis, Calcidius’ In Timaeum, Apuleius’ De Platone,201 and Maximus of Tyre’s Orations, the main idea is that all things are included in fate. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:8834-8839] (Eliasson 2008, p. 166)
  - « As we have seen, this account was inspired by some hints in various Platonic passages, rather than any complete Platonic theory of the matter. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:8862-8864] (Eliasson 2008, p. 167)
- Description proposée : Eliasson discerns a standard Middle-Platonist notion of eph' hemin, found in Alcinous, Ps.-Plutarch, Nemesius, Calcidius, Apuleius (with reservations) and Maximus of Tyre: all things are included in fate, but what depends on us is included hypothetically, neither fated nor necessary, while its consequences are fated — an account inspired by hints in Plato, with a modal taxonomy drawn from Aristotle's EN III. The early Philo (no account of fate; exclusive notion tied to praise and blame) and Plutarch (tripartition fate/chance/eph' hemin) are the exceptions to this standard view.
- Pages : 119-167

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_plotinus_s_notion_of_reconside_5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)

The node restricts the claim to occurrences outside VI.8 and adds 'in his metaphysical framework', which is not his phrase; the actual result for the treatises outside VI.8 (a notion that 'clearly tends towards an exclusive notion') is missing. page_range '169-' is open-ended; chapter 6 runs pp. 169-216.
  - « Now, then, we are more able to say something specific on how Plotinus’ notion relates to this tradition, i.e. what more specifically it is that he criticizes, what he adopts in his own account, and what he achieves by his own analysis. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:8956-8959] (Eliasson 2008, p. 170)
  - « Plotinus’ notion of φ’ μν outside Ennead VI.8 clearly tends towards an exclusive notion, i.e. one in which only some things, namely those that involve a specific rational activity, and in a true sense have their principle in us, are φ’ μν. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:11270-11273] (Eliasson 2008, p. 215)
- Description proposée : In chapter 6 Eliasson re-reads every occurrence of eph' hemin and of epi + dative of person in the Enneads — first outside VI.8 (III.1, I.4, III.2), then within VI.8 — in the light of his study of the Aristotelian, Stoic and Middle-Platonist background, in order to state what Plotinus criticizes, what he adopts, and what he achieves. Outside VI.8 the notion 'clearly tends towards an exclusive notion', restricted to what involves a specific rational activity and truly has its principle in us.

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_that_which_depends_on_us_in_pl_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : τὸ ἐφ' ἡμῖν (that which depends on us) in Plotinus — The standard interpretations of ἐφ' ἡμῖν in Plotinus—freedom, free choice, free will, free action, and self-determination—are all inadequate or partial. A terminological approach tracing the history of the phrase is necessary to properly understand Plotinus's usage.
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 42 (section 2.4). The five labels (freedom, free choice, free will, free action, self-determination) are Eliasson's own groupings of previous scholarship (2.3.1-2.3.5, pp. 27-42). But he does not say they are 'all inadequate or partial': his diagnosis is that the notion has not been treated as a separate issue, that interpreters 'simply stipulate their own readings' without arguing against others, that there is 'no consensus' on sources, and that confusion derives from the 'non-argued-for assumption' that Plotinus and his predecessors discussed one general problem. The terminological approach is motivated in 1.3 (p. 14) and 2.4 as a way to come to terms with 'some of these problems', not stated as strictly 'necessary'.
  - « Rather, as a rule, people tend to simply stipulate their own readings of the notion without taking into account the fact that other people have diﬀerent readings of it. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:1692-1694] (Eliasson 2008, p. 27)
  - « The present study thus aims at coming to terms with some of these problems, by analyzing the notions of φ’ μν in the diﬀerent schools within the tradition of writings on φ’ μν preceding Plotinus. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:2489-2491] (Eliasson 2008, p. 43)
- Description proposée : Eliasson sorts previous readings of Plotinus' to eph' hemin into five kinds — the freedom, free choice, free will, free action and self-determination interpretations — and finds that the notion has seldom been treated as a separate issue, that readings are stipulated rather than argued, that there is no consensus on Plotinus' sources (Stoic, EN III, Alexander, Platonic), and that much confusion stems from the non-argued-for assumption that Plotinus and his predecessors discussed one general problem in different terms. His terminological, historical study of the pre-Plotinian tradition aims to come to terms with these problems.
- Pages : 27-43

- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 218 (lines 11382-11396). ### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_leroux_freedom_theme_imprecise` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Eliasson criticises Leroux (1996) as an instance of the 'freedom interpretation' of Plotinus's to eph' hemin: Leroux does not primarily study the notion as such, his remarks on it are 'rather imprecise', and it is difficult to see a straight line of interpretation. By grouping the attack on determinism, the positive account of what depends on us, the descent of the soul, and the goal of life all under 'freedom'/'liberty'/'liberation', Leroux — Eliasson objects — is not automatically warranted in 'grouping them all together and inventing the theme', and the mixing of eph' hemin with eleutheria 
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 30; the 'rather imprecise' remark, the n. 28 critique ('does not automatically warrant Leroux’s grouping them all together and inventing the theme') and the Leroux 1996, 310-311 statement judged 'not very informative' (p. 31) are all correctly reported. But the clause 'the mixing of eph' hemin with eleutheria makes the distinction vanish between the compatibility issue grounding praise and blame and eleutheria as an attribute of the soul of the wise alone' is Eliasson's criticism of Armstrong (1982), stated in the paragraph preceding the Leroux discussion (pp. 29-30, with n. 27 'Had Armstrong taken this distinction into consideration'). The node transfers that objection to Leroux.
  - « Another example of this line of interpretation is Armstrong (1982), who states that: » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:1806-1807] (Eliasson 2008, p. 29)
  - « the issue of the compatibilism of fate and providence with something being φ’ μν, i.e. concerning the basis for our everyday practice of praise and blame, and on the other hand, the issue of λευερα as an attribute of the soul of the wise alone, entirely vanishes.27 » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:1843-1846] (Eliasson 2008, p. 30)
  - « Thus, he does not primarily study the notion of φ’ μν as such, but still makes some remarks on how he understands it. It turns out however, that these remarks are rather imprecise, and it is diﬃcult to see a straight line of interpretation. » [[Philosophia Antiqua 113] Erik Eliasson - The Notion of That Which Depends on Us in Plotinus and Its Background (Philosophia Antiqua - Volume 113) (2008, Brill Academic Publishers).md:1849-1852] (Eliasson 2008, p. 30)
- Description proposée : Eliasson places Leroux (1996) under the 'freedom interpretation': Leroux studies 'human freedom' rather than the notion of eph' hemin as such, and his remarks on it are 'rather imprecise', with no straight line of interpretation. Leroux groups the attack on determinism, the positive account of what depends on us, the descent of the soul and the goal of life under 'freedom'/'liberty'/'liberation'; Eliasson objects that their interconnection does not warrant 'grouping them all together and inventing the theme'. Leroux's most precise statement (1996, 310-311) is 'not very informative' on either reading. (The eph' hemin/eleutheria conflation charge is levelled at Armstrong 1982, pp. 29-30.)

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_interpretation_of_republic_x_m_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Interpretation of Republic X myth of Er — The myth contains multiple elements whose relationship is difficult to determine: casting of lots constrains order of choice but does not make happy life impossible; the relationship between choosing a 'demon', choosing a life, and valuing virtue remains unclear; the meaning of virtue being 'without master' (ἀδέσποτον) is ambiguous—whether it refers to pre-incarnation choice guaranteeing virtue, or whether the δέ is adversative indicating that many other aspects are determined by necessity in embodied life
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 409 (lines 114-132; the Greek is font-garbled in the file, e.g. '3retQ dA 3d@spoton', so the polytonic tokens in the node cannot be checked against this file). The whole passage sits on p. 409, not 409-410. The description flattens Eliasson's first difficulty: he does not say the lots 'do not make happy life impossible' but points to an inconsistency — at 619b3 being last is a decisive disadvantage without making the happy life entirely impossible, at 619e1 it 'semblerait rendre la vie heureuse impossible', and at 620d the lots again determine only the order. He also frames the list as the passage's 'difficultés les plus évidentes', explicitly declining to assume a single unambiguous meaning intended by Plato.
  - « Et, alors que plus tard (619 e 1) être le dernier à choisir semblerait rendre la vie heureuse impossible, plus loin encore (620 d) les sorts ne déterminent de nouveau que l’ordre selon lequel les âmes s’ap- prochent de Lachésis pour en recevoir les vies qu’elles ont choisies. » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:118-121] (Eliasson 2009, p. 409)
  - « Il suffit ici, au lieu de sup- poser que ce récit a une signification unique, précise et sans ambiguïté, voulue par Platon, d’indiquer brièvement ses difficultés les plus évidentes. » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:111-113] (Eliasson 2009, p. 409)
- Description proposée : Eliasson isolates five elements of the myth of Er (lots constraining the order of choice; choice of a daimon; choice of a life bound by necessity; virtue 'without master'; each having virtue according as he honours it) and, refusing to assume a single unambiguous meaning intended by Plato, lists its most obvious difficulties: the lots determine only the order, yet at 619b3 choosing last is a decisive disadvantage, at 619e1 it would seem to make the happy life impossible, at 620d only order again; the relation between elements (ii), (iii) and (v) is unclear; and 'virtue without master' is ambiguous (pre-incarnation choice guaranteeing virtue, or an adversative de marking that much else is determined by necessity here below).
- Pages : 409

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_laws_x_on_order_and_law_of_fat_5` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Laws X on order and law of fate — The important notion is that of an 'order and law of fate' (τάξις καὶ νόμος τῆς εἱμαρμένης) and especially its relation to the distinction between causes of changes that animated beings have 'in themselves' and the resulting movements that follow the order and law of fate
- Constat : No quote_verbatim; the description is a close rendering of the sentence 'L’important dans ce passage... est la notion d’un ordre et d’une loi du destin... et surtout sa relation avec la distinction... entre les causes des changements des êtres animés que ces derniers ont en eux-mêmes et les déplacements qui en résultent' (p. 410, continuing to p. 411). Pages 410-411 correct. The Greek in the node cannot be verified against the file (font-garbled). One point could be added: Plato here, unlike in the myth of Er, ties the soul's activity to the order and law of fate while keeping it outside fate's determining power.
  - « L’important dans ce passage et dans le récit de l’Athénien en général est la notion d’un ordre et d’une loi du destin » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:181-182] (Eliasson 2009, p. 410)
  - « Platon, plutôt que de considérer que les activités de l’âme sont antérieures à l’action du destin, comme il le fait dans le mythe d’Er, tente ici de souligner comment l’activité de l’âme est liée à l’ordre et à la loi du destin, tout en restant en dehors de sa puissance de détermination. » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:195-199] (Eliasson 2009, p. 411)

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_plato_s_concept_of_fate_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Plato's concept of fate — Plato did not have a fully developed technical theory of fate; his texts provide pre-technical sources, intuitions, and main elements for later Platonic theories
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 408 (lines 62-68). Description matches: Plato probably introduced the notion of fate into a philosophical context, but has no 'véritable théorie sur un sujet philosophique déjà établi'; his texts are 'sources, encore peu techniques' of the intuitions guiding later Platonic theories and of their main elements. Pages 408-411 cover the section 'Les sources platoniciennes'.
  - « On voit facilement cependant qu’il ne s’agit pas chez lui d’une véritable théorie sur un sujet philosophique déjà établi, comme ce sera le cas plus tard2. » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:63-65] (Eliasson 2009, p. 408)
  - « Il reste que, pour les théories platoni- ciennes postérieures, les textes de Platon constituent des sources, encore peu techniques, à la fois des intuitions qui guident leurs développements et de leurs principaux éléments. » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:65-68] (Eliasson 2009, p. 408)

### `scholarly_argument_eliasson_plotinus_s_theory_of_fate_heim_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Plotinus's theory of fate (heimarmene) in Ennead III.1[3] — Plotinus develops a version of Middle Platonic fate theory, not merely refuting Stoicism but positively defending a Platonic account; the treatise should be understood as a reception of Platonic intuitions about fate rather than primarily as anti-Stoic polemic
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at p. 407 (lines 21-34). The first half of the description is faithful (Eliasson will try to show how Plotinus 'propose une version de la théorie médioplatonicienne du destin et de ce qui dépend de nous', specifying 'non seulement ce que Plotin réfute, mais aussi ce qu’il défend lui-même'). The closing contrast 'rather than primarily as anti-Stoic polemic' is not his: he expressly writes 'Sans naturellement négliger l’importance de ces influences souvent mentionnées' (anti-Stoic arguments, Alexander's De fato) and presents the Middle-Platonist reception as a complementary angle, not a replacement.
  - « Sans naturellement négliger l’importance de ces influences souvent mentionnées, nous voudrions ici nous intéresser à la relation de l’analyse plotinienne du destin à celle du médio-platonisme » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:24-26] (Eliasson 2009, p. 407)
  - « Notre objectif est de pré- ciser non seulement ce que, à propos du destin, Plotin réfute, mais aussi ce qu’il défend lui-même. Nous tenterons de montrer en particulier comment Plotin propose une version de la théorie médioplatonicienne du destin » [Eliasson - 2009 - Sur la conception plotinienne du destin dans le traité 3.md:30-33] (Eliasson 2009, p. 407)
- Description proposée : Eliasson accepts that Ennead III.1 [3] belongs to a long tradition of texts on fate and that recent commentaries rightly stress its anti-Stoic arguments and their source in Alexander's De fato. Without neglecting those influences, he examines the relation of Plotinus' analysis of fate to that of Middle Platonism, treating both as receptions of Platonic intuitions or ideas (short of a theory) on a genuinely post-Platonic subject, in order to specify not only what Plotinus refutes but what he defends: a version of the Middle-Platonist theory of fate and of 'what depends on us'.
- Pages : 407

### `scholarly_argument_frede_aristotle_on_will_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle on will — Aristotle does not have a notion of will; he has a theory of choice (prohairesis) but without a will, and therefore should be set aside in tracing the origins of free will
- Constat : The core (Aristotle has a notion of choice but no notion of a will; ch. 2 'Aristotle on Choice without a Will', pp. 19-30) is Frede's. The inferential clause 'and therefore should be set aside in tracing the origins of free will' is not Frede's: it is David Sedley's Foreword wording ('This leads him to set Aristotle aside', p. ix). Frede himself opens ch. 2 by giving 'at least three reasons why we should begin our detailed study with Aristotle' (p. 19). The node thus reverses Frede's own methodological stance. The p. 156-157 quote in verified_reference is real (lines 5313-5316) and refers to Plato AND Aristotle.
  - « There are at least three reasons why we should begin our detailed study with Aristotle. First, the Stoics can only develop a notion of a will, because they have a certain notion of the mind. » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:902-904] (Frede 2011, p. 19)
  - « Neither Plato nor Aristotle has a notion of a will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:919] (Frede 2011, p. 19)
  - « Frede’s strategy is to avoid any initial presup- positions about the term’s precise meaning and instead let his understanding of it emerge from the texts. This leads him to set Aristotle aside » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:186-190] (Sedley, Foreword, p. ix)
- Description proposée : Frede holds that neither Plato nor Aristotle has a notion of a will: Aristotle has a notion of choice (prohairesis), but a willing for him is a direct result of one's cognitive state, and non-rational desires can move us to act without or against our choice. Aristotle's view 'leaves plenty of space for unconstrained human action' but is 'hardly hospitable, even in principle, to a notion of a free will'. Frede nonetheless begins the study with Aristotle, giving three reasons for doing so; 'setting Aristotle aside' is Sedley's Foreword gloss.
- Pages : 19-20, 30; 156-157

### `scholarly_argument_frede_epictetus_and_free_will_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Epictetus and free will — Epictetus is the key figure in whom the earlier Stoic theory of assent, enriched with a developed notion of an inner life, led to the first philosophical concept plausibly identifiable as free will; prohairesis in Epictetus represents what is 'up to us' and constitutes our freedom
- Constat : The description's first clause ('the earlier Stoic theory of assent, enriched with a developed notion of an inner life, led to the first philosophical concept plausibly identifiable as free will') is Sedley's Foreword sentence (p. ix) attributed to Frede. The quote_verbatim is real but sits at lines 1773-1795, printed p. 46 (ch. 3, 'Epictetus and the first notion of a will'), outside the given range 66-88; it establishes the first notion of a WILL, not yet of a FREE will. Frede distinguishes the two steps: notion of a will (pp. 44-48) and combination with freedom (pp. 76-77). The clause 'prohairesis represents what is up to us' compresses Frede: what is up to us is how we deal with impressions; prohairesis is the disposition/ability to choose how to deal with them, 'can be called the will'; and only the wise person has a free will.
  - « So this ability and disposition, insofar as it accounts for your willing whatever it is that you will to do, can be called “the will.” But the will is called prohairesis, rather than boulēsis, to mark that it is an ability to make choices, of which willings are just products. This indeed is the ﬁrst time that we have any notion of a will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:1790-1795] (Frede 2011, p. 46)
  - « With this we can turn to the freedom of the will. For we now have a notion of a will and a notion of freedom, and we need to see how and why the two notions come to be combined in the notion of a free will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:2736-2739] (Frede 2011, p. 76)
  - « arguing that it was in Epictetus that the earlier Stoic theory of assent, enriched with a now developed notion of an inner life, led to the ﬁrst philosophical concept plausibly identiﬁable as free will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/[Sather Classical Lectures, Vol. 68] Michael Frede, A. A. Long, David Sedley - A Free Will_ Origins of the Notion in Ancient Thought (2011, University of California Press) - libgen.li.md:191-194] (Sedley, Foreword, p. ix)
- Description proposée : Frede locates in Epictetus 'the first time that we have any notion of a will' (p. 46): what is up to us is how we deal with our impressions, assent being only one such thing; prohairesis, which defines us as the person we are, is the disposition to choose how to deal with impressions, and 'can be called the will'. Only then (pp. 76-77) does Frede combine will and freedom into 'our first actual notion of a free will', a will no worldly force can constrain — possessed in fact only by the wise. Sedley's Foreword (p. ix) summarises this as assent 'enriched with a now developed notion of an inner life'.
- Pages : 44-48, 76-77 (Sedley p. ix)

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_biblical_compatibilism_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : biblical compatibilism — Maintains that the Hebrew Bible/Old Testament presents a 'biblical compatibilism' where divine providence/heilsgeschichte and human self-responsibility coexist without systematic tension; Ezekiel 18 constitutes a key 'plea for human self-responsibility' (Plädoyer für die Eigenverantwortung)
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim in the file (lines 8057-8067, printed p. 224, running header '224 / V. Das Freiheitsdenken des Origenes'); ch. I.3 = pp. 34-46 (TOC), I.3a 'Biblischer Kompatibilismus' p. 35, I.3b Ezechiel p. 39. The node's phrase 'coexist without systematic tension' is not Fürst's: he stresses that the Hebrew Bible contains passages of 'strikter Determinismus', that 'Freiheit' is neither a word nor a reflected theme there, that its freedom concept is political-religious ('theokratisch konfiguriert') and explicitly NOT one of human self-determination, and that responsibility only 'in einem deterministischen Zusammenhang' appears as a 'Miteinander und Ineinander' of divine and human action. On p. 224 he even concedes 'ausgesprochen deterministische Passagen'. Ezekiel 18 as 'Plädoyer für die Eigenverantwortung' is the section heading (verbatim), but Fürst qualifies: Ezekiel propagated 'nationale' or collective responsibility within which individual responsibility is also stated, not responsibility 'im Sinne des modernen Individualitätsbegriffs'.
  - « Das ist kein Freiheitsbegriff, in dem es um die Selbstbestimmung des Menschen und um die Verantwortung für das eigene Leben geht, wie er sich im griechischen Denken entwickelte (Kap. II). » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:1457-1459] (p. 36)
  - « In einem deterministischen Zusammenhang tauchen damit Vorstellungen auf, die man mit Selbstbestimmung und Eigenverantwortung des Menschen in Verbindung bringen kann. Wir beobachten hier ein ähnliches Miteinander und Ineinander von göttlichem und menschlichem Handeln wie im altgriechischen Epos. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:1530-1535] (p. 38)
  - « Es wäre angebrachter, meine ich, zuzugeben, dass es nun einmal ausgesprochen deterministische Passagen in der Bibel gibt wie die Aussage des Paulus in Röm. 9,16 » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:8038-8040] (p. 223)
- Description proposée : Fürst speaks of a 'biblischer Kompatibilismus': the Hebrew Bible narrates a history steered by God in every detail, with passages of 'strikter Determinismus' and no word or theme 'Freiheit'; yet it is 'von vorne bis hinten' about liberation, and its commands presuppose that humans can decide. This biblical freedom is religious-'theokratisch', not a concept of human self-determination; responsibility surfaces 'in einem deterministischen Zusammenhang' as a 'Miteinander und Ineinander' of divine and human action. Ezekiel 18 is a 'Plädoyer für die Eigenverantwortung', collective ('nationale') yet also individual, plus the permanent possibility of conversion.
- Pages : 34-46 (quote p. 224)

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_early_christian_freedom_theory_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : early Christian freedom theory — Contends that early Christianity developed a distinctive 'freedom pathos' (Freiheitspathos) with freedom as 'cornerstone of Christian philosophy' (Eckpfeiler der christlichen Philosophie); Justin Martyr first systematically formulated 'freedom of decision' (Freiheit der Entscheidung) against pagan fatalism; Clement of Alexandria integrated this with philosophical paideia
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim (lines 5050-5067, p. 139, opening of ch. IV, pp. 139-186 per TOC). 'Freiheitspathos' (ch. IV title; p. 4) and 'Freiheit als Eckpfeiler der christlichen Philosophie' (heading p. 175; p. 177 'Eckpfeiler des christlichen Denkens') are verbatim. But (1) 'Justin first SYSTEMATICALLY formulated' contradicts Fürst: Justin 'proklamiert' the Freiheit der Entscheidung and grounds it 'sehr kurz theoretisch' (p. 160); the apologists 'legten noch keine entfalteten Theorien' (p. 175); systematic conception is reserved to Origen (p. 179; p. 186 'Als erster tat dies systematisch Origenes'). Fürst's 'first' claim is that Justin was the first in this Problemgeschichte to proclaim expressly the 'Freiheit der Entscheidung' (p. 156), and his target is the Stoic Schicksalsfügung / 'Chrysipps Kompatibilismus' (p. 153), with the main driver being anti-gnostic determinism (p. 180). (2) 'Clement integrated this with philosophical paideia' is not in the text: no 'Paideia'/'Bildung' claim in the Clement section (pp. 180-186); Fürst's point is Clement's opposition of Natur and Freiheit (οὐσία vs ἐξουσία), the ontologising of freedom in the forming of one's own nature, 'Grundstein' of a Christian Philosophie der Freiheit. (3) The node omits Fürst's central claim: the Christians were the first to call τὸ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν / τὸ αὐτεξούσιον 'frei' and to attribute freedom to every human as 'natürliche Ausstattung'.
  - « Damit hat Justin die Freiheit der Entscheidung proklamiert und diese sehr kurz theoretisch begründet. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:5801-5802] (p. 160)
  - « Die frühchristlichen Apologeten legten noch keine entfalteten Theorien über Freiheit und Verantwortung vor. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:6335-6336] (p. 175)
  - « Diese Wege zur Freiheit führten vom Determinismus über den Kompatibilismus in den Libertarismus, wie er von Origenes dann erstmals systematisch konzipiert wurde (Kap. V und VI). » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:6496-6499] (p. 179)
- Description proposée : Fürst: from the mid-2nd century Christian Platonists shared the Platonic critique of Stoic Kompatibilismus but, with unprecedented 'Vehemenz', called τὸ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν and τὸ αὐτεξούσιον 'frei' and ascribed freedom of self-determination to every human as natural endowment — a 'Freiheitspathos' that made freedom an 'Eckpfeiler' of Christian thought. Justin (Apol. I 43 f.) was the first expressly to proclaim the 'Freiheit der Entscheidung', with only a brief theoretical grounding; the apologists offered no developed theories. Clement laid the 'Grundstein' of a philosophy of freedom by opposing freedom (ἐξουσία) to fixed nature (οὐσία); Origen first systematised.
- Pages : 139-186

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_free_will_and_self_determinati_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and self-determination in antiquity — The study of free will/self-determination (Selbstbestimmung) has deep roots in ancient thought, with elements remarkably anticipating Kantian and Schillerian concepts; the work traces human self-determination from Homer to Origenes as a continuous intellectual development
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim in the Vorwort (lines 133-143, folio 'IX' at line 128). The node loses two qualifications. (1) Fürst explicitly denies any ancient analogy to Schiller's aesthetic transfer of freedom to natural objects ('keine Analogien im antiken Denken'); only the elements of the Kantian freedom concept Schiller drew on 'erinnern ... erstaunlich stark' at ancient ideas — so 'anticipating Kantian and Schillerian concepts' is too broad. (2) 'continuous intellectual development' is not Fürst's framing: he describes a 'Geschichte des Weges zur Freiheit' with its 'Höhepunkt' and 'entscheidenden Einschnitt' in Origen (pp. 1-2, 291), fed by 'viele Akteure und Traditionen' converging in a 'Mixtur' (pp. 3-4), and confesses that why the Freiheitspathos arose 'ist mir bislang noch nicht wirklich klar geworden' (p. 4). Also 'free will' is a label Fürst deliberately avoids (see node terminology).
  - « Dazu werden sich, soweit ich sehe, keine Analogien im antiken Denken finden lassen. Die Elemente des von ihm dafür herangezogenen Freiheitsbegriffs, die er den Schriften Kants entnahm, erinnern allerdings erstaunlich stark an Gedanken zur Freiheit, die schon in der Antike entwickelt wurden » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:137-142] (p. IX)
  - « Ich würde daher so weit gehen, den entscheidenden Einschnitt in der Geschichte des Freiheitsdenkens in das 3. Jahrhundert n. Chr. zu verlegen und ihn am christlichen Freiheitsphilosophen Origenes festzumachen » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:347-351] (p. 2)
  - « Warum es zu diesem Freiheitspathos kam, ist mir bislang noch nicht wirklich klar geworden. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:433-435] (p. 4)
- Description proposée : In the Vorwort Fürst notes that Schiller's aesthetic definition (freedom as 'reine Selbstbestimmung' transferred to natural objects) has, as far as he sees, no analogy in antiquity, whereas the elements of the Kantian freedom concept Schiller used 'erinnern erstaunlich stark' at ideas already developed in antiquity. The book tells the 'Geschichte des Weges zur Freiheit' from Homer to Origen, whose Freiheitsmetaphysik is its 'Höhepunkt' and the 'entscheidende Einschnitt'; many actors and traditions converged, and Fürst admits he cannot fully explain why the Christian 'Freiheitspathos' arose.
- Pages : VII-IX; 1-5

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_free_will_terminology_and_conc_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will terminology and concepts in antiquity — Argues that ancient concepts of freedom (eleutheria, autexousion) differ fundamentally from modern libertarian free will; ancient 'self-determination' (Selbstbestimmung) must be understood in its own historical contexts rather than anachronistically projected with modern categories
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim (lines 532-541, p. 9) but concerns προαίρεσις, not eleutheria/autexousion. Fürst's terminological point (pp. 7-16) is narrower: there was no concept of 'Wille' before Augustine, προαίρεσις is neither 'freie Wahl' nor 'Willenswahl', rendering τὸ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν as 'Wille/Freiheit/Willensfreiheit' is 'ausgesprochen irreführend', and he will stay close to the ancient terms; τὸ αὐτεξούσιον is a later technical term (1st c. BCE). He also separates political from ethical freedom and notes antiquity never drew the institutional consequence. He does NOT argue that ancient concepts 'differ fundamentally from modern libertarian free will' or reject modern categories: he uses Determinismus/Kompatibilismus/Libertarismus as analytic labels throughout (p. 104 'um moderne Begriffe zu gebrauchen'), calls Origen 'ein Libertarist' (p. 289) and says Kantian elements 'erinnern erstaunlich stark' at ancient ideas (p. IX). p. 291 (Ausklang) is about Origen's new level of debate, not anachronism.
  - « Aus diesem Grund werde ich den Begriff „Willen“ weitestgehend vermeiden und immer nah an den ursprünglichen Bedeutungen derjenigen Begriffe bleiben, die im antiken Diskurs für die Beschreibung menschlichen Entscheidens und Handelns verwendet wurden. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:508-511] (p. 8)
  - « Die häufig anzutreffende Wiedergabe mit „Wille“ oder „Freiheit“ oder „Willensfreiheit“ ist, wie sich zeigen wird, ausgeprochen irreführend, da es in den zugehörigen Konzepten weder um einen „Willen“ noch um „Freiheit“ geht. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:545-549] (pp. 9-10)
  - « Diese Einteilung der jüdischen religiösen Gruppen in – um moderne Begriffe zu gebrauchen – Deterministen (Essener), Libertaristen (Sadduzäer) und Kompatibilisten (Pharisäer) » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:3812-3815] (p. 104)
- Description proposée : Fürst's terminological caution: no concept of 'Wille' existed before Augustine, so he largely avoids the word and stays close to the ancient terms — προαίρεσις (Aristotle's 'überlegtes Streben', not 'freie Wahl'), τὸ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ('das, was an uns liegt', whose rendering as 'Wille/Freiheit/Willensfreiheit' is 'ausgeprochen irreführend'), and the later τὸ αὐτεξούσιον ('Selbstmächtigkeit'). His subject is freedom in the ethical, not political, sense. He nevertheless deploys the modern labels Determinismus, Kompatibilismus and Libertarismus as analytic categories throughout.
- Pages : 7-16

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_libertarian_critique_of_stoic__3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : libertarian critique of Stoic determinism — Traces a tradition of libertarian critique of Stoic compatibilism through Cicero's postulate of will-freedom (Postulat der Willensfreiheit), the Platonic school's 'undetermined decision' (Undeterminierte Entscheidung), and Alexander of Aphrodisias's 'freedom of choice' (Wahlfreiheit) — representing increasingly sophisticated libertarian positions
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim (lines 4421-4430, p. 121); the three sub-headings (Cicero p. 121, platonische Schultradition p. 126, Alexander p. 132) are verbatim and ch. III.3 = pp. 119-138. But Fürst's own label is 'Kritik am stoischen Kompatibilismus' from the Platonists and 'sämtlichen anderen philosophischen Schulen'; he never calls Cicero, the Platonists or Alexander 'libertarisch' — in the whole book that word is reserved for Josephus' Sadducees (with the caveat 'um moderne Begriffe zu gebrauchen', p. 104), the early Christians 'auf dem Weg zu einem Libertarismus ... zu dem man aber nicht wirklich gelangte' (p. 178), and Origen, who 'erstmals systematisch' conceived it (p. 179). 'Increasingly sophisticated' is likewise absent: Fürst reports Alexander's contingency argument as 'nicht stringent' and cites Sharples/Frede critically (p. 136).
  - « Die stoische Freiheit in Form einer inneren Unabhängigkeit, die in einen streng deterministischen Rahmen eingebettet blieb, war aber vielen zu wenig und hat daher Kritik von sämtlichen anderen philosophischen Schulen und besonders von den Platonikern auf sich gezogen. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:4426-4430] (p. 121)
  - « Stringent war dieses Argument Alexanders allerdings nicht, denn er unterschied nicht zwischen absoluter und bedingter Notwendigkeit wie die Stoiker » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:4967-4969] (p. 136)
  - « Diese Wege zur Freiheit führten vom Determinismus über den Kompatibilismus in den Libertarismus, wie er von Origenes dann erstmals systematisch konzipiert wurde » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:6496-6498] (p. 179)
- Description proposée : Fürst (ch. III.3, 'Kritik am stoischen Kompatibilismus') traces the imperial-age criticism of Chrysippus' Kompatibilismus: Cicero, first witness, drawing on Carneades, posits a 'Wille' as mental cause ('Postulat der Willensfreiheit'); the Platonic school tradition (Plutarch, ps.-Plutarch, Alcinous, Apuleius) insists that not everything falls under fate, leaving room for an 'undeterminierte Entscheidung'; Alexander of Aphrodisias defends 'Wahlfreiheit' between alternatives, an argument Fürst judges 'nicht stringent'. He does not label these positions 'libertarisch' nor rank them by sophistication.
- Pages : 119-138

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_origen_s_libertarian_compatibi_4` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's kompatibilistischer Libertarismus — Fürst coins 'kompatibilistischer Libertarismus' (compatibilist libertarianism) as a deliberately double-edged characterization of Origen. Insofar as Origen frames freedom within the teleological order of divine providence — on the ground of what Fürst calls biblical compatibilism — his concept of freedom is a compatibilism ('ist sein Freiheitskonzept ein Kompatibilismus'); yet he simultaneously propagates a libertarianism 'as strong as one can imagine' ('einen Libertarismus, wie man ihn sich stärker kaum vorstellen kann'), making freedom the ontolog
- Constat : All quoted German is verbatim: main quote lines 10363-10374 (p. 289); 'Wofür auch immer man eher votiert ...' lines 10375-10377 (p. 289; file capitalises 'Wofür'); 'Origenes konzipierte keinen Kausaldeterminismus ... das wäre das stoische Modell' lines 10349-10351 (p. 288); 'Gewebe' line 10352 (p. 288); εἱρμός retained but refilled with another meaning pp. 286-287; foreknowledge / providential pre-arrangement pp. 283-286; 'determinierte Aspekte der Wirklichkeit' and reliable regularities pp. 289-290; ch. V.3 pp. 217-239 (TOC). Section VI.4 = pp. 282-290. 'Coins' is acceptable with Fürst's own caveat (n. 99): he used the Junktur before reading that Christian List claims it. Minor nuance the node could add: on p. 290 Fürst goes one step beyond suspension — 'Das würde bedeuten, dass der Kompatibilismus das tragfähigste Konzept ist', the question being only which Kompatibilismus one holds; and his closing verdict is that Origen shifted the accents 'klar weg vom Determinismus hin zum Libertarismus' while this Libertarismus 'mit determinierten Aspekten der Wirklichkeit kompatibel blieb'.
  - « Insofern Origenes damit ein Freiheitsdenken im teleologischen Rahmen einer göttlichen Vorsehung entwarf, sich also auf dem Boden des biblischen Kompatibilismus bewegte, ist sein Freiheitskonzept ein Kompatibilismus. Allerdings propagierte er zugleich einen Libertarismus, wie man ihn sich stärker kaum vorstellen kann. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:10363-10368] (p. 289)
  - « Origenes konzipierte keinen Kausaldeterminismus, in den Handlungen, für die Menschen die Ursache sind, eingereiht werden – das wäre das stoische Modell. Er dachte vielmehr an ein Gewebe von miteinander zusammenhängenden Entscheidungen und Handlungen freier Wesen » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:10349-10353] (p. 288)
  - « Das würde bedeuten, dass der Kompatibilismus das tragfähigste Konzept ist und es eigentlich nur um die Frage geht, ob Freiheit und Determinismus einander ausschließen oder nicht und inwiefern sie vereinbar sind, d. h. welches Konzept von Kompatibilismus man vertritt. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:10405-10409] (p. 290)

### `scholarly_argument_f_rst_origen_s_metaphysics_of_freedo_5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's metaphysics of freedom — Argues Origen develops a 'theology of freedom' (Theologie der Freiheit) where God is conceived as freedom and movement itself; creation is 'world in movement' (Welt in Bewegung) of free rational creatures; apokatastasis (1 Cor 15:28 'God all in all') represents not determinist necessity but the teleological goal of freely achieved unity
- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim (lines 9346-9352, p. 260); headings 'Theologie der Freiheit' / 'Gott als Freiheit und Bewegung' (p. 259), 'Welt in Bewegung' (p. 248), 'Gott „alles in allem“' (p. 273) and ch. VI = pp. 247-290 all confirmed. God as 'ungezeugte Freiheit' and 'Eigenbewegung', origin of the whole moved world, is faithful. The 1 Cor 15:28 point is faithful in substance (perfection reached 'über die Freiheit', 'nicht durch Zwang und Gewalt', p. 280), but the node flattens a tension Fürst keeps open: the Telosformel raises for him 'das Problem ..., ob nicht auch dieses durch und durch libertarische Konzept am Ende in einen Determinismus mündet' (p. 283), reinforced by God's foreknowledge and pre-arrangement, which he resolves only via the foreknowledge-is-not-causation argument and the verdict 'Libertarist mit kompatibilistischen Neigungen'. Note also: Fürst does not use the word 'apokatastasis' in these pages (grep 0 hits); he speaks of 'Vollendung'/'Vollkommenheit' and the 'Telosformel'.
  - « Der Weg zu diesem Ziel, das Origenes wieder und wieder mit dieser Wendung aus dem Ersten Korintherbrief beschrieb, führt über die Freiheit. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:10046-10048] (p. 280)
  - « Mit der Telosformel, dass Gott „alles in allem“ ist und im Zustand der Vollendung die Freiheit von den Vernunftwesen so wie von Gott nur zum Guten gebraucht wird, taucht das Problem auf, ob nicht auch dieses durch und durch libertarische Konzept am Ende in einen Determinismus mündet. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:10137-10149] (pp. 282-283)
  - « Der Orientierungspunkt für die Freiheit des Menschen ist bei Origenes ein Gott, den er seinerseits als Freiheit bestimmte, und zwar als „ungezeugte Freiheit“ (libertas ingenita). » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:9317-9320] (p. 259)
- Description proposée : Fürst reads Origen's Freiheitsmetaphysik as a 'Theologie der Freiheit': God is 'ungezeugte Freiheit', pure 'Eigenbewegung', origin of the whole moved world; creation is a 'Welt in Bewegung' of self-moving rational beings. The telos 'Gott alles in allem' (1 Cor 15:28) is reached 'über die Freiheit', by persuasion, 'nicht durch Zwang und Gewalt'. Yet Fürst flags that this Telosformel, together with divine foreknowledge and pre-arrangement, raises the problem whether the 'durch und durch libertarische Konzept' finally issues in a Determinismus — a tension he settles only as 'Libertarist mit kompatibilistischen Neigungen'.
- Pages : 247-290 (esp. 259-261, 280-283)

- Constat : quote_verbatim is verbatim (lines 3210-3228, p. 86); heading 'Kausaldeterminismus und Eigenverantwortung: Der Kompatibilismus des Stoikers Chrysipp' confirmed (line 2745, p. 73; II.4 = pp. 73-91 per TOC).   - « Aus diesem Grund werde ich den Begriff „Willen“ weitestgehend vermeiden und immer nah an den ursprünglichen Bedeutungen derjenigen Begriffe bleiben, die im antiken Diskurs für die Beschreibung menschlichen Entscheidens und Handelns verwendet wurden. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:508-511] (p. 8)
  - « Worum es mir also im Folgenden geht, ist die Frage, welchen Handlungsspielraum ein individueller Mensch hat. » [Alfons Fürst - Wege zur Freiheit_ Menschliche Selbstbestimmung von Homer bis Origenes-Mohr Siebeck (2022).md:757-758] (p. 15)
- Description proposée : In 'Thematische Eingrenzungen' Fürst delimits his terms negatively: the Willensbegriff is discussed only to be excluded — antiquity before Augustine had no concept of will, so he avoids the word and stays close to the ancient terms ('Um einen Willen geht es mir im Folgenden also nicht'). His subject is 'das, was an uns liegt', human 'Selbstbestimmung' / 'Selbstmächtigkeit', and freedom in the ethical sense of an individual's Handlungsspielraum, as distinct from political freedom.
- Pages : 7-16
