### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_origen_s_theory_of_autonomy_an_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's theory of autonomy and free will — Origen's autonomy does not require alternative possibilities (the ability to do otherwise); rather, it requires that every human being have available the motivation to revise radically his or her character, whatever its current state. This modifies Stoic theories and raises problems about what motivates vicious characters to make moral progress.
- Constat : The thesis statement is exactly as described: Gibbons sides with Frede and Boys-Stones against the 'alternative possibilities' reading, then locates Origen's non-Stoic move in the requirement that every person have the motivation to revise character radically, which raises the worst-case motivation problem. quote_verbatim is verbatim in the file (l. 88-97, printed p. 674, strict-cite map_conf 0.98). Page range 673-675 covers the framing (p. 673), the thesis (p. 674) and the grace/absolute-cause restatement (p. 675). Only cosmetic issue: the node label is truncated mid-parenthesis.
  - « Here, I argue that, while Frede and Boys-Stones are right to call into question those interpretations of Origen that take him as committed to the view that human beings have the ability to act otherwise, his modifications to Stoic theories leave some problems with his account. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:88-91] (Gibbons 2016, p. 674)
  - « Origen has difficulties explaining how the char- acter of a superlatively vicious person can serve as the absolute cause of its own revision. This difficulty has larger implications for Origen’s understanding of the relationship between autonomy and grace. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:152-155] (Gibbons 2016, p. 675)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_assent_and_character_ground_embodied_autonomy` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons argues that Origen's account of embodied autonomy is substantially Stoic: rational beings are authors of their actions because proairesis can assent or dissent to the propositional content of representations. The same representation can be used differently by differently formed characters, so the agent's own character is the absolute cause of assent. This causal authorship does not entail that acting otherwise remains causally available under exactly the same character, psychological states, and circumstances; praise and blame attach to proairesis as the locus of autonomy, not to indet
- Constat : All elements are in the text: embodied autonomy 'more or less straightforwardly Stoic' (p. 677-678); rational beings as authors of their actions via the assent/dissent mechanism (p. 678); the two-ascetics example showing the agent is himself the 'absolute cause' (p. 678, quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 350-355); the definition of alternative possibilities as acting differently 'given the exact same character and psychological states and same set of circumstances' (n. 4, p. 674); proairesis as 'the locus of praise or blame' and 'the locus of human autonomy' (p. 679). Gibbons says 'absolute cause of his action' rather than 'of assent', a negligible shift. Page range 675-680 is broad; the material sits on pp. 677-679.
  - « This does not imply the ability to act otherwise, but that whether one assents or not will be a matter of one’s own character. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:350-351] (Gibbons 2016, p. 678)
  - « In these passages, προαίρεσις—our capacity to assent and dissent to our mental repre- sentations—is identified as the locus of human autonomy. That we choose to have the rational desires we have accounts for why we are agents rather than mere cosmic pawns. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:426-429] (Gibbons 2016, p. 679)
  - « Once human beings are embodied, how- ever, Origen’s account of autonomy in many senses appears to be more or less straightforwardly Stoic. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:301-302,329] (Gibbons 2016, pp. 677-678)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_universal_radical_character_revisability` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons identifies Origen's decisive departure from Epictetus in the scope of free will. Epictetus reserves an unconstrained will for the sage, whereas Origen holds that every rational being retains a free will because autonomy includes the capacity to revise radically which desires motivate it. Origen thereby broadens what is eph' hemin: previous choices and the character they formed cannot impose a ceiling on the degree of moral improvement or deterioration available to an agent.
- Constat : Content matches pp. 677, 679-680 and 686. Two attribution nuances worth keeping in mind though they do not change the verdict: (a) the claim that only the sage's προαίρεσις is unconstrained is reported as Frede's reading of Epictetus ('This, Frede claims', p. 677, n. 19); (b) the observation that for Origen all rational beings have a free will (De princ. 1.3.8) is credited to Frede ('Frede notes', p. 679, n. 29). Gibbons's own contribution is the explanation that this reflects Origen's 'stronger sense' of autonomy as the capacity to revise one's motivating desires, and that Origen 'seems to broaden' what is 'up to us' (p. 680). The 'deterioration' clause is supported by 'the superlatively virtuous vicious' (l. 486-487) and De princ. 3.1.5. quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 489-496, printed p. 680.
  - « Yet, Frede notes that for Origen, unlike Epictetus, it is not only the virtuous person who retains his or her free will; all rational beings, in fact, have a free will (De principiis 1.3.8).29 This difference reflects his departure from Epictetus in his stronger sense of taking the autonomy of the will to reside specifically in its capacity to revise what desires it is motivated by » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:429-433] (Gibbons 2016, p. 679)
  - « Yet, if our προαίρεσις is a will, it is not necessarily a free will, for such a will is only possessed by the sage. This, Frede claims, is because only the sage’s προαίρεσις is unconstrained » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:277-279] (Gibbons 2016, p. 677)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_pauline_anthropology_preserves_conflicting_motivations` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons argues that Origen's Pauline anthropology supplies the motivational architecture for universal revisability. The soul stands between spirit and flesh, so better spiritual desires and worse bodily desires remain available whatever a person's present character; proairesis can align itself with either. This availability resembles an intuition behind alternative-possibilities accounts, but it does not make choice indeterminate: a person authors character revision because the revision proceeds from that person's own present, previously defeated motivation.
- Constat : Matches pp. 681-683: Pauline division into spirit, soul and body (1 Thess. 5:23), the soul 'always has both better and worse desires available to it', the will (uoluntas) 'intermediate between flesh and spirit', the hedged concession that this 'perhaps shares something with intuitions about indeterminist freedom', and the denial of indeterminacy grounded in revision 'on the basis of one's own present (but hitherto-defeated) motivation'. The node's 'resembles' preserves Gibbons's 'perhaps'. One minor omission: Gibbons pairs this with the prelapsarian self-creation of character (Boys-Stones) as a second shared intuition (l. 654-656). quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 658-661, printed p. 683.
  - « The soul, whatever its present char- acter, therefore always has both better and worse desires available to it, a fact which pre- sents the occasion for the will to change whether it takes the pursuit of the appetitive desires or the pursuit of the normative good as its end. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:580-583] (Gibbons 2016, p. 682)
  - « his claim that autonomy requires the availability of conflicting motivations perhaps shares something with intuitions about indeterminist freedom as well. None of this, however, actually commits Origen to indeterminacy in human choice. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:656-659] (Gibbons 2016, p. 683)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_propatheia_discloses_character_for_self_judgment` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons argues that Origen transforms the Stoic theory of pre-passion by making propatheia diagnostically disclose character. A pre-passion is involuntary and therefore not itself blameworthy, but its severity indicates how strongly proairesis is inclined toward fleshly desires. Temptation can thus make a corrupted volitional disposition transparent to the agent, while repeated dissent weakens the accompanying affect. Gibbons also marks the limit of this mechanism: it does not explain why a person who sees his or her motivations clearly but is content with them would revise them.
- Constat : Matches pp. 683-685: Origen 'argued, as the Stoics did not, that pre-passions could render transparent the degree to which the soul has identified with the body'; pre-passion is ἀπροαίρετον and does not incur blame, but its severity indicates the inclination of προαίρεσις toward the flesh; the resolute ascetic is subject to 'increasingly weaker pre-passions'; and the explicitly stated limitation about the person 'clear-eyed about his or her motivations' who 'remains happy to leave them as such'. quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 706-711, printed p. 684.
  - « Yet, Origen argued, as the Stoics did not, that pre-passions could render trans- parent the degree to which the soul has identified with the body. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:678-679] (Gibbons 2016, p. 683)
  - « A limitation of this analysis of character revision is that it does not account for a person who is clear-eyed about his or her motivations and remains happy to leave them as such. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:765-767] (Gibbons 2016, p. 685)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_remedial_punishment_requires_self_interpretation` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons reads Origenian remedial punishment as efficacious only through the punished agent's self-interpretation. God arranges trials that bring hidden vice to light, but the suffering must be perceived as chastisement and used as an occasion to judge the orientation of one's own proairesis; otherwise it is merely random suffering. God therefore does not directly compel character revision: improvement requires the person's critical judgment of his or her own dispositional higher-order volitions.
- Constat : Matches pp. 685-687: 'remedial punishment is only efficacious when it is accompanied by the right self-interpretative activity'; the circumstances God arranges 'are occasions to bring one's own vices to light'; punishment must be interpreted as punishment or it is 'simply a random act of suffering'; improvement requires 'one's own critical judgement of one's dispositional higher-order volitions'; and 'God does not directly compel character revision' (p. 687). quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 885-895, printed p. 687.
  - « For Origen, remedial punishment is only efficacious when it is accompanied by the right self-interpretative activity. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:774-775] (Gibbons 2016, p. 685)
  - « Otherwise, such punishment is not punishment but simply a random act of suffering. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:891-892] (Gibbons 2016, p. 687)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_moral_progress_partly_constitutes_autonomy` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons distinguishes Origen from Epictetus by reversing the explanatory relation between autonomy and moral progress. For Epictetus, recognizing the autonomy of proairesis can motivate progress. For Origen, the capacity of even a superlatively vicious person to reflect on character and become superlatively virtuous is a necessary condition for assents and dissents to count as eph' hemin. The possibility of moral progress is therefore partially constitutive of autonomy, not merely a benefit made possible by it.
- Constat : Matches p. 686 (and the anticipation at p. 680, l. 483-484: 'for Epictetus, the possibility for moral progress is not itself understood as constitutive of our autonomy'). One attribution nuance: the Epictetus side of the contrast is explicitly 'Bobzien's interpretation of Epictetus' (l. 816; cf. l. 482 'as Bobzien observes'), which Gibbons adopts. quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 820-828, printed p. 686.
  - « One might contrast Origen’s understanding of the connection between autonomy and moral progress with Bobzien’s interpretation of Epictetus. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:815-816] (Gibbons 2016, p. 686)
  - « rather, for Origen the possibility for moral progress is itself partially consti- tutive of autonomy. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:827-828] (Gibbons 2016, p. 686)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_origenian_rehabilitation_more_agentive_than_olympiodorus` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons argues that Origen's physician analogy assigns rehabilitation a more agentive structure than Olympiodorus' parallel use of medical punishment. Both allow rehabilitation of the superlatively vicious, but Olympiodorus treats punishment principally as something done to the tyrant. Origen instead says that God delays intervention so moral failure becomes apparent to the sufferer; correction requires the sufferer to recognize punishment as punishment and judge proairesis as needing revision.
- Constat : Matches pp. 686-687: Olympiodorus speaks of remedial punishment 'as something that is done to one, without a sense that one must interpret one's suffering as punishment'; in his doctor analogy God waits for dispositions 'to set and become stable', whereas for Origen God waits 'so that human moral failings can become apparent to the person'; both agree the superlatively vicious can be rehabilitated, and Origen 'appears to place a greater emphasis on the role of human agency'. The node's 'more agentive structure' preserves this hedged comparative. quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 880-885, printed p. 687.
  - « In Olympiodorus’ discussions of just punishment imposed upon the tyrant, on the other hand, remedial punishment is spoken of as something that is done to one, without a sense that one must interpret one’s suffering as punishment if it is to count as punishment. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:848-851] (Gibbons 2016, p. 686)
  - « While Origen and Olympiodorus both agree that a superlatively vicious character can become rehabilitated, Origen appears to place a greater emphasis on the role of human agency in that rehabilitation than we find in Olympiodorus. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:895-898] (Gibbons 2016, p. 687)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_providence_makes_defeated_motivation_indefeasible` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons reconstructs Origen's account of providential motivation without alternative possibilities: God arranges historical circumstances suited to each person's character until a previously defeated spiritual desire becomes an indefeasible, necessitating motivation. The will is not directly compelled, because the response proceeds through the person's own desire and judgment, yet without divine intervention the person might never encounter circumstances capable of provoking the required reflection. Moral progress is therefore subject to significant situational qualifications.
- Constat : Matches p. 687 (quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 903-914) and the earlier statements at p. 675 (God 'brings about the circumstances such that an individual, given the character that he or she has, will be subject to the motiv- ations that necessitate the will's revision') and p. 685 (God can 'bring about certain situa- tions in which my defeated spiritual desires will become indefeasible'). The Judas case on pp. 688-689 applies the same schema ('necessitate the desired volitional response'). 'Significant situational qualifications' is Gibbons's own phrase.
  - « On this account, God does not directly compel the will, but brings about the circumstances such that an individual, given the character that he or she has, will be subject to the motiv- ations that necessitate the will’s revision. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:147-150] (Gibbons 2016, p. 675)
  - « in the absence of a the- ory of autonomy dependent on alternative possibilities, it is only given the right contexts and circumstances that our defeated spiritual motivations become indefeasible. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:912-914] (Gibbons 2016, p. 687)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_judas_does_not_establish_alternative_possibilities` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons reads Judas as the test case for Origen's non-indeterminist account. Judas retains contrary motivations: covetousness governs the betrayal, while Jesus' teaching leaves a remnant of virtue that later grounds repentance when the prediction and its aftermath expose his corrupted will. Origen's hypothetical-fate argument shows that foretelling does not compel betrayal, but does not establish that Judas could have done otherwise under the same conditions. An indeterminist reading would make Jesus' intervention capable of failing, a possibility Gibbons says Origen does not appear to enterta
- Constat : Matches pp. 688-689: Origen invokes the hypothetical-fate argument (C. Cels. 2.20) to claim that the foretelling 'did not compel Judas' action'; Gibbons, relying on Boys-Stones's analysis of that argument among the Platonists (n. 47), holds that 'just the fact that Origen invokes it does not provide evi- dence' for the ability-to-choose-otherwise reading; C. Cels. 2.11 supplies the two opposing motivations and the 'remnant ... of virtue'; and the indeterminist reading would entail that 'God's intervention' could fail, 'a possibility Origen does not seem to entertain' (with n. 48 crediting Boys-Stones for the underlying point about finite divine knowledge). quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 954-966, printed p. 688.
  - « yet, in light of Boys-Stones’s work rejecting this interpret- ation in the Platonic context, just the fact that Origen invokes it does not provide evi- dence for this interpretation. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:935-937] (Gibbons 2016, p. 688)
  - « If this is the case, it would be possible for God’s intervention to fail, a possibility Origen does not seem to entertain. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:965-966] (Gibbons 2016, p. 688)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_bootstrapping_problem_for_superlative_vice` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons formulates a bootstrapping problem for Origen's account of the superlatively vicious. Ordinary self-improvement can be causally scaffolded by an existing virtue or quasi-virtue, such as concern for another person or shame. If an agent has no relevant virtue or quasi-virtue, however, it is unclear how that agent's present character can be the determining and absolute cause of its own radical revision. Pauline anthropology guarantees that a defeated spiritual motivation is available, but does not explain why a maximally vicious will endorses it; the Stoics can avoid the problem by allowi
- Constat : Matches p. 689 (quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 1019-1031): the room-mate/kitchen example of scaffolding by 'another virtue or quasi-virtue (such as concern for others, or shame)'; the difficulty for a character with 'no rele- vant virtues or quasi-virtues'; bootstrapping as 'a more pressing problem for him than it is for the Stoics, who have more room to admit that certain characters are simply not capable of being motivated'; Pauline anthropology explains availability but not endorsement. The node's 'can avoid the problem' slightly firms up Gibbons's 'have more room to admit', but the comparative point is hers. 'Determining cause' (l. 1021) and 'absolute cause' (l. 153) are both Gibbons's terms.
  - « Yet, one might ask—what is it about the superlatively vicious person’s character that explains that revision? And if it cannot explain that revision, how can it be the determining cause of it? » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:1004-1006] (Gibbons 2016, p. 689)
  - « I think that this question of bootstrapping remains a more pressing problem for him than it is for the Stoics, who have more room to admit that certain characters are simply not capable of being motivated to develop morally. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:1024-1026] (Gibbons 2016, p. 689)

### `scholarly_argument_gibbons_grace_free_will_account_remains_incomplete` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gibbons concludes that Origen's theory of moral motivation remains incomplete at the point where universal restoration and Stoic causal authorship meet. To explain radical revision in the worst cases, one could make the agent's present motivations indeterminate causes or make God rather than the agent the absolute cause; Origen endorses neither option. His Stoic account lets providential interventions achieve their intended outcomes without straightforwardly making God the absolute cause of choice, but his non-Stoic insistence that every person retain motivation for radical revision prevents h
- Constat : Matches p. 690 (quote_verbatim verbatim at l. 1047-1055): the two options (indeterminate causes, or God as absolute cause) that Origen does not endorse but 'later Christian philosophers' would; the Stoic account allowing God's interactions to 'cannot fail to have the outcomes God intends, without rendering God the absolute cause of human choice'; the non-Stoic insistence on universally available motivation; 'the kinds of questions about the relationship between grace and free will with which authors like Augustine would later wrestle'; 'remains incomplete'. Gibbons adds that whether Origen himself recognized these problems 'is difficult to say' (l. 1067-1068, n. 50 on Heine), which the node does not contradict.
  - « Origen’s use of Stoic discussions of assent helps him develop an account of human autonomy in which God’s interactions with individual human beings cannot fail to have the outcomes God intends, without rendering God the absolute cause of human choice. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:1057-1060] (Gibbons 2016, p. 690)
  - « the theory of moral motivation he provides in defence of that commitment remains incomplete. The extent to which Origen himself recognized these problems is difficult to say. » [06_Patristique/Gibbons - 2016 - HUMAN AUTONOMY AND ITS LIMITS IN THE THOUGHT OF ORIGEN OF ALEXANDRIA.md:1066-1068] (Gibbons 2016, p. 690)

## irwin-meyer
Sources utilisées : /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md, /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Both sources present and native (MANIFEST.md lines 61 and 92). Irwin: page_map method header_detected, conf 0.95 (0.90 for p. 453); agentic_search --strict-cite accepts the file (pdf_overlap 1.00). Meyer: page_map method page_labels, conf 0.98; printed pages cross-checked against pdftotext footers for pp. 33-34, 84-85, 183-185 (page numbers are footers, so the page map's footer-aware assignment is correct). All eight quote_verbatim fields were found verbatim in the files (Irwin L205-208, 154-162, 112-121, 72-76, 327-337, 788-796, 844-847; Meyer L4551-4562, 2015-2028, 9608-9615, 9565-9575, 9545-9564). Recurrent problems in the Meyer lot: (1) descriptions written from general knowledge of the topic (cylinder analogy, 'compatibilist', 'intellectualist', 'apatheia', Aristotle 'rejecting' Socrates) rather than from Meyer, who explicitly declines to discuss the fate/determinism question and reads Aristotle as agreeing with Socrates on akrasia; (2) page ranges guessed from the table of contents (Plato node, akrasia node) rather than from the located passage. The Greek tokens in the Epicurus node are not in Meyer's file (she transliterates 'despotin'); they are attributed to a TLG extraction and were not checked here. Minor factual error in that node: 'declinatio' occurs once in the book (p. 161, Stoic ekklisis), though the swerve is indeed never discussed. Irwin lot is largely sound; the two OVERSIMPLIFIED verdicts concern conditionals/qualifications flattened (p. 455 'ought not to infer'; n. 8-9 that the two-view schema is Gauthier's and inadequate for Aristotle), and the DISTORTED verdict is limited to the added 'merely verbal'/'undecidable' diagnosis.

### `scholarly_argument_irwin_aquinas_s_interpretation_of_ar_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aquinas's interpretation of Aristotle on will — Aquinas makes two key claims: (1) Aristotle's 'wish' (boulesis) is really will, and (2) Aristotle's 'voluntary' (hekousion) concerns the role of will; Aquinas's exegesis is careful and sometimes can be improved to support his general interpretation better; a reasonable case can be made for some of Aquinas's claims about Aristotle having a concept of will

Second, he claims that Aristotle's remarks about 'voluntary' (hekousion) action are about the role of the will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:205-208] (Irwin 1992, p. 456)

### `scholarly_argument_irwin_augustine_s_concept_of_will_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Augustine's concept of will — Augustine developed a distinctive, pervasive concept of will as the unforced movement of the mind toward getting or keeping something; will is central to moral psychology, virtue as good use of free choice, and the ordering of love; this has no clear parallel in earlier Greek philosophers
- Constat : The Augustine material is accurately reported (will = 'unforced movement of the mind towards getting or keeping something', L131-132 p. 454; virtue = 'the good use of free choice', ordering of love, L147-149 p. 455; De Duab. Anim. 14 in n. 10). But the closing clause 'this has no clear parallel in earlier Greek philosophers' drops Irwin's qualification: what lacks earlier parallel is 'this pervasive and explicit appeal to the will', and Irwin immediately adds 'We ought not to infer, however, that the earlier philosophers have no concept of the will' (L154-157). As phrased, the node lets the absence of the appeal read as absence of the concept, the inference the article is written to block.
  - « Rejecting the view that we are passive in relation to our choices, he identifies the will with the unforced movement of the mind towards getting or keeping something. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:130-132] (Irwin 1992, p. 454)
  - « Virtue, as Augustine conceives it, is precisely the good use of free choice. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:147-148] (Irwin 1992, p. 455)
  - « If we compare these remarks of Augustine's about the will with Plato, or Aristotle, or the Stoics, we find no earlier parallels for this pervasive and explicit appeal to the will. We ought not to infer, however, that the earlier philosophers have no concept of the will. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:154-157] (Irwin 1992, p. 455)
- Description proposée : Irwin presents Augustine's moral psychology as the locus where a conception of the will as a third element beyond belief and desire is 'prominent': the will is 'the unforced movement of the mind towards getting or keeping something', the cause of both sin and turning to virtue; virtue is 'the good use of free choice' and the right ordering of love. Plato, Aristotle and the Stoics offer 'no earlier parallels for this pervasive and explicit appeal to the will' — but Irwin insists one 'ought not to infer' from this that they lack the concept.
- Pages : 454-455

  - « If these views fail to do justice to Greek moral psychology, then it may be reasonable to attribute a concept of the will to Greek philosophers. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:160-162] (Irwin 1992, p. 455)
  - « I have tried to show, however, that the rationalist interpretation is quite defensible as the best account of Aristotle's various remarks about desire and action. Aquinas provides us with a good argument for attributing a concept of the will to Aristotle. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:481-485] (Irwin 1992, p. 461)
  - « This is an inadequate account of Plato (in the Republic) and Aristotle, some of its inadequacies will be suggested by my later remarks about Aristotle. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Irwin-DiscoveredWill-1992.md:889-890] (Irwin 1992, p. 469 n. 9)
- Description proposée : Irwin frames the question conditionally: whether Greek philosophers have a concept of the will depends on whether the intellectualist and anti-intellectualist accounts 'provide an accurate account of what Greek philosophers have to offer'; if they 'fail to do justice to Greek moral psychology', it 'may be reasonable' to attribute the concept. He pursues the test through Aristotle, via Aquinas, and concludes that the rationalist reading of boulesis is 'quite defensible as the best account' and that a concept of the will must be attributed to Aristotle.

- Description proposée : Setting out the case of those who deny the Greeks a concept of the will ('derived from Gauthier', n. 8), Irwin distinguishes intellectualist theories (Socratic, Stoic: only belief about the better option affects choice) from the Platonic-Aristotelian divided soul. Both 'seem to treat agents as passive subjects of their desires' and to overlook 'our capacity to reflect on our desires and to choose between them'; if such reflective decision must be recognised, 'it seems' the will is a third element beyond belief and desire. ### `scholarly_argument_meyer_aristotle_on_prohairesis_and_v_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle on prohairesis and voluntary action — Aristotle's concept of prohairesis (deliberate choice) is central to his account of ethical excellence and moral responsibility; voluntary action requires that the origin of action be in the agent and that the agent know the particular circumstances of the action
- Constat : The voluntary-action half is faithful: origin in the agent (L3870-3873, p. 71) and knowledge of 'the particulars' (L3893-3895, p. 71). The prohairesis half is not Meyer's position. She leaves the word untranslated, glosses it as 'the general phenomenon of doing something for a reason' / 'motivation' (pp. 68-69), and states that 'Voluntariness rather than prohairesis is the central notion here' for praise and blame (p. 71). She also warns against reading 'moral responsibility' into Aristotle: praiseworthiness depends on the goodness of actions and motivations, not on 'whether we are responsible for them' (pp. 70-71), and Aristotle is 'quite innocent' of the modern assumption linking responsibility for character to responsibility for action (p. 86). So 'prohairesis is central to his account of ethical excellence and moral responsibility' inverts her emphasis. No strict-cite issue: file native, page_labels conf 0.98, footers checked against the PDF.
  - « Voluntariness rather than prohairesis is the central notion here even though, as Aristotle insists, prohairesis reveals character better than action does (1111b5–6). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:3858-3860] (Meyer 2008, p. 71)
  - « Prohairesis, which I will leave untranslated,48 captures the general phenomenon of doing something for a reason. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:3771-3772] (Meyer 2008, p. 69)
  - « First of all, a voluntary action must ‘originate’ in the agent (EN 1111a23; cf. 1110a15–17). By this Aristotle means nothing more mysterious than that it is the agent, rather than someone or something else, who moves his own bodily parts. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:3870-3873] (Meyer 2008, p. 71)
- Description proposée : Meyer leaves prohairesis untranslated: it 'captures the general phenomenon of doing something for a reason', is a determinate decision informed by deliberation, and 'rather than one's action best reveals one's character' (the goal for the sake of which one acts). For praise and blame, however, 'voluntariness rather than prohairesis is the central notion'. Aristotle's two criteria for the voluntary are that the action 'originate' in the agent (the agent moves his own bodily parts) and that the agent know 'the particulars' of the action; ignorance of the good does not make an action involuntary.
- Pages : 68-72

### `scholarly_argument_meyer_aristotle_on_responsibility_fo_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle on responsibility for character — Aristotle holds that we are responsible for our character states because we have control over the activities that form our habits, though this is complicated by the role of upbringing and external circumstances
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at L4551-4562 (p. 85; ligature 'ﬁrst' present in the file). The node's 'because we have control over the activities that form our habits' is not Meyer's formulation: her two steps are that character is formed by actions and that 'we know this when we are performing the character-forming actions', hence we 'voluntarily' become what we are. 'Complicated by the role of upbringing and external circumstances' flattens her specific position: the upbringing objection 'makes the mistake' of thinking the argument needs responsibility for knowing right and wrong; Aristotle starts from the 'much weaker assumption' that his well-raised audience does know it; he is not arguing that one is responsible 'no matter what the circumstances'; and the responsibility is 'at best partial' ('co-causes in a way', 1114b23). The discussion runs to p. 86.
  - « The objection, however, makes the mistake of supposing that Aristotle’s argument depends on the assumption that we are responsible for knowing what sorts of actions are unjust, intemperate, and so on. Aristotle, however, starts from a much weaker assumption: that we do in fact know this. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:4570-4573] (Meyer 2008, p. 85)
  - « He explicitly acknowledges that the sort of responsibility for character he argues for is at best partial: ‘we are co-causes in a way (sunaitioi pôs) of our characters’ (1114b23; cf. 1114b2). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:4614-4616] (Meyer 2008, p. 86)
- Description proposée : Meyer reads EN III 5 as a 'very simple' argument: we become just or unjust by our actions, and we know this while performing the character-forming actions, so we 'voluntarily become the sorts of people we are'. The modern objection from deprived upbringing misfires: Aristotle does not assume we are responsible for knowing right from wrong, only that his well-educated audience does know it; he is not claiming responsibility 'no matter what the circumstances'. The responsibility he defends is 'at best partial' ('co-causes in a way', 1114b23), and voluntariness interests him as an expression, not a source, of character.
- Pages : 85-86

### `scholarly_argument_meyer_aristotle_on_weakness_of_will__7` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle on weakness of will (akrasia) — Aristotle rejects the Socratic intellectualist thesis that virtue is simply knowledge, recognizing the phenomenon of akrasia (weakness of will) where agents act against their better judgment due to conflicting desires; this preserves a more robust role for non-rational motivation and complicates the relationship between knowledge and action
- Constat : No passage on pp. 72-79 discusses akrasia; the only akrasia treatment is at pp. 80-81 (L4353-4370), plus a mention at p. 71 (L3861). Meyer says the reverse of the node: Aristotle 'is self-consciously writing in the tradition of Plato's Socrates in the Protagoras', 'agrees with Plato's Socrates that knowing what one should do is impossible without properly cultivated desires', and in EN VII 1-3 'agrees with the Socrates of Plato's Protagoras' that the acratic and the encratic 'do not fully know'. What Aristotle rejects, on her account (p. 72), is not the premise that wrongdoing results from ignorance ('agrees whole-heartedly') but the Platonic inference that wrongdoing is therefore involuntary. Meyer does not use 'intellectualist' and does not frame a rejection of 'virtue is knowledge'.
  - « Aristotle agrees with Plato’s Socrates that knowing what one should do is impossible without properly cultivated desires and sensibilities. To think or know that something is good involves desiring and taking pleasure in it. This is why Aristotle, in his own discussion of acrasia (weakness of will) in EN VII 1–3 agrees with the Socrates of Plato’s Protagoras » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:4359-4364] (Meyer 2008, pp. 80-81)
  - « Aristotle agrees whole-heartedly with the premise of this reasoning, since he thinks any wrongdoing is a result of ethical ignorance (as we will see in more detail when we discuss the intellectual excellences). But he rejects the inference to the Platonic conclusion precisely because wrongdoing is a premier instance of blameworthy activity. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:3925-3929] (Meyer 2008, p. 72)
  - « the acratic person acts against his prohairesis, but his action still reveals a signiﬁcant weakness in his character (EN 1145b10–12; EE 1223a36–b3). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:3861-3862] (Meyer 2008, p. 71)
- Description proposée : On Meyer's reading Aristotle stands in the tradition of Plato's Socrates in the Protagoras: knowing what one should do is impossible without properly cultivated desires, so in EN VII 1-3 he agrees with Socrates that the acratic (weak-willed) and encratic (self-controlled) person, whose desires conflict with reason, 'do not fully know' that this is the right thing to do. Aristotle accepts the Platonic premise that wrongdoing results from ethical ignorance; what he rejects is the inference that wrongdoing is therefore involuntary, since wrongdoing is blameworthy.
- Pages : 71-72, 80-81

- Nœud actuel : Plato on knowledge, virtue, and free will — Plato's Socrates holds that virtue is knowledge and that no one does wrong willingly; this intellectualist position implies that moral failure is due to ignorance rather than weakness of will, though Plato complicates this in later dialogues
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at L2015-2028, which is pp. 33-34 (section 'Paideia: learning to be good', 32-35), not 14-20 or 35-38 ('Excellence and knowledge' at 14-17 is about care of the soul; 'Knowledge: understanding the good' at 35-38 is about knowledge vs. true belief and the forms). More seriously, 'Plato complicates this in later dialogues' contradicts Meyer: she presents 'the view that emerges across Plato's dialogues' as unified, cites the Laws for it, and says that 'Even in the Republic', despite the division of the psyche in Book IV, Socrates insists on the same point. She does not use 'intellectualist' or the formula 'virtue is knowledge' here; her formulation is that knowing (or believing) something good requires desiring it.
  - « On the view that emerges across Plato’s dialogues, knowing (or even believing) that something is good requires that one desire or take pleasure in it. Hence learning to take pleasure in something is part of learning that it is good.92 » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:2011-2014] (Meyer 2008, p. 33)
  - « There is no such thing, on this view, as knowingly doing wrong. We all want the good (Rep. 505e, Meno 78b). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:2015-2016] (Meyer 2008, p. 33)
  - « Even in the Republic, where the division of the psyche in Book IV explicitly allows that a person’s rational impulses can conﬂict with her emotions and desires (439b–440a), Socrates insists that those who are properly qualiﬁed to become rulers » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:2029-2033] (Meyer 2008, p. 34)
- Description proposée : Meyer: on 'the view that emerges across Plato's dialogues', knowing or even believing that something is good requires desiring or taking pleasure in it; hence 'there is no such thing, on this view, as knowingly doing wrong'. We all want the good, so bad action 'must be explained by ignorance' (Laws 860d-861a); apparent cases of acting against one's knowledge under emotion are, Socrates argues in the Protagoras, really cases of ignorance. Meyer treats this as constant, adding that 'even in the Republic', despite the tripartite psyche, Socrates insists on it; she does not describe later dialogues as complicating it.
- Pages : 33-34 (cf. 72)

### `scholarly_argument_meyer_stoic_determinism_and_moral_re_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic determinism and moral responsibility — The Stoics maintained determinism (fate) while preserving moral accountability through their distinction between internal assent and external causation; Chrysippus used the cylinder analogy to explain how fate is compatible with responsibility
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at L9565-9575, which is p. 184 (PDF footer checked), not 183. The quote is about the earlier Stoic notion of 'up to us' (action up to me if the impulse depends on my assent; praise and blame only for what is up to us) and Epictetus's divergence from it. Nothing in the book presents the cylinder analogy ('cylinder' has zero occurrences), and Meyer explicitly says the question whether fated actions can be up to us 'is an issue in their metaphysics and philosophy of nature – which is why we do not discuss that question in this volume' (p. 183); n. 1 merely points to Cicero On Fate 39-43, Gellius 7.2.6-13 and to Meyer 1994/1999, Bobzien 1998, Salles 2001 for the Stoic reply. 'Preserving moral accountability through their distinction between internal assent and external causation' loosely paraphrases n. 2 (internal vs external causes), but the thesis and the cylinder are attributed to a book that declines to treat them. p. 171 only refers the reader to Appendix 1.
  - « The question of whether our actions can be up to us if they are fated is an issue in their metaphysics and philosophy of nature – which is why we do not discuss that question in this volume.3 » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9528-9530] (Meyer 2008, p. 183)
  - « According to the earlier Stoics, an action is ‘up to me’ (roughly) if the impulse that causes it depends on my assent (Alexander, On Fate 181.13 /LS 62G1; cf. Cicero, On Fate 43). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9567-9569] (Meyer 2008, p. 184)
  - « 1 The criticism is articulated, along with the Stoic reply, at Cicero, On Fate 39–43 /LS 62C and Gellius 7.2.6–13 /LS 62D. For a discussion of the issue, and the merits of the Stoic response, see Meyer 1994 and 1999, Bobzien 1998 and Salles 2001. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9592-9595] (Meyer 2008, p. 184 n. 1)
- Description proposée : Meyer deliberately does not discuss whether fated actions can be 'up to us' — 'an issue in their metaphysics and philosophy of nature'. She reports only that the Stoics disputed with contemporaries whether their thesis of fate ('a determinist thesis') undermined what is up to us; that for the earlier Stoics an action is up to me if the impulse causing it depends on my assent, so the actions of wise and foolish alike are up to them and 'praise and blame are appropriate only for what is up to us' (Gellius 7.2.6-13); and (n. 2) that the ancient dispute concerns internal versus external causes. No cylinder analogy appears.
- Pages : 183-185 (Appendix 1, with nn. 1-2); cf. 171

### `scholarly_argument_meyer_stoic_freedom_and_what_is_up_t_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic freedom and what is 'up to us' — The Stoics developed a compatibilist account of freedom: true freedom consists in following nature/reason, and what is 'up to us' (eph' hēmin) is our assent to impressions rather than external outcomes; this is treated in a dedicated appendix as a complex topic in Stoic ethics
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at L9545-9564 (pp. 183-184). But 'compatibilist account of freedom' is imported vocabulary that contradicts the appendix's opening thesis: Stoic eleutheria, contrasted with slavery, must not be mistaken for the 'freedom' spoken of 'in contrast with determinism'; 'even here, the issue of determinism is not at stake'. Meyer uses 'compatibilism' only to name the modern issue she sets apart (n. 2). The node also merges two notions Meyer separates: the wise and the fool are 'equally up to them', yet only the wise are free; and 'the only thing up to us is the correct use of impressions' is Epictetus's view, which 'diverges from the earlier Stoic notion'. 'True freedom consists in following nature/reason' is a gloss; her formulation is 'neither hindered nor compelled' (p. 173) and eliminating the passions (p. 184).
  - « It is important not to mistake the freedom (eleutheria) that the Stoics contrast with slavery for another kind of ‘freedom’ which, in modern discussions, is often spoken of in contrast with determinism. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9515-9517] (Meyer 2008, p. 183)
  - « On their view, the actions of both the sage and the fool are equally ‘up to them’, but it is only the wise who are free. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9523-9524] (Meyer 2008, p. 183)
  - « In claiming that the only thing that is up to us is the correct use of impressions, Epictetus diverges from the earlier Stoic notion of ‘what is up to us’. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9565-9567] (Meyer 2008, p. 184)
- Description proposée : Meyer's Appendix 1 separates two notions: the freedom (eleutheria) the Stoics attribute to the sage, contrasted with slavery and not with determinism, and 'what is up to us' (eph' hemin), disputed in the fate debate. Sage and fool are 'equally up to them', but only the wise are free. Epictetus links the two by holding that the only thing up to us is 'the correct use of impressions' — assenting only to correct impulsive impressions and so being without passions — thereby diverging from the earlier Stoics, for whom an action is up to me if its impulse depends on my assent. Even in Epictetus 'the issue of determinism is not at stake'.
- Pages : 183-185; cf. 171-174

### `scholarly_argument_meyer_stoic_passions_and_freedom_5` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic passions and freedom — The Stoics held that passions are false judgments and that freedom consists in the absence of passion (apatheia); only the wise person is truly free because only they have correct judgments and are not subject to emotional disturbance
- Constat : The passions half is at pp. 159-161: 'A passion, in their view, is a judgment or belief. It is irrational in virtue of being false' (p. 160); 'a passion involves a false judgment about the good' (p. 161); 'the wise man will be free from passions' (p. 159). The freedom half is not on pp. 159-170 but at pp. 171-174 ('Only the wise are free': freedom = neither hindered nor compelled; 'At the root of the Stoic contention that only the sage is free is their equally notorious doctrine that the sage will be free from the passions', p. 174) and p. 184 ('eliminating all passions ... hence the claim that only the wise are free'). Substance is faithful; two small drifts: Meyer never uses 'apatheia' (she says 'free from passions', 'without passions'), and she makes freedom from passions the root/condition of the sage's freedom rather than saying freedom 'consists in' it; 'emotional disturbance' is a gloss for hindrance and compulsion.
  - « A passion, in their view, is a judgment or belief. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:8367] (Meyer 2008, p. 160)
  - « At the root of the Stoic contention that only the sage is free is their equally notorious doctrine that the sage will be free from the passions. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9080-9081] (Meyer 2008, p. 174)
  - « Only the person who in fact succeeds in assenting correctly, thereby eliminating all passions from her psyche, will have achieved this freedom – hence the claim that only the wise are free. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Susan Sauve Meyer - Ancient Ethics (2008).md:9562-9564] (Meyer 2008, p. 184)
- Description proposée : Meyer: for the Stoics a passion 'is a judgment or belief', irrational 'in virtue of being false' — a false judgment about the good — and also an impulse; virtue requires the extirpation of all passions ('the wise man will be free from passions'). Stoic freedom means being 'neither hindered nor compelled'; because passions (unlike 'good feelings') expose one to impediment and compulsion, the doctrine that the sage is free from passions lies 'at the root' of the paradox that 'only the wise are free'. In Epictetus, correct assent that eliminates the passions is what achieves this freedom.
- Pages : 159-161, 171-174, 183-184

### `scholarly_argument_natali_aristotle_s_importance_for_ant_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's importance for anti-determinism — Aristotle's reflection on cause, necessity, contingency of future events and human choices is of major importance and has been seriously underestimated by attempts to minimize the determinism/indeterminism debate
- Constat : Passage located verbatim in the Introduzione (printed p. 13, folio read in the page-preserving extraction and confirmed by pdftotext on the PDF). Two losses: (1) the target is not 'attempts' in general but one named 'tentativo recente (Bobzien)' to reduce the determinism/indeterminism debate to a short episode originating in the 2nd c. CE; (2) the editors do not flatly assert the underestimation, they say the objection 'si può avanzare' (can be raised) against that attempt. Attribution: the Introduzione is co-signed 'Carlo Natali e Stefano Maso' (SOMMARIO line 272); labelling it 'Natali' alone is incomplete, not wrong. Strict-cite gate refuses this file (page_map audit failed), so the citation form is [file:line] with the folio-verified printed page.
  - « Anche il tentativo recente (Bobzien) di ridurre il di-battito tra determinismo e indeterminismo ad un episodio piuttosto limitato nel tempo, originato nel II secolo d.C. e spentosi presto, tende a ridurre l’impor-tanza dello scontro tra determinismo causale e posizioni opposte ad esso. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:475-478] (printed p. 13)
  - « tale tentativo si può avanzare l’obiezione di avere seriamente sottostimato l’importanza della riflessione aristotelica sulla causa, la necessità e la contin-genza degli eventi futuri e delle scelte umane. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:479-481] (printed p. 13)
  - « Introduzione    Carlo Natali e Stefano Maso » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:272] (SOMMARIO, printed p. 7)
- Description proposée : Natali & Maso (Introduzione): against the recent attempt (Bobzien) to reduce the determinism/indeterminism debate to a rather short-lived episode originating in the 2nd century CE, which tends to diminish the importance of the clash between causal determinism and its opponents, the editors say the objection can be raised that this attempt has seriously underestimated the importance of Aristotle's reflection on cause, necessity and the contingency of future events and human choices.

- Constat : Quote verified verbatim on printed p. 243 (folio at line 11222). St. rep. 1057A) hence assents co-fated; conclusion 'completely determinist', 'only one compatible with the doctrine of everlasting recurrence' (Origen C. Cels. V 20); n. 93 on printed p. 237 (folio 238 at line 10981) withdraws Gourinat 1996 pp. 118-19 and Bobzien 1998 p. 295.

  - « In realtà allora, secondo la testimonianza di Cicerone, fu Epicuro, all’inizio del periodo ellenistico, a intro-durre il principio cruciale che fa dipendere la verità futura da cause preesistenti. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:11531-11533] (printed p. 249)
  - « Oltre la chiarificazione del ruolo decisivo di Epicuro, questa attribuzione ribadisce la tesi della Bobzien (pp. 75-81) che il nesso crisippeo fra verità futura e cause non era anche epicureo. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:11523-11525] (printed p. 248, n. 2)

### `scholarly_argument_sedley_2005_causal_birth_of_determinism` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's De Interpretatione 9 was unknown to the Hellenistic debate attested in Cicero's De fato: Cicero derides the denial of bivalence as 'miserevole imperizia logica' (Fat. 37-38) — inconceivable had he known Aristotle's chapter — and even lists Aristotle among the 'ancient' determinists (Fat. 39); the debate's eminent logician is instead Diodorus Cronus. The direct Aristotelian inference (antecedent truth → necessity → determinism) is absent: the only argument in play runs 'if already true, it already has causes; if it already has causes, it is predetermined' (a nine-step reconstruction
- Constat : All claims located: 'miserevole imperizia logica' at De fato 37-38 'inconcepibile se egli fosse consapevole del capitolo aristotelico' (p. 247); ch. 39 lists Aristotle among the 'antichi' determinists; 'Il logico eminente ... è piuttosto Diodoro Crono'; the direct inference (true → necessary → predetermined) 'non si trova nel De fato', only 'se è già vero, ha già cause; e se ha già cause, è già predeterminato'; nine-step reconstruction ending '9. Allora, esiste il fato.' (lines 11460-11492); immutability of future truth as 'un semplice fatto della logica dei tempi che, poiché non implica cause antecedenti, non può mai inibire o minacciare l'autonomia umana' (p. 251, chs 17-18 and 28); 'cause immutabili ed eterne' as the real danger; conclusion 'nacque in questo modo il determinismo' (p. 257). Chapter opens on p. 244 by folio.
  - « Invece, solo il seguente tipo d’argomento è di-scusso: se è già vero, ha già cause; e se ha già cause, è già predeterminato. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:11459-11460] (printed p. 247)
  - « Come egli spiega, essa è un semplice fatto della logica dei tempi che, poi-ché non implica cause antecedenti, non può mai inibire o minacciare l’autono-mia umana. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:11656-11658] (printed p. 251)
  - « Così quell’intuizione originale venne a generare per la prima volta il concetto d’un nesso causale comprensivo. In altri termini, nacque in questo modo il determinismo.3 » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:11917-11919] (printed p. 257)

### `scholarly_argument_maso_2005_ciceronian_clinamen_distortion` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Cicero was close to the Epicurean milieu from youth — his first teacher was the Epicurean Phaedrus (before 90 BCE, on Maso's re-dating of ad fam. 13.1.2), and 'omnes mihi Epicuri sententiae satis notae sunt' (fin. 1.16) — so his anti-Epicureanism stems neither from ignorance nor misunderstanding. In rendering the swerve, however, Cicero systematically omits the element that saves it from contradiction: where Lucretius (DRN 2.217-220, 259-260) distinguishes the fall, the deviation, and the indeterminable but real moment/place at which deviation OCCURS (incerto tempore ferme incertisque locis — 
- Constat : Author correctly identified (Stefano Maso). Quote verified verbatim on printed p. 271 (folio at line 12576). Checked: Phaedrus as first teacher, ad fam. 13.1.2, 'prima dell'87 ... a non più tardi del 90' (p. 258); fin. 1.16; Lucretius 2.217-220 and n. 17 on 2.259-60 ('incerto tempore ferme', occasionality); nat. d. 1.69 'directo deorsum feratur, declinare paululum' and the 'perennemente deviata, cioè curva' line; De fato 22 elachiston 'si riferisca a un corpusculum, non a una deviazione'; 'plaga, per Epicuro, è pura conseguenza del clinamen'; 'nessuno dei quattro scenari ricostruibili ... è di per sé adeguato'; the 'animi motum volontarium' Carneades had suggested, fat. 23; Carneades' fallacy of the same root ('Entrambi non sono cioè in grado di concepire l'«occasionalità» della deviazione'). Chapter opens on p. 258 (confirmed on the PDF).
  - « Si trattava evidentemente di contribuire anche in questa occasione alla pro-grammatica svalutazione dell’Epicureismo. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:12589-12590] (printed p. 271)
  - « Come si vede, nessuno dei quattro scenari ricostruibili attraverso l’interpre-tazione di Cicerone è di per sé adeguato: » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:12561-12562] (printed p. 270)
  - « Entrambi non sono cioè in grado di concepire l’«occasionalità» della deviazione quale dato appartenente » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:12549-12550] (printed p. 270)

### `scholarly_argument_fazzo_2005_alexander_antideterminism_context` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : De mixtione 12, 227.1-10 — reducing the major Stoic doctrines (mixture, soul, fate, providence, principles and god, unity and sympathy of the universe) to the 'stupefying' thesis of body passing through body — reads as an agenda of Alexander's anti-Stoic treatises (De mixtione, De anima, De fato, De providentia, De principiis). His antideterminism also reacts against the totalising astrological fatalism of Severan culture, while making real concessions to astral influence, grounded in an enlarged Aristotelian physics of celestial-sublunary interaction; fate is defined complementarily to to eph
- Constat : Author correctly identified (Silvia Fazzo). Quote verified verbatim on printed p. 274 (folio at line 12704; the file hyphenates 'astro-logico' across lines 12707-12708). Checked: De mixtione 227.1-10 as 'una specie di agenda della trattatistica antistoica' listing De mixtione, De anima, De fato, De providentia, De principiis (p. 276); fate 'in modo complementare rispetto a ciò che è in nostro potere', 'koinê prolêpsis' and 'quando gli uomini universalmente concordano, allora è la natura che parla' (p. 274); De fato 'caso atipico', 'scritto occasionale', 'aspetto autobiografico, che costituisce un unicum'; fate = 'natura individuale', providence = 'natura, in senso cosmico'; physis 'non riveste un ruolo analogamente significativo e centrale nei grandi commenti'; commentaries mention neither Stoic determinism nor 'to sôma dia sômatos chôrein' (p. 296); 'ipotesi non unicista bensì piuttosto (e perlomeno) dualista', 'ipotesi provvisoria', treatises to 'Alessandro il giovane', core of commentaries to 'Alessandro il vecchio' ('ipotesi attraente ma non verificabile') or to predecessors (pp. 297-298); Karakasu epigraph 2001, T. Aurelius Alexandros father and son. Minor nuance: Fazzo says statistical tools 'could' be applied and that this is not the place ('Non è questo il luogo'), so 'statistical style markers' overstates what she actually deploys; the multiple-solutions point is hers (p. 298).
  - « L’antideterminismo di Alessandro non si riduce in effetti a una polemica antistoica, ma comporta una forma di reazione anche contro le forme più estre-me e totalizzanti di quel fatalismo astro-logico che caratterizza la cultura romana all’epoca e alla corte dei Severi. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:12705-12708] (printed p. 274)
  - « Non menziona però né il determinismo stoico, né quella tesi della compenetrabilità dei corpi (to sôma dia sômatos chôrein) cui il De mixtione di Alessandro (227.1-10, cfr. su-pra) riduce tutte le loro dottrine fisiche. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:13781-13784] (printed p. 296)
  - « Ma il corpo centrale dei commenti si attribuirebbe alla tradizione preceden-te: o ad Alessandro il vecchio, nel caso dell’ipotesi, attraente ma non verificabi-le, di una trasmissione di padre in figlio; oppure, più in generale – in un modo che in questo momento non è possibile precisare – al magistero dei predecessori. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:13878-13881] (printed p. 298)

### `scholarly_argument_masi_2005_epicurus_partial_antideterminism` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Book XXV of On Nature, occasioned by the theory of psychological development, argues against Democritean mechanism to secure the physical conditions of Epicurus' ethico-pedagogical project. His solution distinguishes the causal contributions of nature, environment and ourselves, and locates a principle of self-determination in the mind's interpretative activity: the developed 'product' (apogegennēmenon), differing from its constituent atoms, acquires emergent, autonomous causal power capable of conditioning their behaviour; from a certain point the subject can correct the orientation of its ps
- Constat : Author correctly identified (Francesca Guadalupe Masi). No quote_verbatim in the node; all claims located: book XXV occasioned by 'sviluppo psicologico', arguing 'contro il meccanicismo democriteo' for the 'condizioni fisiche del suo progetto etico-pedagogico'; the mind as 'prodotto' acquiring 'un potere causale autonomo da quello dei suoi principi costitutivi ed in grado ... di condizionarne l'attività' (p. 189); 'da un certo momento in poi ... è in grado di determinarsi da sé' via 'un'attività interpretativa autonoma' conditioning 'l'impatto degli stimoli percettivi' (p. 190); T8 read with 'ejstin Masi; hJmi'n Sedley, Laursen' (n. 113), 'tre fondamentali tendenze di sviluppo psicologico dovuto alle loro differenti condizioni strutturali iniziali' (p. 194) against an 'assolutamente indeterminato' character at birth, matched with Seneca Ep. 52 3-6 (n. 115) on Epicurus' three types of men; 'soluzione parziale', Epicurus 'non discute la questione della concatenazione dei moti', 'riconoscere alla diavnoia un potere causale autonomo senza riuscire a giustificarlo pienamente da un punto di vista fisico' (pp. 196-197). 'Libertà senza clinamen' (Cronache Ercolanesi 36, 2006) announced in the asterisk note; and on p. 191 Masi states that for the mind/atoms relation Epicurus 'non abbia alcun bisogno di ricorrere al clinamen'. Pages: chapter opens on printed p. 168 by folio (PDF index 166 = section divider, 167 blank, 168 = opening without folio, 169 next), whereas the volume's SOMMARIO prints 167; ends p. 197.
  - « Alla luce delle indicazioni raccolte, quindi, ci pare che T8 possa essere letto nel modo seguente. Si danno da sempre, per gli uomini, tre fondamentali ten-denze di sviluppo psicologico dovuto alle loro differenti condizioni strutturali iniziali e alle loro capacità di crescita: » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:8861-8864] (printed p. 194)
  - « Così come ci pare evidente che, a tale scopo, il filosofo non abbia alcun bisogno di ricorrere al clinamen. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:8728-8729] (printed p. 191)
  - « Epicuro finisce di fatto per riconoscere alla diavnoia un potere causale autonomo senza riuscire a giustificarlo pienamente da un punto di vista fisico. » [La_Catena_Delle_Cause_Determinismo_E_Ant.md:9011-9013] (printed p. 197)

Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).pdf, 04_Littérature_secondaire/03_Paul/Maston_2010.summary.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Wells_2014.summary.md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Both works are in the fonds as native extractions (MANIFEST: native_pymupdf_page_preserving; Maston 227 pp., Wells 385 pp.) with PDFs alongside. Every quote_verbatim in both lots is present in the book file word for word, allowing only for line-break hyphenation and footnote superscripts; every quote_page in the lots is correct. Printed pages reported here were read from the running heads of the PDFs via pdftotext (page split on form feed), per the CLAUDE.md printed-page rule. Two infrastructure caveats: (1) agentic_search.py --strict-cite returns STRICT GATE FAILED for both files (AUDIT-FAIL: audit status not passed, min_tests) and its citation_page from page_map.json is systematically LOW — by 3 pages for Maston (reports p. 174 for the sentence on printed p. 177) and by 4 pages for Wells (reports p. 305 for printed p. 309); page_map/page_map_audit for these two files should be regenerated before any draft cites them through the tool. (2) The lots' page_range fields that look like line references ('Maston_2010.md:8723-8741', '12581-12650', '13087-13094') point to a filename that does not exist and to stale line numbers (offset ≈ +280-320 lines for Maston, ≈ +600 for Wells relative to the current .md); the same stale numbers sit in Maston_2010.summary.md and Wells_2014.summary.md, which should be refreshed. Substantive problems: two nodes (maston_paul_and_libertarian_free_will_0, wells_pauline_anthropology_and_free__0) attribute 'libertarian free will' claims to authors who do not use the term (Maston only in n. 169 on VanLandingham; Wells never); wells_new_creation_as_reconstitution_6 presents 'dual-agency' as Wells's term although it occurs only in the acknowledgements; wells_philo inverts Wells's emphasis; maston_predestination adds a 'deterministic' label Maston withholds; maston_moral_optimism turns 'adopts an alternative Jewish understanding' into 'presents a new model'; wells_faith_works drops Wells's concession about justification. The Maston summary's third 'key quotation' (line 633) is a paraphrase, not a sentence in the book (the real sentence is at line 736, p. 10). Greek in evidence quotes (Wells p. 225) is copied verbatim from the file; hyphenated line breaks in the .md were joined when quoting.

### `scholarly_argument_maston_ben_sira_and_human_agency_1` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Ben Sira and human agency — Ben Sira prioritizes human agency through the two-ways tradition derived from Deuteronomy 30.15-20; the individual human is the primary actor, with God's judgment as reaction to human action
- Constat : Description faithful: Maston roots Ben Sira's two-ways pattern in Deut 30.15-20, makes the individual the primary actor and reads judgment as God's re-action. quote_verbatim is present verbatim at book lines 8991-9003 and quote_page p. 175 is correct (PDF running head: first page of the Conclusion, next page = 176). But page_range 'Maston_2010.md:8723-8741' names a non-existent file with stale line numbers (lines 8723-8741 of the real file are Romans 8 material, p. 167).
  - « He employs the two-ways pattern to argue that the primary actor in the divine-human relationship is the individual human (Sir 15.14–17). The human possesses the moral capacity to obey and Torah observance leads to life and blessing. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:8992-8995] (Maston 2010, p. 175)
  - « Ben Sira constructs his understanding of the divine-human relationship on the two-ways model, which he finds in Deuteronomy 30.15–20. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:1188-1189] (Maston 2010, p. 16)
  - « Ben Sira’s view of divine and human agency prioritises human action throughout. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:9003-9004] (Maston 2010, p. 175)
- Description proposée : Ben Sira builds the divine-human relationship on the two-ways model he finds in Deuteronomy 30.15-20 (Sir 15.14-17): the individual human is the primary actor, possesses the moral capacity to obey, and Torah observance leads to life; the relationship is modelled on Creator-creation rather than on a covenant of pure grace; God's primary role is judge, and judgment is his re-action to what the human has done. Ben Sira's view 'prioritises human action throughout'.
- Pages : 175 (Conclusion recap, book lines 8991-9004); ch. 1 pp. 22-73, esp. 16, 24-27, 58

### `scholarly_argument_maston_christological_distinctiveness_5` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Christological distinctiveness of Paul's agency model — Unlike the Hodayot which attribute deliverance to pre-temporal election, Paul centres everything on the Christ-event: God condemned Sin in the flesh of his Son, and the Spirit is specifically the 'Spirit of Christ'
- Constat : Description faithful: Maston contrasts the Hodayot's pre-temporal decision with the Christ event as the point of divine intervention, and identifies the Spirit with the 'Spirit of Christ' (Rom 8.9). Quote verbatim at lines 7925-7932; quote_page p. 154 verified on the PDF running head. page_range 'Maston_2010.md:7860-7890' is a stale reference (those lines of the real file are the 'maverick Jew' paragraph, p. 153).
  - « Paul modifies the pattern of the divine-human relationship to account for this event (1) by making it, rather than the pre-temporal decision of God, the point at which God intervenes and (2) by redefining the divine identity around Christ. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:7925-7928] (Maston 2010, p. 154)
  - « in Christ. In so doing, he views the “Spirit of God” as the “Spirit of Christ,” for » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:8114] (Maston 2010, p. 158)
- Description proposée : Paul's alterations to the Hodayot-like pattern are 'the centre of Paul's thought': Christ's death is the decisive point at which God intervenes, in place of God's pre-temporal decision, and the divine identity is redefined around Christ — 'God condemned Sin in the flesh' of his Son (Rom 8.3); the 'Spirit of God' is understood as the 'Spirit of Christ' (Rom 8.9), the risen Christ present in believers.
- Pages : 154 (quote, lines 7925-7932); §B.1 'The Christological Modification' pp. 154-159, 'Spirit of Christ' p. 158

### `scholarly_argument_maston_divine_human_agency_in_second__0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : divine-human agency in Second Temple Judaism — Argues that Jewish texts from the Second Temple period exhibit diverse models of divine-human agency, not a single uniform pattern. Sirach represents a 'two-ways theology' where human obedience to Torah is central and humans have genuine capacity to obey, while the Hodayot from Qumran emphasizes radical divine initiative with human creaturely incapacity.
- Constat : Faithful. Maston's conclusion argues that Ben Sira and the Hodayot show Judaism 'was not monolithic', that covenantal nomism flattens the distinctions, and that both the 'legalistic' label and Sanders' grace-only claim fail. Quote verbatim at lines 9019-9032; quote_page p. 176 verified on the PDF running head ('176 Conclusion').
  - « The perspectives on divine and human agency put forth by Ben Sira and the Hodayot indicate that Judaism was not monolithic. Attempts to discover in these texts a single pattern, such as covenantal nomism, will inevitably flatten out the distinctions that are visible on the surface of the texts. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:9022-9026] (Maston 2010, p. 176)

### `scholarly_argument_maston_free_will_and_determinism_in_s_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in Sirach — Maintains that Sirach presents humans as genuinely free and capable of obeying Torah. Wisdom is available through human effort and endurance. Divine providence operates but does not negate human moral responsibility. The 'two ways' of life and death are real alternatives before the human agent.
- Constat : Faithful. Maston: humans 'created morally neutral', capable of choosing life or death and keeping the commandments (p. 58); Wisdom acquired through patience, discipline and endurance (§B.2.a 'Wisdom as the Result of Endurance', pp. 59-64); God's providence and foreknowledge do not negate human freedom nor determine human destiny (§B.2.b 'Freedom and Providence', pp. 64-66). Quote verbatim at lines 3100-3112; quote_page p. 58 verified on the PDF running head. page_range 'Chapter 1, 22-73' matches the table of contents.
  - « Humans are created morally neutral, and they are capable of choosing between life and death (v.17) and of keeping the commandments if they desire (v.15). » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:3100-3102] (Maston 2010, p. 58)
  - « does not ultimately determine human destiny, but neither does human freedom negate God’s foreknowledge and providence. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:3469-3470] (Maston 2010, p. 65)
  - « Just as hunting for animals requires patience and discipline and the pursuit of love takes endurance, one’s attempt to capture Wisdom requires the same. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:3268-3270] (Maston 2010, p. 62)

### `scholarly_argument_maston_hodayot_and_divine_agency_2` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Hodayot and divine agency — The Hodayot stress divine predestination, election, and the Spirit as exclusive source of human obedience; humans are sinful creatures ruled by perverse spirits, with deliverance through election, Spirit-gift, purification, and knowledge
- Constat : Description faithful in substance (pessimistic anthropology, creatures of clay ruled by perverse spirits, election prior to creation, spirit as the thread uniting all divine acts, knowledge and empowerment to obey). Quote verbatim at lines 7882-7893 (ch. 3's recap of the Hodayot); quote_page p. 153 verified on the PDF running head. page_range 'Maston_2010.md:8742-8755' is stale (those lines of the real file are p. 167). 'Exclusive source' should be read with Maston's caveat that divine action 'does not turn the human agent into a purely passive recipient' (p. 122).

The solution to this problem is divine intervention. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:7883-7886] (Maston 2010, p. 153)

- Description proposée : The Hodayot develop 'an extremely pessimistic anthropology': humans are sinful 'creatures of clay' ruled by their perverse spirits. The solution is divine intervention, whose constant thread is the divine spirit: God elects before creation, assigns the elect to righteous paths, gives his spirit, which liberates from perverse spirits, gives knowledge and empowers obedience to the Torah. The stress on divine action does not make the human 'a purely passive recipient'.
- Pages : 153 (quote, lines 7882-7893); ch. 2 pp. 75-122, conclusion p. 122

- Description proposée : Josephus' three accounts of the Jewish schools (J.W. 2.119-166; Ant. 13.171-173; 18.11-25) show that divine and human agency was discussed by ancient Jews and that different perspectives were held: plotted on a single line, Essenes and Sadducees sit at the extremes, Pharisees in the middle.
