### `scholarly_argument_maston_moral_responsibility_despite_d_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : moral responsibility despite deterministic frameworks — Acknowledges that even in more deterministic texts like the Hodayot, some form of human response remains meaningful within the predetermined structure. In Paul, the 'death' of the ego in Romans 7 does not eliminate human participation but reconfigures it as participation in Christ through the Spirit. The question is not 'free will vs. determinism' in modern philosophical terms but how different texts configure the relationship between divine and human action.
- Constat : The quoted paragraph (lines 9116-9129, p. 178 verified) supports the first sentence. The second and third sentences are the node's glosses: Maston does not describe the Romans 7 'death' as reconfigured 'participation in Christ' (his terms: under the law the human 'has no agency', in Christ the Spirit 'establishes human agency'); and his point that commentators 'impose a particular definition of "free will"' is made specifically about the Hodayot (p. 112), not as a general methodological claim about modern philosophical terms — he actually calls for a study correlating Paul with the philosophical debates on fate and free will (p. 179).
  - « Even in the case of the Hodayot and Paul, both of which stress the priority of divine action, the human agent is not portrayed as merely a passive recipient of grace. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:9120-9122] (Maston 2010, p. 178)
  - « These three proposals also impose a particular definition of “free will” onto the Hodayot. For these actions to be genuine human actions, it is implied, humans must be morally free and thus capable of determining their own direction. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:5791-5793] (Maston 2010, p. 112)
  - « The philosophical schools debated vigorously the relationship between fate and free will. A study of these texts that attempts to correlate them with Paul would help clarify Paul’s place within the Greco-Roman intellectual world. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:9181-9184] (Maston 2010, p. 179)
- Description proposée : All three texts make obedience necessary for salvation; in the Hodayot and Paul, which prioritise divine action, the human is 'not portrayed as merely a passive recipient of grace' — the Spirit transforms and empowers the human to obey, so that neither a view prioritising the human nor one eliminating the human is acceptable. Under the law the human 'has no agency'; in Christ the Spirit 'establishes human agency'. On the Hodayot, Maston holds that commentators who find a predestination/free-will contradiction 'impose a particular definition of "free will"' foreign to the hymnists.

### `scholarly_argument_maston_paul_s_critique_of_moral_optim_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Paul's critique of moral optimism and human agency in Romans 7-8 — Contends that Romans 7:7-25 critiques the 'two-ways theology' exemplified by Sirach—specifically the confidence that humans can obey Torah and thereby attain life. Romans 8:1-13 then presents a new model: obedience accomplished only through the Spirit and Christ's work, not human initiative. This represents a radical reconfiguration of divine-human agency.
- Constat : The critique of the two-ways theology and Sin's takeover are faithful (quote verbatim at lines 6492-6499, p. 126 verified; 'Moral Optimism' is §A.1.b p. 136; 'Sin's Takeover of Human Capacity' §A.2 p. 140). But 'presents a new model' and 'radical reconfiguration' lose Maston's central qualification: Paul 'adopts an alternative Jewish understanding' (a Hodayot-like pattern), so that his view 'fits squarely within the spectrum of Jewish views'. Maston explicitly rejects the picture of Paul as a maverick who 'radicalised divine action'.

Obedience is possible because of the Spirit of God. » [03_Paul/Divine and Human Agency in Second Temple Judaism and Paul_ A -- Jason Maston -- 1, 1, 2010 -- Mohr Siebeck -- 9783161516405 -- 0b228a0486e4e7293480e4698717d4e1 -- Anna’s Archive.md:6495-6498] (Maston 2010, p. 126)

- Description proposée : In Romans 7.7-25 Paul portrays the 'I' as the human agent of the two-ways pattern (Ben Sira's) and shows that tradition's 'powerlessness' to produce adequate obedience: Sin takes over human capacity, so that under the law 'the human has no agency'. In 8.1-13 Paul does not invent a new model but 'adopts an alternative Jewish understanding' — a Hodayot-like pattern in which God's deliverance and the imparted Spirit make obedience possible — modified christologically. Paul's view thus 'fits squarely within the spectrum of Jewish views'.

### `scholarly_argument_maston_predestination_and_human_incap_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : predestination and human incapacity in the Hodayot — Argues that the Hodayot present a radically different model where human creaturely existence entails fundamental weakness—both material and moral. This is not merely 'covenantal nomism' but a more deterministic framework.

- Description proposée : The Hodayot interlock two themes: humans are frail, sinful creatures (material and moral weakness, the problem of being a creature), and God delivers — deciding before creation who will be saved, purifying the elect and giving knowledge, all through God's spirit 'functioning as the agent of predestination'. ### `scholarly_argument_wells_new_creation_as_reconstitution_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : New creation as reconstitution of human agent — In Paul's theology, 'new creation' (2 Cor 5:17; Gal 6:15) represents the eschatological reconstitution of the human moral agent in Christ. Wells's terms are 'integration of grace and agency' (§11.2), 'dynamic integration' and 'coinherence'.

Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:217-218] (Wells 2014, p. x)

Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:13018-13019] (Wells 2014, p. 294)

The rebellious 'I' dies in co-crucifixion with Christ and is 'determined by God's new creation' (Gal 2:20; 6:14-15; 2 Cor 5:17). ### `scholarly_argument_wells_pauline_integration_of_grace_a_0` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Pauline integration of grace and agency — Paul does not present an absolute contrast between divine and human agency; rather, grace and human agency are integrated through the concept of heart transformation. The 'new creation' in Christ reconstitutes the moral agent, enabling genuine human agency that is simultaneously enabled by divine grace.
- Constat : No quote supplied. The description summarises the book's conclusions fairly ('dichotomies between divine and human agency are not unqualified', p. 303; new creation reconstitutes the moral agent, pp. 294-297), but pp. 1-11 do not assert them: there Wells sets out the Martyn/Engberg-Pedersen dispute, poses the dilemma as a question, and states his aim to investigate 'the precise relationship between grace, human transformation, and obedience'. The claims belong to chs. 8-11.
  - « To state the problem sharply: If in Paul there is a fundamental contrast between divine and human agency, what do we make of statements in which the two agencies seem to stand together in harmonious rather than antagonistic relation? » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:371-374] (Wells 2014, p. 2)
  - « By investigating the precise relationship between grace, human transformation, and obedience in Paul against the backdrop of other Jewish perspectives, I aim to move the debate another step beyond old and new perspective paradigms. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:603-608] (Wells 2014, p. 9)
  - « On the one hand, dichotomies between divine and human agency are not unqualified. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:13421-13422] (Wells 2014, p. 303)
- Description proposée : In the Introduction Wells frames the problem through Martyn (no autonomy, only enslavement or God-incited obedience) versus Engberg-Pedersen (no competitive concept of agency), asking what becomes of the harmonious statements if the contrast is fundamental, and of the Pauline antithesis if it is not; he aims to examine 'precisely how' grace and works coexist by tracing Paul's reading of the heart-transformation narratives (Deuteronomy 30, Jeremiah, Ezekiel). The conclusions — dichotomies 'not unqualified', the moral agent reconstituted by new creation, agencies coinhering — are argued in chs. 8-11.
- Pages : 1-11 (problem and method, lines 225-700); conclusions pp. 293-304

### `scholarly_argument_wells_philo_on_deuteronomy_and_heart_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Philo on Deuteronomy 30 and heart-circumcision — Philo interprets Deuteronomy 30 and the circumcision of the heart in ways that emphasize divine grace while maintaining human moral responsibility. His allegorical reading preserves both elements of the divine-human dynamic.
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 9176-9190; quote_page p. 205 verified on the PDF running head ('Philo 205'). The description inverts Wells's emphasis: for Wells it is 'unusual for [Philo] to deny humanity's moral competence', his occasional dismissals of human agency are 'primarily for philosophical reasons', and grace, though necessary for the ascent, is reserved for the receptive and not given to the unworthy (p. 206). 'Allegorical reading' is not Wells's characterisation in this conclusion. page_range 188-204 stops at the start of §7.4; the chapter runs to p. 208.
  - « It is unusual for him to deny humanity’s moral competence, and Deuteronomy 30:11–14 justifies his view. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:9180-9181] (Wells 2014, p. 205)
  - « Once we bracket this motive out, we see in fact that Philo is unwilling to dispense with human agency. Nevertheless, for all humanity’s virtue-seeking capacities, no one ascends to God without his enabling grace. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:9187-9190] (Wells 2014, p. 205)
  - « It would therefore be unreasonable for God to act contrary to this order by giving his grace to the unreceptive. » [02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/NTSupp 157 Kyle B. Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:9198-9199] (Wells 2014, p. 206)
- Description proposée : Philo's reading of Deuteronomy 30 serves his concern with the soul's ascent to God. It is 'unusual for him to deny humanity's moral competence', which Deut 30:11-14 warrants; the few who forfeit their potential fall under LXX Deut 29:4. Where Philo dismisses human agency he does so 'primarily for philosophical reasons' (divine causation, a rational universe), not from praise of God, conviction of sin or experience of grace; bracketing that motive, he is 'unwilling to dispense with human agency'. Yet no one ascends without God's 'enabling grace', reserved for the receptive.
- Pages : 188-208 (ch. 7); §7.4 Conclusion pp. 204-208; quote p. 205 (lines 9176-9190)

Wells-Grace and Agency in Paul and Second Temple Judaism_ Interpreting the Transformation of the Heart-Brill Academic Publishers (2014).md:12314-12316] (Wells 2014, p. 275)

## ramelli-2014
Sources utilisées : /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md, /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .pdf, /Users/romaingirardi/Desktop/DOCTORAT/Doctorat SHAL/04_Littérature_secondaire/05_Origene/Ramelli_2014.summary.md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Source found and native (extraction_method: native, 54 PDF pages = printed pp. 237-289, Philosophie antique 14, 2014). All ten quote_verbatim strings are genuinely in the .md; the audit turns on framing and pages. Two systemic issues. (1) page_map.json projects lines 214 and 1280-1363 one page too low for this file (line 214 → 240 whereas PDF page 6 carries folio 241; line 1280 → 263 whereas PDF page 29 carries folio '264 Ilaria Ramelli'; lines 1315-1338 → 264 whereas folio 265; line 1363 → 265 whereas folio 266); lines 137 and 1070 are projected correctly. All printed pages above were read off the running headers in the .md (lines 103, 166, 212, 1037, 1082, 1267, 1313, 1360, 1406, 2131) and cross-checked with pdftotext -layout on the PDF; a page_map re-audit for this file is advisable. (2) Six nodes cite the derivative Ramelli_2014.summary.md as source_file instead of the article; the summary's 'Relevance to Thesis' section imports 'libertarian', which Ramelli never uses, and is the origin of the distorted 'synthesis' node. Ramelli's own vocabulary: 'Stoic compatibilism between Fate and free will' (p. 264, her term), 'Stoic fatalistic determinism', 'Gnostic determinism', 'free will and responsibility', 'praise and blame', 'apokatastasis'. The Greek lemma for fate placed in the 'Moral responsibility and fate' node does not occur anywhere in the article; the krasis lemma occurs only inside Alexander's Greek in n. 70, Ramelli's own word being the mixis term at line 1049.

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_alexander_of_aphrodisias_as_so_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander of Aphrodisias as source for Origen's philosophy — Alexander of Aphrodisias was very probably a philosophical source for Origen of Alexandria, with parallels across multiple domains including anti-determinism, De Principiis structure, technical terminology, and metaphysical concepts
- Constat : quote_verbatim is the article's abstract, verbatim at lines 105-121 (printed p. 238, folio '238 Ilaria Ramelli' read on PDF page 3). The description reproduces Ramelli's own list of clues (structure of Peri Archon, technical expression, hypostasis, mixture, hyle/eidos, refutation of Stoic determinism) and her modal 'very probable'. Page range 237-289 is the article's printed range.
  - « It is very probable that Origen knew Alexander of Aphrodisias’ works, which were read at the school of Plotinus, and drew inspiration from them. Many clues support my hypo-thesis. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:105-108] (p. 238)
  - « Both the presentation and the refutation of Stoic determinism are very similar in Alexander and Origen. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:120-121] (p. 238)

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_alexander_s_concept_of_to_eph__5` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander's concept of to eph' hemin — Alexander maintained that Stoic compatibilism between Fate and free will was unsuccessful because it excluded to eph' hemin (what depends on us) and undermined moral responsibility; this same criticism was applied by Origen against Gnostic determinism
- Constat : Quote located verbatim at lines 1280-1283; printed p. 264 (PDF page 29 carries the folio '264 Ilaria Ramelli'; note the project page_map projects this line to p. 263, which is off by one). 'Compatibilism' is Ramelli's own word here, so it may be reported as hers. The description tracks the passage; one qualification Ramelli adds in n. 83 is not in the node: she reports Strobel 2010's conclusion that Alexander's notion of eph' hemin 'is in the end similar to that of the Stoics, whom he criticised'. Worth keeping alongside the node since the topic is 'Alexander's concept of to eph' hemin'.
  - « Alexander deems Stoic compatibilism between Fate and free will unsuccessful, untenable,82 and dangerous for moral life, in that it excludes responsibility (DF chs. 7-21). It rules out τὸ ἐφ᾿ἡμῖν.83 This is in fact the same criticism that Origen levelled against Gnostic determinism. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1280-1283] (p. 264)
  - « His conclusion is that Alexander’s notion of ἐφ᾿ἡμῖν is in the end similar to that of the Stoics, whom he criticised. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1304-1305] (p. 264, n. 83)

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_anti_determinism_and_free_will_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Anti-determinism and free will — Both Alexander and Origen refuted Stoic determinism using very similar arguments, particularly emphasizing praise/blame and the efficacy of petitionary prayer; Alexander deemed Stoic compatibilism between Fate and free will untenable and dangerous for moral life because it excluded responsibility and ruled out to eph' hemin
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 1325-1331, printed p. 265 (PDF page 30, folio 265; page_map projects p. 264, off by one). Ramelli's wording is 'the same' arguments (praise/blame, prayer of request and thanksgiving), and she frames Alexander's treatment as 'very likely to be in the background' of Origen's refutation.
  - « The very arguments based on the importance of praise and blame and the prayer of request and thanksgiving are the same in Origen (the author of a De oratione) and Alexander (see esp. DF 20). » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1329-1331] (p. 265)
  - « Indeed, Origen’s attack on Stoic determi-nism has much in common with Alexander’s. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1323-1324] (p. 265)

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_free_will_as_central_to_origen_4` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 1368-1373; the CC IV, 67-68 translation follows at lines 1377-1384. Printed p. 266 (PDF page 31, folio '266 Ilaria Ramelli'; page_map projects p. 265, off by one). 'Fundamentally motivated' is a fair gloss of 'really the gist of Origen's polemic'. Ramelli also stresses that CC IV, 12 and 67-68 are 'absolutely reliable passages preserved in their original Greek'.

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_matter_and_evil_origen_s_align_4` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Matter and evil: Origen's alignment with Alexander against Plotinus — Alexander refused to connect matter with evil; Origen similarly refused this connection, making Alexander closer to Origen than to Plotinus on this point

Printed pp. 240-241: PDF page 6 carries the folio 'Origen and Alexander of Aphrodisias 241' with the quoted line (page_map projects p. 240 for line 214, off by one). The description renders Ramelli's remark correctly, including the contrast with Plotinus taken from Lavaud 2008. Her formulation is a personal judgement ('seems to me'), which the node could preserve.

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_moral_responsibility_and_fate_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Moral responsibility and fate — Both Alexander and Origen engaged with the topic of fate (εἱμαρμένη/fatum) as a subject worthy of philosophical and even imperial attention; Alexander's De Fato was part of a broader philosophical debate on fate and determinism
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 137-143, printed p. 239 (PDF page 4, the page between folios 238 and 240; it carries no running header). But the description misreads the passage: the two authors Ramelli pairs as dedicating a De Fato to an emperor are Alexander and Bardaisan of Edessa, not Origen. Origen does not appear in the quoted passage at all. The Greek term in the description does not occur in the article (rg for the heimarmene lemma returns nothing in the .md). 'Alexander's De Fato was part of a broader debate' is supported by p. 264 ('He entered an ancient debate – very lively in his day'), but Ramelli adds there that Alexander 'never mentions the Stoics' in De Fato and develops 'an Aristotelian notion of fate' that Frede calls 'quite a weak conception of fate'.
  - « It is worth noticing that, likewise, Bardaisan, the Christian Middle Platonist from Edessa, dedi-cated his own De Fato to an «Antoninus» who may have been either Mar-cus Aurelius or Caracalla himself.2 The topic was being hotly debated in philosophical circles and was deemed worthy of imperial dedications. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:139-143] (p. 239)
  - « Alexander tackled the question of determinism in De Fato – which, albeit he never mentions the Stoics therein, is largely considered to be devoted to a refutation of Stoic determinism,80 » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1270-1272] (p. 264)
  - « He entered an ancient debate – very lively in his day, as Bardaisan’s own De Fato testifies – by developing an Aristotelian notion of fate. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1275-1277] (p. 264)
- Description proposée : Ramelli dates Alexander's De Fato by its dedication to Septimius Severus and Caracalla (198-209 CE) and notes that Bardaisan of Edessa likewise dedicated a De Fato to an 'Antoninus': the topic of fate 'was being hotly debated in philosophical circles and was deemed worthy of imperial dedications'. Alexander entered this debate by 'developing an Aristotelian notion of fate' residing in the natural constitution of things — a 'weak conception of fate' (Frede). Origen is not among the imperial dedicators; Ramelli connects him to the debate only through his refutation of Stoic and Gnostic determinism.
- Pages : 239, 264

- Nœud actuel : Origen's distinction between Christian and Stoic apokatastasis — Origen distinguished his Christian doctrine of apokatastasis from Stoic deterministic apokatastasis in two key ways: (a) the Stoics posited infinite aeons while Origen postulated an end to all aeons; (b) the Stoics held identical repetition while Origen held that each aeon differs due to rational creatures' free choices
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 1361-1368; the two differences are laid out from p. 265 ('There are two main differences') into p. 266 (PDF pages 30-31; page_map again projects one page too low). Description accurate. One small qualification is dropped: Ramelli writes that on the Stoic view the same people behave 'in the very same way (or almost)', with n. 90 on Gourinat 2002 — the 'or almost' is her concession to the Stoic allowance for minimal differences (cf. p. 267 on Dion's warts).

The first is that the Stoics posited an infinite series of aeons, whereas Origen postulated an end » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1340-1342] (p. 265)

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_origen_s_modification_of_stoic_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's modification of Stoic mixture doctrine in relation to Alexander's critique — Origen modified the Stoic doctrine of mixture (κρᾶσις) in a direction that approaches Alexander's critique of that doctrine
- Constat : Quote verbatim at lines 1070-1074, printed p. 259 (folio at line 1037; section continues on p. 260, folio at line 1082). Two losses. (1) Ramelli hedges the whole point at every step — 'makes me surmise that Origen might have had Alexander of Aphrodisias in mind', 'This suggests, once again, an influence', 'Origen is likely to have had Alexander's treatment in mind', abstract 'Origen seems to have modified' — whereas the node states a flat 'Origen modified'. (2) The Greek term in the description is not the one Ramelli uses in this passage: she writes the Stoic image illustrates mixture with the term at line 1049 (μῖξις); the krasis lemma appears only inside the Greek of n. 70 quoting Alexander, never in her own prose. The substance (each component keeps its own qualities for the Stoics; in Origen fire prevails over iron and the divine light over the soul, as in Alexander's De mixt.) is otherwise correct.
  - « One further, related point makes me surmise that Origen might have had Alexander of Aphrodisias in mind, again in connection with a core theological theory of Origen’s. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1041-1043] (p. 259)
  - « This image of iron and fire was already adopted by ancient Stoics, especially Chrysippus, to illustrate mixture (μῖξις); it is found in SVF II, 471 and 473 » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1047-1049] (p. 259)
  - « Origen is likely to have had Alexander’s treatment in mind when he described the union of natures – the soul and the divine Logos – in the one Hypostasis of Christ » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1093-1095] (p. 260)
- Description proposée : Ramelli surmises that Origen 'might have had Alexander of Aphrodisias in mind' when, in Princ. II, 6, 3 and 6, he describes the union of soul and Logos in Christ through the Stoic image of iron and fire. For the Stoics (SVF II, 471 and 473) each component in the mixture keeps its own substance and qualities; in Origen fire prevails over iron and the divine light over the soul. This 'suggests, once again, an influence from Alexander of Aphrodisias and Aristotle', since Alexander's De mixtione criticised Chrysippus's doctrine with the very same fire-and-iron example. Origen 'seems to have modified' the Stoic doctrine.
- Pages : 259-260

### `scholarly_argument_ramelli_origen_s_synthesis_of_philosop_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's synthesis of philosophical anti-determinism with Christian theology — Origen synthesized Platonic-Aristotelian anti-determinism (mediated through Alexander of Aphrodisias) with Christian theology, producing a distinctively Christian doctrine of free will grounded in moral responsibility and oriented toward apokatastasis
- Constat : The quote_verbatim is real (lines 1331-1339, printed p. 265, PDF page 30), but the description is not Ramelli's claim: it is the 'H3 relevance' gloss of the derivative file Ramelli_2014.summary.md (which also uses 'libertarian', a term absent from the article). 'Synthesized', 'Platonic-Aristotelian anti-determinism', 'mediated through Alexander' and 'a distinctively Christian doctrine of free will... oriented toward apokatastasis' do not occur in the article (rg for synthes/Platonic-Aristotelian/mediat finds only 'through mediators' on p. 283, about Aristotle). Ramelli's own claims are more modest and probabilistic: Alexander's treatment is 'very likely to be in the background' of Origen's refutation; the presentation and refutation of Stoic determinism are 'very similar'; Origen wanted his Christian apokatastasis to be seen as not implying determinism; and, in conclusion, Alexander 'is certainly not... the main inspirer of Origen's thought as a whole', though likely 'some significant influence'.
  - « Alexander’s treatment is very likely to be in the background of Origen’s refutation of determinism » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1315-1316] (p. 265)
  - « He wanted to make it clear that his own Christian doctrine of apokatastasis was quite different from the Stoic one, and did not imply determinism. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:1337-1339] (p. 265)
  - « It is certainly not the case that Alexander is the main inspirer of Origen’s thought as a whole, but, in the light of the present investigation, it is likely that he did exert some signi-ficant influence on Origen. » [05_Origene/Ramelli - 2014 - Alexander of Aphrodisias  a source of Origen’s philosophy .md:2154-2157] (p. 283)
- Description proposée : Ramelli holds that Alexander's De Fato is 'very likely to be in the background' of Origen's refutation of determinism: Origen refuted both Gnostic (Valentinian) and Stoic determinism, 'just like Alexander', within his discussion of the Stoic succession of aeons (CC IV, 12; IV, 67-68; V, 20; Princ. II, 3), and 'wanted to make it clear that his own Christian doctrine of apokatastasis was quite different from the Stoic one, and did not imply determinism'. She concludes that Alexander is 'certainly not' the main inspirer of Origen's thought as a whole, but likely exerted 'some significant influence'.
- Pages : 264-267, 283

### `scholarly_argument_telfer_basil_the_great_on_autexousia_5` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Basil the Great on autexousia — Basil the Great identifies autexousia with 'the image of God' in humanity, rather than identifying the image of God with reason
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 203-207, printed p. 126 (page_map conf 0.95, agentic_search --strict-cite OK). Telfer reports Basil (n. 3: In ps. xlviii. 8) as calling autexousia 'the image of God' rather than 'the concomitant gift of reason', and frames this ('therefore') as the outcome of the fourth-century view of autexousia as prerequisite of salvation by divinization; the image is 'only potentially like' its archetype and autexousia 'makes man potentially like God' (p. 127). The description keeps the contrast intact. verified_reference 'p.126-127' is acceptable (sentence on 126, gloss continues on 127).
  - « In the optimism of that age, autexousia, in spite of its acknowledged possibilities of ill, came to be regarded chiefly as the prerequisite of the process of salvation by divinization. Basil the Great3, therefore, calls autexousia 'the image of God', rather than the concomitant gift of reason. » [04_Apologistes_Justin/TELFER, W. (1957). AUTEXOUSIA.md:203-207] (Telfer 1957, p. 126)
  - « An image can be seen in something only potentially like, and in many ways unlike, its archetype, and autexousia makes man potentially like God. » [04_Apologistes_Justin/TELFER, W. (1957). AUTEXOUSIA.md:207-212] (Telfer 1957, pp. 126-127)

### `scholarly_argument_telfer_christian_autexousia_and_jewis_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Christian autexousia and Jewish angelology — In Justin, autexousia applies equally to angels and humans — Telfer's own compressed Greek gloss «γένεα αὐτεξούσια» (JTS n.s. 8, 1957, p. 124), not a verbatim phrase of Justin — creating a parity between humans and angels that distinguishes Eastern from Western thinking on free will
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 92-96, p. 124 (the Greek in the .md and in the PDF text layer is garbled: 'yevea avre£ovola'; the polytonic form in the node cannot be checked against the file, only against the page image). The description drops the causal link Telfer states: the Justinian doctrine 'has relations with Jewish angelology, and because of this was committed to a notion of parity' — parity is a commitment arising from the angelological relation, not something 'created' by applying the word to both races. It also turns Telfer's hedged 'may be said to have stepped off with different feet in East and West' into a flat 'distinguishes Eastern from Western thinking'. The Dialogue passage (praise/blame apply to angels and men only because they can turn either way) is the second Justin witness Telfer adduces. Note: the corpus text of 2 Apol. 7 reads a singular 'genos' construction, confirming the node's remark that Telfer's parenthesis is his own compressed gloss.
  - « In this Second Apology, Justin says that God made the race of men and the race of angels alike self-determining (yevea avre£ovola), and looked forward to crowning their right use of this prerogative with the gift of incorruptibility. He does not, however, revoke the prerogative, even if used only to earn punishment. » [04_Apologistes_Justin/TELFER, W. (1957). AUTEXOUSIA.md:92-96] (Telfer 1957, p. 124)
  - « Thus the Christian doctrine of human autexousia, as it appears in Justin, has relations with Jewish angelology, and because of this was committed to a notion of parity, in respect to autexousia, between men and angels. » [04_Apologistes_Justin/TELFER, W. (1957). AUTEXOUSIA.md:103-106] (Telfer 1957, p. 124)
- Description proposée : Telfer reads Justin (Second Apology; Dialogue) as making God create the race of men and the race of angels alike self-determining, a prerogative not revoked even when used only to earn punishment. Because the Christian doctrine of human autexousia, as it appears in Justin, 'has relations with Jewish angelology', it was 'committed to a notion of parity, in respect to autexousia, between men and angels'; Latin thought on liberum arbitrium, starting from human psychology, was never so committed, so that Christian thought on free will 'may be said' to have stepped off with different feet in East and West.
- Pages : 124

### `scholarly_argument_telfer_development_of_autexousia_conc_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 65-72, p. 124. Two vocabulary drifts, not distortions: Telfer calls the noun an 'adjectival noun' (not 'abstract noun'), and says the adjective had 'taken its place in the ethical vocabulary, as meaning able to take moral decisions' (not 'technical term for moral self-determination'). The Greek forms in the node (treptotes/atreptotes) are garbled in the .md ('TpeTTTonjs', 'drpe-TTTOTr/s') and unverifiable from the file. page_range 123-126 correct.

AUTEXOUSIA.md:67-72] (Telfer 1957, p. 124)

- Nœud actuel : origin of autexousia terminology — The adjective αὐτεξούσιος is attested as early as Chrysippus (3rd century BC), but the substantive noun τὸ αὐτεξούσιον does not appear until the second Christian century; Justin Martyr introduces it as a theological term for universal human self-determination

He also describes the word's status as 'in the ethical vocabulary, as meaning able to take moral decisions', not as a 'theological term', and immediately qualifies that 'the idea itself was not original with Justin' and that even his employment of the adjective 'can hardly be original'. - Description proposée : Telfer: the adjective is 'in evidence as far back as Chrysippus in the third century B.C.', with independence as its earliest sense; the 'adjectival noun' naming self-determination 'does not appear until the second Christian century'. ### `scholarly_argument_voelke_determinism_and_moral_responsi_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : determinism and moral responsibility — Stoic compatibilism: determinism (fate) is compatible with moral responsibility because assent (sunkatathesis) remains 'up to us' (eph' hēmin) even within causal necessity
- Constat : Voelke never uses the word 'compatibilism'; that label is imported. His reconciliation of freedom and fate (Conclusions, pp. 195-196) runs through the Stoic distinction of causes: when the cause 'parfaite en elle-même' is in us, the action is 'en notre pouvoir', which 'signifie pour les stoïciens qu'elle est libre'; responsibility for evil follows (p. 196). The node's mechanism ('assent remains up to us') is only half of it — for Voelke assent is free because it expresses the soul's own tension/nature acting as perfect cause, and he adds a qualification the node drops: under Chrysippus' doctrine of the kataleptic impression the freedom of assent is 'gravement compromise' (pp. 36-37). Pages from OCR running headers; .md is empty so no [file.md:line] form exists.
  - « On sait comment la distinction physique de ces deux types de causes permet de concilier l'affirmation de la liberté humaine avec celle de la fatalité universelle : lorsque la cause « parfaite en elle-méme » se trouve en nous, son action est « en notre pouvoir », ce qui signifie pour les stoiciens qu'elle est libre. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 195 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 189]
  - « Aussi pourra-t-il juger les hommes responsables du mal qu'ils commettent et pourtant les supporter avec patience » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 196 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 190]
  - « Dés lors elle nous « conduit » (2) — nécessairement, semble-t-il — à l'assentiment, dont la liberté est gravement compromise. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 36 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 31]
- Description proposée : Voelke: the Stoic distinction between the cause 'perfect in itself' (perfecta et principalis) and merely auxiliary/antecedent causes reconciles human freedom with universal fatality. When the perfect cause lies in us, the action is 'in our power', which for the Stoics means it is free; assent to an impression is free although conditioned by an external impression, because it expresses the soul's own force and nature. Men are thus responsible for the evil they do, yet to be borne with patience. Voelke notes that Chrysippus' kataleptic-impression doctrine seriously compromises the freedom of assent (pp. 36-37).
- Pages : 195-196 (with 36-37)

### `scholarly_argument_voelke_free_will_in_stoicism_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : free will in Stoicism — The Stoics developed a concept of will (volonté/boulēsis) that is distinct from modern notions of free will; it is rational assent to fate rather than autonomous choice
- Constat : Two misreadings. (a) The node equates Voelke's 'volonté' with boulēsis. For Voelke boulēsis (rational wish, Cicero's voluntas) is only one species of tendency (pp. 57-58); his thesis is that judgement and tendency are derived forms of a 'volonté primordiale qui est tension, force, effort sur soi-même' (p. 193) and that 'la raison est volonté' (p. 7). (b) 'rational assent to fate rather than autonomous choice' opposes what Voelke unites: consent to Destiny is the 'acte volontaire par excellence' by which we accede to freedom (pp. 97, 99); Epictetus' prohairesis is a 'choix primordial' and Voelke ends on 'l'autonomie du sage stoïcien' (p. 201). What Voelke does say (p. 146 n.) is that Stoic inner freedom is not 'libre arbitre' understood as the power to choose indifferently between two opposites — a narrower claim than the node's.
  - « En définitive, jugement et tendance ne sont donc que des formes dérivées de volonté, tenant toute leur réalité d'une volonté primordiale qui est tension, force, effort sur soi-même. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 193 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 187]

  - « On peut néanmoins conce-voir une autonomie qui ne se réduise pas au seul acte de poser une forme universelle, mais dont l'exercice soit en méme temps réalisation d'un contenu. Telle est l'autonomie du sage stoïcien. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 201 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 195]
- Description proposée : Voelke: in Stoic rationalism 'reason is will'. Boulēsis (rational wish; Cicero's voluntas) is only one species of tendency; judgement (assent) and tendency are both derived forms of a primordial will that is tension (tonos), force, effort upon oneself. Stoic inner freedom is not 'libre arbitre' understood as the power to choose indifferently between two opposites, and the will is never freed from the cosmic order; but consent to Destiny is the voluntary act par excellence, and the sage realises an autonomy whose exercise is also the realisation of his own nature.

### `scholarly_argument_voelke_prohairesis_and_the_will_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : prohairesis and the will — The Stoic prohairesis (faculty of choice/assent) constitutes the core of the self and the locus of moral freedom, distinct from Aristotelian prohairesis
- Constat : Voelke's claims are about Epictetus, not 'the Stoic prohairesis': the old Stoics 'ne paraissent pas avoir fait un ample usage de cette notion' (p. 144) and its 'mise en relief' is Epictetus' most notable novelty (p. 198). In Epictetus the prohairesis is 'parfois considérée comme le moi lui-même' (p. 153) and 'le principe de la seule liberté dont nous disposions, la liberté intérieure' (p. 198) — which Voelke explicitly says is not a libre arbitre (p. 146 n.). The Aristotle contrast is present (pp. 143-144: Aristotelian pro- marks preference, Stoic pro- temporal anteriority), but Voelke also notes Epictetus sometimes gives the prefix the comparative value (p. 147). Voelke's own gloss is 'pouvoir de juger, de choisir et de vouloir accompagné de conscience réflexive', not 'faculty of choice/assent'.
  - « Pouvoir suprême de l'être humain, la prohatrésis est parfois considérée comme le moi lui-méme. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 153 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 147]
  - « A vrai dire, les anciens stoiciens ne paraissent pas avoir fait un ample usage de cette notion, et, dans les rares fragments où elle apparaît, elle désigne l'intention, la volonté, sans nuance particuliére. » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 144 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 138]
  - « la mise en relief de la prohairésis, pouvoir de juger, de choisir et de vouloir accompagné de conscience réflexive, intention fondant tous nos actes. Cette fonction complexe est le principe de la seule liberté dont nous disposions, la liberté intérieure » [OCR-degraded: Voelke 1973, p. 198 — 10_Scripts/ocr_work/…Voelke…results.json, PDF index 192]
- Description proposée : Voelke: in Epictetus (the old Stoics scarcely used the notion) the prohairesis is the primordial choice rooting our moral project — a power of judging, choosing and willing accompanied by reflexive consciousness, immanent to desire, tendency and assent rather than transcending them (against Marcus Aurelius). It is sometimes identified with the self ('le moi lui-même', 'personne morale') and is the principle of the only freedom we have, inner freedom, which is not a libre arbitre. Unlike Aristotle's prohairesis, where pro- marks preference, the Stoic definition gives the prefix a temporal value ('choix avant le choix').
- Pages : 142-154, 198

### `scholarly_argument_voelke_will_of_nature` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chapter VII, 'La volonté de la Nature'. Diverse Stoic texts present nature as an agent exercising voluntary activity: identified with the omnipresent logos, Destiny and Providence, nature is a self-moving generative fire proceeding methodically (Galen, Defin. med. 95 = SVF II, 1133), and in the exposé following Zeno's definition the nature of the world possesses all the movements, tendencies and inclinations of will — hormai — with corresponding actions (Cicero, Nat. Deor. II 22, 58 = SVF I, 172). Unlike Aristotle's nature, which 'wants' but cannot move itself and depends on a transcendent fir
- Constat : Chapter VII verified: nature as agent exercising voluntary activity, identified with logos/Destiny/Providence, Galen Defin. med. 95 = SVF II 1133 and Cicero Nat. Deor. II 22, 58 = SVF I 172 (p. 106), Aristotle contrast (pp. 106-107), imperial period clearest (p. 107), Epictetus' will of nature, Seneca's Nat. Quaest. decrees and moral edification (pp. 107-108), Marcus' two natures (pp. 109-110); Conclusions p. 195 'pas par un abus de langage'. Minor omission only: Voelke also notes Seneca's occasional idea of a cruel design of nature (p. 109).

When the perfect cause is in us, our action is 'in our power', which for the Stoics means it is free: the assent given to a representation is free, even though conditioned by an impression of external origin, because in it the soul's own force and nature express themselves (suapte vi et natura, De Fato 19, 43) — not a s
- Constat : Conclusions pp. 195-197 verified: cause 'parfaite en elle-même' (De Fato 18, 41 in n. 2), auxiliary/antecedent causes, 'en notre pouvoir' = free, assent free though conditioned by external impression, 'sa force et sa nature propres' (De Fato 19, 43 in n. 4), reason as perfect cause (Origen De Princ. ### `scholarly_argument_voelke_seneca_augustine_divided_will` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Seneca's reflections on the will do not form a systematically elaborated doctrine, but he tends to make will the determining element of morality: militant, it is the artisan of all progress; triumphant, the living realisation of the good; perverted, the essence of evil. In his stress on the will Seneca is often close to Augustine: both feel profoundly the inner tearing of the soul and evoke it in almost identical terms — the divided soul does not will 'totally' and floats between diverse volitions (Seneca, Ep. 20, 6 and 35, 4; Augustine, Conf. VIII 9, 21 and 10, 22-23). But for Seneca the will
- Constat : Conclusions pp. 198-200 verified: no systematically elaborated doctrine; will as determining element of morality (militante / triomphante / pervertie); closeness to Augustine; divided soul does not will 'totalement' (Conf. VIII 9, 21; Sen. Ep. 20, 6) and 'flotte' (Conf. VIII 10, 23; Ep. 35, 4); Seneca's unity by progress vs Augustine's return to God and Adam's sin (Conf. VIII 10, 22); Augustine on usage and good/bad will (Civ. Dei XIV 8, 3 and XIV 6); nature's force to straighten the will (Ep. 116, 8) vs God alone (Civ. Dei XIV 11, 1). Seneca chapter = pp. 161-190 (chapter opens at p. 161; divided will pp. 172-173).
